1.
The
C
-global stable splitting of
{\mathbf{U}}

A key player in this paper is the
C
-global ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{U}}

made from unitary groups, see
[
24
, Example 2.37]
or Construction
B.17
.
Our definition of the global Segal–Becker splitting
depends on a
C
-global stable splitting of
{\mathbf{U}}
:
In
[
25
]
, I construct certain morphisms

s_{k}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
in the
C
-global stable
homotopy category such that the combined morphism

(1.1)

\sum s_{k}\ :\ \bigvee_{k\geq 0}\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}

is a
C
-global equivalence, see
[
25
, Theorem 4.10]
.
The splitting (
1.1
)
is a global-equivariant refinement of Miller’s stable splitting

[
21
]
of the infinite unitary group.
In this section we review the splitting,
and then translate it into an interpretation
of the group
\llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}
of stable
C
-global morphisms
in terms of
\ad(k)
-graded
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant
homotopy groups of
X
, see Theorem
1.16
.

The
extended unitary group

\tilde{U}(k)\ =\ U(k)\rtimes C

is the semi-direct product of the group
C=\Gal({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}})
acting on the unitary group

U(k)\ =\ \{A\in M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\colon A\cdot\bar{A}^{t}=E_{k}\}

by coordinatewise complex conjugation.
The orthogonal
C
-space
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
is made from Grassmannians
of complex
k
-planes, with involution by complex conjugation; see Construction
B.1
.
And
(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}
denotes the global Thom space over
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
associated with the
adjoint representation
\ad(k)
of the extended unitary group
\tilde{U}(k)
;
see
[
25
, Example 3.12]
.

The
C
-global stable splitting morphism
s_{k}\colon(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}

ultimately stems from a specific
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant stable splitting of
the ‘top cell’ in
U(k)^{\ad}
.
We review this splitting now, following Crabb’s exposition in
[
9
, page 39]
.
In
[
24
]
and
[
25
]
we use different conventions
on whether the suspension coordinate in an orthogonal suspension spectrum is
written on the left or right of the argument.
In this paper, we adopt the convention of
[
24
]
,
with the suspension coordinate written on the left;
this entails some minor changes to formulas in
[
25
]
,
moving some suspension coordinates to the other side.

Construction 1.2

(Splitting the top cell off
U(k)^{\ad}
)
.

We write

\ad(k)\ =\ \{X\in M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\colon X=-\bar{X}^{t}\}

for the
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space of skew-hermitian complex
k\times k
matrices.
The unitary group
U(k)
acts on
\ad(k)
by conjugation,
and the group
C
acts by coordinatewise complex conjugation.
Together with the euclidean inner product
\langle X,Y\rangle=\trc(\bar{X}^{t}\cdot Y)=-\trc(X\cdot Y)
,
these data make
\ad(k)
into an orthogonal
\tilde{U}(k)
-representation;
this action witnesses
\ad(k)
as the adjoint representation of
\tilde{U}(k)
,
whence the name.

The
Cayley transform
is the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant open embedding

\ad(k)\ \longrightarrow\ U(k)^{\ad}\ ,\quad X\ \longmapsto\ (X-1)(X+1)^{-1}

onto the subspace of
U(k)
of those matrices that do not have +1 as an eigenvalue.
The associated collapse map

U(k)^{\ad}\ \longrightarrow\ S^{\ad(k)}

admits a section in the stable homotopy category of genuine
\tilde{U}(k)
-spectra, as follows.
We write

\sa(k)\ =\ \{Z\in M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\colon Z=\bar{Z}^{t}\}

for the
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space of hermitian complex
k\times k
matrices.
Much like for
\ad(k)
, the unitary group
U(k)
acts on
\sa(k)
by conjugation,
the group
C
acts by coordinatewise complex conjugation,
and an invariant inner product is given by

\langle Z,Z^{\prime}\rangle=\trc(\bar{Z}^{t}\cdot Z^{\prime})=\trc(Z\cdot Z^{\prime})
;
these data make
\sa(k)
into another orthogonal
\tilde{U}(k)
-representation.
A basic linear algebra fact, sometimes referred to as ‘polar decomposition’,
is that the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant map

(1.3)

\phi_{k}\ :\ \sa(k)\times U(k)^{\ad}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})=\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)\ ,\quad(Z,A)\mapsto A\cdot\exp(-Z)

is an open embedding onto the general linear group
GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
;
for a proof, see for example
[
25
, Proposition B.17]
.
This open embedding has an associated
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant collapse map

(1.4)

t_{k}\ :\ S^{\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}\ \longrightarrow\ S^{\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)^{\ad}_{+}

that is a stable section to the previous collapse map
U(k)^{\ad}\longrightarrow S^{\ad(k)}
,
see the argument after the proof of Theorem 1.8 in
[
9
, page 39]
,
or the proof of
[
25
, Theorem 4.7]
.

Next we recall how the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant stable splitting (
1.4
)
gives rise to a specific equivariant homotopy class
\tau_{k}
in

\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
; this class in turn characterizes the

C
-global splitting morphism

s_{k}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}\allowbreak\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}

by the relation (
1.9
).

Throughout this paper, an
augmented Lie group

is a continuous homomorphism
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C

from a compact Lie group
G
to
C=\Gal({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}})
.
A recurring example is the extended unitary group,
augented by the projection
\tilde{U}(k)=U(k)\rtimes C\longrightarrow C
.
For an orthogonal
C
-spectrum
X
we will often abbreviate
the equivariant homotopy groups
\pi_{*}^{G}(\alpha^{*}(X))
to
\pi_{*}^{G}(X)

when the augmentation
\alpha
is clear from the context.

Construction 1.5

(The
C
-global splitting morphism)
.

We write
\nu_{k}
for the
tautological Real representation

of the extended unitary group
\tilde{U}(k)
.
By definition, this is the vector space
{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
with tautological
U(k)
-action, and with

C
acting by coordinatewise complex conjugation.
If
W
is a hermitian inner product space,
we write
uW
for the underlying
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space, endowed with
the euclidean inner product
\langle v,w\rangle=\text{Re}(v,w)
,
the real part of the hermitian inner product.
The map

(1.6)

\zeta^{k}\ :\ \nu_{k}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}u(\nu_{k})=u(\nu_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}\ ,\quad w\longmapsto(1\otimes w-i\otimes iw)/\sqrt{2}

is a
U(k)
-equivariant
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear isometric embedding.
The map
\zeta^{k}
commutes with the complex conjugations,
which acts diagonally on the target, through complex conjugation on
{\mathbb{C}}
and on
\nu_{k}
.
So conjugation by
\zeta^{k}
and extension by the identity
on the orthogonal complement of its image
is a continuous
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant group monomorphism

(1.7)

\zeta^{k}_{*}\ :\ U(k)^{\ad}=U(\nu_{k})\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ .

Here
U(k)
acts on
{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))
via its action on
u(\nu_{k})

and the functorially of
{\mathbf{U}}
; and complex conjugation acts diagonally,
through its action on
{\mathbf{U}}
and on
\nu_{k}
.

The
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant collapse map
t_{k}
was defined in (
1.4
).
In
[
25
, Construction 4.4]
, we define a class

(1.8)

\tau_{k}\ \in\ \pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})

(there denoted
\langle t_{k,0}\rangle
)
as the one represented by the
\tilde{U}(k)
-map

\displaystyle S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}\ \xrightarrow{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{k}}\

\displaystyle S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)^{\ad}_{+}\xrightarrow{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge(\zeta^{k}_{*})_{+}}\ S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))_{+}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})_{+}}\

\displaystyle S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))_{+}=(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))\ .

Here
i_{1}\colon u(\nu_{k})\longrightarrow u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k)
is the embedding of the first summand.
In
[
25
]
, we consider
\tau_{k}
as an equivariant
stable homotopy class of the
k
-th stage of the eigenspace filtration of
{\mathbf{U}}
,
but now we work in the ambient orthogonal space
{\mathbf{U}}
.

The
tautological class

e_{\tilde{U}(k),\ad(k)}\ \in\ \pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)})

is defined in
[
25
, (A.16)]
.
By
[
25
, Theorem A.17 (i)]
, the pair

(\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)},e_{\tilde{U}(k),\ad(k)})
represents the functor

\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}\colon{\mathcal{GH}}_{C}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{A}}b
on the
C
-global stable homotopy category.
The
C
-global splitting morphism

s_{k}\colon\allowbreak\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}

is defined in
[
25
, (4.6)]

by the property that it takes the tautological class
to
\tau_{k}
, i.e., by the relation

(1.9)

(s_{k})_{*}(e_{\tilde{U}(k),\ad(k)})\ =\ \tau_{k}\ .

In this paper, we shall mostly work with the reduced suspension spectrum

\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}
(as opposed to the unreduced one),
and with the ‘reduced’ version of the class
\tau_{k}
.
We endow
{\mathbf{U}}
with the intrinsic basepoint
consisting of the multiplicative units.
We write
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}
for
{\mathbf{U}}
with an additional basepoint added.
This comes with based maps
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow S^{0}
and
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
;
the first of these takes
{\mathbf{U}}
to the non-basepoint of
S^{0}
,
and the second is the identity on
{\mathbf{U}}
and maps the extra basepoint to the
intrinsic basepoint.
We write

\varrho\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma^{\infty}S^{0}={\mathbb{S}}\text{\qquad and\qquad}q\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}

for the morphisms induced on reduced suspension
C
-spectra.
The combined morphism

(1.10)

(\varrho,q)\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ {\mathbb{S}}\times(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

is then a
C
-global equivalence.

The next proposition shows that for all
k\geq 1
, the class
\tau_{k}

defined in (
1.8
)
lies in the augmentation ideal, i.e., the kernel of
the homomorphism
\varrho_{*}\colon\pi_{\star}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\longrightarrow\pi_{\star}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbb{S}})
.

Proposition 1.11
.

For every
k\geq 1
, the relation
\varrho_{*}(\tau_{k})=0

holds in
\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbb{S}})
.

Proof.

Every matrix in
\sa(k)
is unitarily diagonalizable with real eigenvalues.
We write
\sa^{\geq 0}(k)
for the closed
\tilde{U}(k)
-invariant subspace
of
\sa(k)
consisting of those hermitian matrices all of whose eigenvalues
are greater or equal to
0
.
The continuous
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant map

(1.12)

\sa^{\geq 0}(k)\times[0,\infty)\ \longrightarrow\ \sa^{\geq 0}(k)\ ,\quad(Z,t)\ \mapsto\ Z\ +\ t\cdot E_{k}

is proper. Indeed, if
Z\in\sa^{\geq 0}(k)
has eigenvalues
x_{1},\dots,x_{k}
,
and
t\geq 0
, then
Z+tE_{k}
is again hermitian,
has eigenvalues
x_{1}+t,\dots,x_{k}+t
, and thus also lies in
\sa^{\geq 0}(k)
.
So

|\!|(Z,t)|\!|\ =\ \sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+\dots+x_{k}^{2}+t^{2}}\ \leq\ \sqrt{(x_{1}+t)^{2}+\dots+(x_{k}+t)^{2}}=\ |\!|Z+tE_{k}|\!|\ .

As a proper map, (
1.12
) extends continuously
to the one-point compactifications

(\sa^{\geq 0}(k)\cup\{\infty\})\wedge[0,\infty]\ \longrightarrow\ \sa^{\geq 0}(k)\cup\{\infty\}\ ;

this map is a
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant contracting homotopy
of the space
\sa^{\geq 0}(k)\cup\{\infty\}
.

The singular value decomposition of complex matrices shows that every

X\in M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
is of the form
X=A\cdot Z
for some
A\in U(k)

and some
Z\in\sa^{\geq 0}(k)
. The matrix
Z
in this decomposition is unique,
namely the only matrix in
\sa^{\geq 0}(k)
such that
Z^{2}=\bar{X}^{t}\cdot X
.
So the composite

(1.13)

\sa^{\geq 0}(k)\ \xrightarrow{\text{incl}}\ M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ U(k)\backslash M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})

is a continuous bijection, where the right hand side denotes the orbit space
by the
U(k)
-action by left multiplication.
The map

M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ \sa^{\geq 0}(k)\ ,\quad X\ \longmapsto\sqrt{\bar{X}^{t}\cdot X}

is continuous and invariant under left multiplication by unitary matrices.
So it descends to a continuous map on
U(k)\backslash M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
,
showing that the composite (
1.13
) is in fact a homeomorphism.

The conjugation action of
\tilde{U}(k)
on
M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
passes to a
well-defined action on the orbit space
U(k)\backslash M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})

and the map (
1.13
) is
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant for this induced action
on the target, and the conjugation action on the source.
As an equivariant homeomorphism, the map (
1.13
) extends
to a
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant homeomorphism between the one-point
compactifications.
We showed above that
\sa^{\geq 0}(k)\cup\{\infty\}
is equivariantly contractible;
the upshot is that the one-point compactification

(U(k)\backslash M_{k}({\mathbb{C}}))\cup\{\infty\}\ =\ U(k)\backslash S^{M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})}

is also
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariantly contractible.

By inspection of definitions, the composite

(1.14)

S^{M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})}\ \xrightarrow{\ t_{k}\ }\ S^{\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)_{+}\ \xrightarrow{S^{\sa(k)}\wedge\varrho}\ S^{\sa(k)}

is given by

X\ \longmapsto\ \begin{cases}-\ln(\sqrt{\bar{X}^{t}\cdot X})&\text{ for $X\in GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})$, and}\\
\quad\infty&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

In particular, this composite is invariant under left translation by elements of
U(k)
,
so it factors through the orbit space
U(k)\backslash S^{M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})}
.
Since the latter is equivariantly contractible, the composite (
1.14
)
is
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariantly null-homotopic.
Since the defining representative of the class
\varrho_{*}(\tau_{k})

factors through a suspension of the composite (
1.14
),
this proves that
\varrho_{*}(\tau_{k})=0
.
∎

We set

(1.15)

\omega_{k}\ =\ q_{*}(\tau_{k})\ \in\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\ .

For
k\geq 1
, the class
\tau_{k}
lies in the augmentation ideal
by Proposition
1.11
,
so its projection
\omega_{k}
to the reduced suspension spectrum does not lose any information.

The following representability result is a fairly direct consequence of the
stable splitting (
1.1
).
We shall use it to construct
C
-global stable morphisms with source
\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}
,
and to check commutativity of diagrams whose initial object is
\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}
.
We let
\llbracket-,-\rrbracket^{C}
denote the group of morphisms in the
C
-global stable
homotopy category.

Theorem 1.16
.

For every
C
-global spectrum
X
, the evaluation map

\llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ {\prod}_{k\geq 1}\,\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(X)\ ,\quad f\ \longmapsto\ (f_{*}(\omega_{k}))_{k\geq 1}

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

The splitting (
1.1
) proved in
[
25
, Theorem 4.10]

and the representability property of
(\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})^{\ad(k)},\allowbreak e_{\tilde{U}(k),\ad(k)})

together provide the natural isomorphism

(1.17)

\llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ {\prod}_{k\geq 0}\,\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(X)\ ,\quad f\longmapsto(f_{*}(\tau_{k}))_{k\geq 0}\ .

The
C
-global equivalence (
1.10
)
induces another isomorphism

\llbracket{\mathbb{S}},X\rrbracket^{C}\times\llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ \llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ ,\quad(a,b)\ \longmapsto\ a\circ\varrho+b\circ q\ .

The morphism
\varrho\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{S}}
sends the class
\tau_{0}

to
1\in\pi_{0}({\mathbb{S}})
, so the composite

\llbracket{\mathbb{S}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ \xrightarrow{\ \varrho^{*}\ }\ \llbracket\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}},X\rrbracket^{C}\ \xrightarrow{f\mapsto f_{*}(\tau_{0})}\ \pi_{0}^{C}(X)

is an isomorphism. Moreover,
q_{*}(\tau_{0})=0
and
q_{*}(\tau_{k})=\omega_{k}
for
k\geq 1
,
so the isomorphism (
1.17
) restricts to an isomorphism
as in the statement of the theorem, where now the factor indexed by
k=0
is omitted.
∎

We shall also need a certain relation between the
class
\tau_{k}
and the
k
-fold exterior product of copies of
\tau_{1}
,
see Theorem
1.21
.
The ultra-commutative multiplication on
{\mathbf{U}}
induces a

C
-global ring spectrum structure on the unreduced suspension spectrum
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
.
This, in turn, induces products on the equivariant homotopy groups of
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
.
We set

\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1}\ =\ p_{1}^{*}(\tau_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{k}^{*}(\tau_{1})\ \in\ \pi_{k\sigma}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ ,

where
p_{i}\colon\tilde{T}^{k}\longrightarrow\tilde{T}
is the projection to the
i
-th factor.
We have also identified the sign representation
with
\ad(1)
by sending
x\in\sigma
to
i\cdot x\in\ad(1)=i\cdot{\mathbb{R}}
.

Construction 1.18
.

We define the diagonal embedding

\Delta\ :\ {\mathbb{C}}^{k}\ \longrightarrow\ M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\text{\qquad by\qquad}\Delta(z_{1},\dots,z_{k})\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}z_{1}&0&0&\dots&0\\
0&z_{2}&0&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&0&z_{k-1}&0\\
0&\dots&0&0&z_{k}\end{pmatrix}\ .

Whenever convenient we use
\Delta
to identify

T^{k}
with its image in
U(k)
, the diagonal maximal torus.
The diagonal embedding extends to an embedding of the semi-direct products
by complex conjugation,

\tilde{T}^{k}\ =\ T^{k}\rtimes C\ \longrightarrow\ U(k)\rtimes C\ =\ \tilde{U}(k)\ .

We let
D\subset M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the
\tilde{T}^{k}
-invariant

{\mathbb{C}}
-subspace of lower subdiagonal matrices, i.e., of the form:

(1.19)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0&\dots&0\\
z_{2,1}&0&0&\dots&0\\
z_{3,1}&z_{3,2}&0&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
z_{k,1}&z_{k,2}&\dots&z_{k,k-1}&0\end{pmatrix}

The maps

(1.20)

\displaystyle k\sigma\oplus D\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \ad(k)\ ,

\displaystyle\quad(x_{1},\dots,x_{k};A)\

\displaystyle\longmapsto\

\displaystyle\Delta(ix_{1},\dots,ix_{k})\

\displaystyle+\ A-\bar{A}^{t}

\displaystyle{\mathbb{R}}^{k}\oplus D\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ \sa(k)\ ,

\displaystyle\quad(y_{1},\dots,y_{k};A)\

\displaystyle\longmapsto\

\displaystyle\Delta(y_{1},\dots,y_{k})\ \

\displaystyle+\ A+\bar{A}^{t}

are
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear and
\tilde{T}^{k}
-equivariant isomorphisms.
The first one identifies
D
with the subspace of off-diagonal matrices

\ad(k)-\ad(k)^{T^{k}}
in
\ad(k)
, as orthogonal
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representations.
And the second one identifies
D
with the subspace of off-diagonal matrices

\sa(k)-\sa(k)^{T^{k}}
in
\sa(k)
.

The class
\tau_{k}
in
\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})

was defined in (
1.8
).
And
a_{D}\in\pi_{-D}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbb{S}})
denote the pre-Euler class (
0.1
)
of the
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
D
.

Theorem 1.21
.

The relation

a_{D}^{2}\cdot\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\tau_{k})\ =\ a_{D}\cdot(\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1})

holds in the group
\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{k\sigma-D}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
.

Proof.

The diagonal map
\Delta
identifies
\ad(1)^{k}
with
\ad(k)^{T^{k}}
,
and it identifies
\sa(1)^{k}
with
\sa(k)^{T^{k}}
.
We denote all these restrictions by
\Delta
, too.
The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{(\sa(1)\times U(1))^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\phi_{1})^{k}}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{(\sa(1)\oplus\ad(1))^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
shuffle
\textstyle{\sa(1)^{k}\times U(1)^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta\times\Delta}
\textstyle{\sa(1)^{k}\oplus\ad(1)^{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta\times\Delta}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\times U(k)^{\ad}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{k}}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}

So the diagram of collapse maps associated with the horizontal open embeddings commutes, too.
Hence also the left part of the following diagram of based continuous
\tilde{T}^{k}
-maps
commutes:

\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge(S^{\sa(1)\oplus\ad(1)})^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{1}^{\wedge k}}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge(S^{\sa(1)}\wedge U(1)_{+})^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
shuffle
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge(S^{\sa(1)}\wedge(\zeta^{1}_{*})_{+})^{\wedge k}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge(S^{\sa(1)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))_{+})^{\wedge k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
shuffle
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(1)^{k}\oplus\ad(1)^{k}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\Delta\wedge\Delta}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge S^{\sa(1)^{k}}\wedge U(1)^{k}_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge S^{\sa(1)^{k}}\wedge(\zeta^{1}_{*})^{k}_{+}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\Delta\wedge\Delta_{+}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge S^{\sa(1)^{k}}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))^{k}_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\Delta\wedge\mu^{(k)}_{+}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{k}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)^{\ad}_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge({\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})\circ\zeta^{k}_{*})_{+}}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge(\zeta^{k}_{*})_{+}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})_{+}}
\textstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))_{+}}

The iterated multiplication morphism

\mu^{(k)}\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))\times\dots\times{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1})\oplus\dots\oplus u(\nu_{1}))={\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))

of
{\mathbf{U}}
is given by orthogonal direct sum.
So it participates in a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{U(1)\times\dots\times U(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Delta}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{1}_{*}\times\dots\times\zeta^{1}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))\times\dots\times{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu^{(k)}}
\textstyle{U(k)^{\ad}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\zeta^{k}_{*}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))}

This implies the commutativity of the middle right part of the above diagram.

Now we can wrap up.
Under the isomorphisms (
1.20
), the maps

\Delta\colon S^{\sa(1)^{k}}\longrightarrow S^{\sa(k)}
and
\Delta\colon S^{\ad(1)^{k}}\longrightarrow S^{\ad(k)}

both represents the pre-Euler class
a_{D}
.
The clockwise composite in the above commutative diagram
thus represents the class
a_{D}\cdot(\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1})
.
And the counter clockwise composite represents the class

a_{D}^{2}\cdot\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\tau_{k})
,
so the diagram witnesses the desired relation.
∎

2.
Relations among equivariant stable homotopy classes in
{\mathbf{U}}

The purpose of this section is to establishing some crucial relations between
certain families of
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant homotopy classes of
the
C
-global spectrum
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
, namely the classes

\tau_{k}
defined in (
1.8
),
their reduced cousins
\omega_{k}
from (
1.15
),
and certain classes
v_{k}
and
u_{k}
that we introduce in (
2.2
)
and (
2.11
) below.
The main result here is the formula of Theorem
2.16
,
which will eventually be used to identify the effect
of our global Segal–Becker splitting on equivariant homotopy groups
and on equivariant cohomology theories.

Construction 2.1
.

For
k\geq 0
, the diagonal matrix
-E_{k}=\Delta(-1,\dots,-1)

is a
\tilde{U}(k)
-fixed point of
U(k)^{\ad}
.
Thus

\zeta^{k}_{*}(-E_{k})\ \in\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))

is again a
\tilde{U}(k)
-fixed point, where

\zeta^{k}_{*}\colon U(k)^{\ad}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))
was defined in (
1.7
).
We write

(2.2)

v_{k}\ \in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})

for its stabilization, i.e., the class represented by the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant map

S^{\nu_{k}}\ \xrightarrow{-\wedge\zeta^{k}_{*}(-E_{k})}\ S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))_{+}\ =\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{k}))\ .

For
0\leq j\leq k
, we write
U(k-j,j)
for the block subgroup of
U(k)
,
consisting of the matrices of the form

\left(\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\
0&B\end{smallmatrix}\right)

for
(A,B)\in U(k-j)\times U(j)
.
We write
\tilde{U}(k-j,j)=U(k-j,j)\rtimes C
for the
semi-direct product with
C
acting by coordinatewise complex conjugation.
The class
\tau_{j}
was defined in (
1.8
),
and
a_{\ad(j)}\in\pi_{-\ad(j)}^{\tilde{U}(j)}({\mathbb{S}})
is the pre-Euler class
(
0.1
) of the adjoint representation.

Theorem 2.3
.

For every
k\geq 1
, the relation

v_{k}\ =\ \sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(1\times(a_{\ad(j)}\cdot\tau_{j}))

holds in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
.

Proof.

The map
\phi_{k}
defined in (
1.3
)
is an open embedding with image
GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Every matrix
Y
in
\sa(k)\cap GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
is unitarily diagonalizable with
non-zero real eigenvalues.
If
i
is the number of positive eigenvalues of
Y
, counted with multiplicity,
it is thus of the form

(2.4)

Y\ =\ B\cdot\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\exp(-Z)&0\\
0&-\exp(-Z^{\prime})\end{smallmatrix}\right)\cdot B^{-1}

for some
B\in U(k)
and
(Z,Z^{\prime})\in\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)
.
Moreover, this representation is unique up to changing
(B,Z,Z^{\prime})

to
(B\cdot(A\oplus A^{\prime})^{-1},{{}^{A}Z},{{}^{A^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}})

for some
(A,A^{\prime})\in U(i)\times U(k-i)
.
This shows that the map

g_{i}\ :\ \tilde{U}(k)\times_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}(\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i))\ \longrightarrow\ \sa(k)\cap GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})

sending the equivalence class
[B;Z,Z^{\prime}]
to (
2.4
)
is a homeomorphism onto the open and closed subspace
of matrices with exactly
i
positive eigenvalues.
Altogether, this shows that the
following commutative square of
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant maps is a pullback:

\textstyle{\coprod_{0\leq i\leq k}\,\tilde{U}(k)\times_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\coprod g_{i}}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(-,0)}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\times U(k)^{\ad}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{GL_{k}({\mathbb{C}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}

The left vertical map is given on the
i
-th summand by
the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant extension of the map

\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)\ \longrightarrow\ \sa(k)\times U(k)^{\ad}\ ,\quad(Z,Z^{\prime})\longmapsto\ \left(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}Z&0\\
0&Z^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right),\left(\begin{smallmatrix}E_{i}&0\\
0&-E_{k-i}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\right)\ .

The pullback yields a commutative diagram for the collapse maps associated
with the horizontal open embeddings; after smashing with
S^{\nu_{k}}
,
this becomes the commutative left part of the following diagram:

\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\wedge 0}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\bigvee g_{i}^{\natural}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\left(\bigvee_{0\leq i\leq k}\tilde{U}(k)\ltimes_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}S^{\sa(i)\oplus\sa(k-i)}\right)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{k}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)^{\ad}_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge({\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})\circ\zeta^{k}_{*})_{+}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))_{+}}

The counter clockwise composite represents the class
a_{\ad(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}
.
So the diagram witnesses the relation

a_{\ad(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}\ =\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq k}f_{i}\ ,

where
f_{i}
is represented by the suspension by
S^{\nu_{k}}
of the composite

\displaystyle S^{\sa(k)}\

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\ g_{i}^{\natural}\ }\ \tilde{U}(k)\ltimes_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}S^{\sa(i)\oplus\sa(k-i)}\ \xrightarrow{\ \xi^{\flat}_{i}\ }\ S^{\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))_{+}\ .

The second map
\xi^{\flat}_{i}
is the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant extension of

\displaystyle\xi_{i}\ :\ S^{\sa(i)\oplus\sa(k-i)}\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\qquad S^{\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))_{+}

\displaystyle(Z,Z^{\prime})\quad

\displaystyle\longmapsto\ \left(\begin{smallmatrix}Z&0\\
0&Z^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\wedge({\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})\circ\zeta^{k}_{*})\left(\begin{smallmatrix}E_{i}&0\\
0&-E_{k-i}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ .

The collapse map
g_{i}^{\natural}
associated with
g_{i}

is closely related to, but different from, the collapse map
that features in the definition of the transfer
\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}
.
The latter collapse map, discussed for example in
[
24
, (3.2.10)]
,
is based on a different open embedding, namely the
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant extension
of the map

\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)\ \longrightarrow\ \sa(k)\ ,\quad(Z,Z^{\prime})\ \longmapsto\ \left(\begin{smallmatrix}E_{i}+Z&0\\
0&-E_{k-i}+Z^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)

restricted to a small open disc around
(0,0)
.
The differential of this second embedding at
(0,0)
is the block embedding

\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)\longrightarrow\sa(k)
, while
the differential of the embedding
g_{i}
at
(0,0)
is the map

\sa(i)\times\sa(k-i)\ \longrightarrow\ \sa(k)\ ,\quad(Z,Z)\ \longmapsto\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-Z&0\\
0&Z^{\prime}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\ .

The differentials of these two embeddings differ by the sign in the first block.
We write
\epsilon_{i}\in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(i)}({\mathbb{S}})
for the unit represented by
the map
S^{\sa(i)}\longrightarrow S^{\sa(i)}
,
Z\mapsto-Z
.
Then the composite
f_{i}
differs from the transfer of
1\times v_{k-i}

by multiplication by the equivariant homotopy class
\epsilon_{i}\times 1

in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}({\mathbb{S}})
.
In other words:

f_{i}\ =\ \tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}\left(\epsilon_{i}\times v_{k-i}\right)\ .

Altogether we have thus shown the relation

(2.5)

a_{\ad(k)}\cdot\tau_{k}\ =\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\epsilon_{i}\times v_{k-i})

in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
.

Now we are only a few algebraic manipulations away from the desired formula.
We consider the morphism of orthogonal
C
-spectra

\varrho\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{S}}

induced by the based map
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow S^{0}
that collapses
{\mathbf{U}}
to the non-basepoint.
It satisfies
\varrho_{*}(v_{k-i})=1
in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}({\mathbb{S}})
. So

\varrho_{*}\left(\epsilon_{i}\times v_{k-i}\right)\ =\ \epsilon_{i}\times\varrho_{*}(v_{k-i})\ =\ \epsilon_{i}\times 1\ .

Because
\varrho_{*}(\tau_{k})=0
by Proposition
1.11
,
applying
\varrho_{*}
to (
2.5
) yields

(2.6)

\sum_{0\leq i\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\epsilon_{i}\times 1)\ =\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}\left(\varrho_{*}\left(\epsilon_{i}\times v_{k-i}\right)\right)\ =\ \varrho_{*}(a_{\ad(k)}\cdot\tau_{k})\ =\ 0

whenever
k\geq 1
. Now we deduce

\displaystyle\sum_{0\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}

\displaystyle(1\times(a_{\ad(j)}\cdot\tau_{j}))\ =_{\eqref{eq:eq:tau_intermsof_v}}\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(1\times\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,j-i)}^{\tilde{U}(j)}(\epsilon_{i}\times v_{j-i}))

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-j,j-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\epsilon_{i}\times 1\times v_{j-i})

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{0\leq d\leq k}\ \sum_{0\leq i\leq k-d}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-d,d)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\tr_{\tilde{U}(i,k-d-i)}^{\tilde{U}(k-d)}(\epsilon_{i}\times 1)\times v_{d})\ =_{\eqref{cor:sum_eps_vanish}}\ v_{k}\ .

The third equation is the variable substitution
d=j-i
.
∎

We write

(2.7)

\mathfrak{c}\ :\ S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ U(1)\ ,\quad\mathfrak{c}(x)=(x+i)(x-i)^{-1}

for the Cayley transform; it is
C
-equivariant for the sign action on the source,
and complex conjugation on the target.
We identify the sign representation
with
\ad(1)
by sending
x\in\sigma
to
i\cdot x\in\ad(1)=i\cdot{\mathbb{R}}
.
We have
\sa(1)={\mathbb{R}}
with trivial
\tilde{T}
-action.
Then the collapse map
t_{1}\colon S^{\sa(1)\oplus\ad(1)}\longrightarrow S^{\sa(1)}\wedge U(1)_{+}

becomes a map
t_{1}\colon S^{1\oplus\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{1}\wedge U(1)_{+}
.

Proposition 2.8
.

The composite
(S^{1}\wedge q)\circ t_{1}\colon S^{1\oplus\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{1}\wedge U(1)

is
C
-equivariantly homotopic
to the suspension of the Cayley transform (
2.7
).

Proof.

The Cayley transform is a homeomorphism, so we may show that the composite

(2.9)

S^{1\oplus\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ t_{1}\ }\ S^{1}\wedge U(1)_{+}\ \xrightarrow{S^{1}\wedge q}\ S^{1}\wedge U(1)\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{S^{1}\wedge\mathfrak{c}^{-1}}\ S^{1\oplus\sigma}

is
C
-equivariantly homotopic to the identity.
This is the case if and only if the underlying non-equivariant
morphism and the restriction to
C
-fixed points are homotopic to the respective identity maps.

The restriction of (
2.9
) to
C
-fixed points is the composite

(2.10)

S^{1}\ \xrightarrow{(t_{1})^{C}}\ S^{1}\wedge\{\pm 1\}_{+}\ \xrightarrow{S^{1}\wedge q}\ S^{1}\wedge\{\pm 1\}\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{S^{1}\wedge(\mathfrak{c}^{-1})^{C}}\ S^{1}\ .

Expanding all formulas shows that this composite
collapses the contractible subset
[0,\infty]
of
S^{1}

to the basepoint and is given on
{\mathbb{R}}_{<0}
by the formula

f\ :\ {\mathbb{R}}_{<0}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{R}}\ ,\quad f(y)\ =\ -\ln(-y)\ .

So the composite (
2.10
) is indeed homotopic to the identity.

The map (
2.9
) itself
collapses the contractible subset
[0,\infty]\times\{0\}
of
S^{2}

to the basepoint and factors through a homeomorphism

S^{2}/([0,\infty]\times\{0\})\ \cong\ S^{2}\ .

So the composite (
2.9
) is a non-equivariant homotopy equivalence.
Expanding all formulas shows that the composite (
2.9
)
is given on
{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}
by

F\ :\ {\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}_{>0}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{R}}^{2}\ ,\quad F(x,y)\ =\ \left(-\ln(\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}),\ x/y+\sqrt{(x/y)^{2}+1}\right)\ .

So (
2.9
) fixes the point
(0,1)
,
and it is smooth near
(0,1)
with differential

D_{(0,1)}F=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}0&-1\\
1&\ 0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.
Since this differential has determinant
1
,
the composite (
2.9
) is indeed
non-equivariantly homotopic to the identity.
This concludes the proof of the claim that

(S^{1}\wedge q)\circ t_{1}
is
C
-equivariantly homotopic to
S^{1}\wedge\mathfrak{c}
.
∎

For
k\geq 1
, we define a class

(2.11)

u_{k}\ \in\ \pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

by stabilizing the class in
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbf{U}},1)
represented by the composite

(2.12)

\delta_{k}\ :\ S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{\ \mathfrak{c}\ }\ U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }\ U(k)^{\ad}\ \xrightarrow{\zeta^{k}_{*}}\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ .

The map
\partial\colon U(1)\longrightarrow U(k)
is the diagonal map sending
an element of
U(1)
to the constant diagonal matrix in
U(k)
, and
\zeta^{k}_{*}

was defined in (
1.7
).
And ‘stabilizing’ means that
u_{k}
is represented by the composite

S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\delta_{k}}\ S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ =\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{k}))\ .

The unstable representative
\delta_{k}
of
u_{k}
satisfies
\delta(0)=\zeta_{*}^{k}(-E_{k})
,
which is the unstable representative for the class
v_{k}
defined in (
2.2
).
So comparison of the definitions shows the relation

(2.13)

a_{\sigma}\cdot u_{k}\ =\ q_{*}(v_{k})

in the group
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
,
where
a_{\sigma}
is the pre-Euler class (
0.1
) of the sign representation.

We write
\overline{\ad}(k)
for the
reduced adjoint representation

of
\tilde{U}(k)
, i.e., the
\tilde{U}(k)
-subrepresentation
consisting of those matrices in
\ad(k)
whose trace is 0.
Similarly,

\overline{\sa}(k)\ =\ \{Z\in\sa(k)\colon\trc(Z)=0\}\ .

The classes
a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}
and
a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}

are the associated pre-Euler classes (
0.1
).
The class
\omega_{k}
was defined in (
1.15
).

Theorem 2.14
.

For every
k\geq 1
, the relation

a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}\cdot a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}\cdot\omega_{k}\ =\ a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}\cdot u_{k}

holds in the group
\pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\sigma-\overline{\sa}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
.

Proof.

The map
\partial\colon{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
,
\partial(x)=\Delta(x,\dots,x)

takes
U(1)
isomorphically onto the center of
U(k)
,
it identifies
\ad(1)
with
\ad(k)^{U(k)}
,
and it identifies
\sa(1)
with
\sa(k)^{U(k)}
.
We denote all these restrictions by
\partial
, too.
The following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\sa(1)\times U(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\partial\times\partial}
\textstyle{\sa(1)\oplus\ad(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial\times\partial}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\times U(k)^{\ad}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{k}}
\textstyle{\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}

So the diagram of collapse maps associated with the horizontal open embeddings commutes, too.
Thus the left part of the following diagram of based continuous
\tilde{U}(k)
-maps
commutes:

\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(1)\oplus\ad(1)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\partial\wedge\partial}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{1}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(1)}\wedge U(1)_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(1)}\wedge(\zeta^{k}_{*}\circ\partial\circ q)}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\partial\wedge\partial_{+}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(1)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge\partial\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)\oplus\ad(k)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}}\wedge t_{k}}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge U(k)^{\ad}_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge({\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})\circ\zeta^{k}_{*}\circ q)}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge(\zeta^{k}_{*}\circ q)}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(i_{1})}
\textstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sa(k)}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k})\oplus\sa(k))}

The map
\partial\colon S^{\sa(1)}\longrightarrow S^{\sa(k)}

represents the pre-Euler class
a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}
.
Proposition
2.8
shows that
the composite
(S^{1}\wedge q)\circ t_{1}\colon S^{1\oplus\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{1}\wedge U(1)

is
C
-equivariantly homotopic
to the suspension of the Cayley transform (
2.7
).
So the clockwise composite in the above commutative diagram
represents the class
a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}\cdot u_{k}
.
And the counter clockwise composite represents the class

a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}\cdot a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}\cdot\omega_{k}
,
so the diagram witnesses the desired relation.
∎

¨

In the next proposition
that we work in the ordinary, non-extended unitary group
U(k,l)
,
as opposed to the extended unitary group
\tilde{U}(k,l)
.
This is important because the classes

\res^{\tilde{U}(k+l)}_{\tilde{U}(k,l)}(u_{k+l})\text{\qquad and\qquad}p_{1}^{*}(u_{k})\ +\ p_{2}^{*}(u_{l})

are
different
in the group
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k,l)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
.
This feature is another manifestation of the non-additivity of certain
pieces of structure for surjectively augmented Lie groups,
such as the non-additivity in Example
4.23
or Remark
5.5
.

Proposition 2.15
.

For all
k,l\geq 1
, the relation

\res^{\tilde{U}(k+l)}_{U(k,l)}(u_{k+l})\ =\ p_{1}^{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{U(k)}(u_{k}))\ +\ p_{2}^{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(l)}_{U(l)}(u_{l}))

holds in the group
\pi_{1}^{U(k,l)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
,
where
p_{1}\colon U(k,l)\longrightarrow U(k)
and
p_{2}\colon U(k,l)\longrightarrow U(l)

are the projections to the blocks.

Proof.

The map
\delta_{k+l}
defined in (
2.12
) is the product of the
maps
\delta_{k}
and
\delta_{l}
, in the sense that it factors as the composite

S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{(\delta_{k},\delta_{l})}\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\times{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{l}))\ \xrightarrow{\mu_{\nu_{k},\nu_{l}}}\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k+l}))\ .

So the relation
[\delta_{k+l}]=p_{1}^{*}[\delta_{k}]\cdot p_{2}^{*}[\delta_{l}]

holds in the group
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k,l)}({\mathbf{U}},1)
, where the multiplication
is formed under the group structure
arising from the ultra-commutative multiplication of
{\mathbf{U}}
.
The subgroup
U(k,l)
of
\tilde{U}(k,l)
acts trivially on the sign representation,
so restriction yields the relation

\res^{\tilde{U}(k,l)}_{U(k,l)}[\delta_{k+l}]\ =\ p_{1}^{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{U(k)}[\delta_{k}])\cdot p_{2}^{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(l)}_{U(l)}[\delta_{l}])

in
\pi_{1}^{U(k,l)}({\mathbf{U}},1)
.
The Eckmann–Hilton argument shows that the group structure on

\pi_{1}^{U(k,l)}({\mathbf{U}},1)
from the multiplication of
{\mathbf{U}}

agrees with that as a fundamental group, i.e., by concatenation of loops.
The stabilization map

\sigma^{U(k,l)}\ :\ \pi_{1}^{U(k,l)}({\mathbf{U}},1)\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{1}^{U(k,l)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

is a group homomorphism for the concatenation of loops
on the source and on the target, where it is the group structure coming from stability,
i.e., the usual addition on equivariant stable homotopy groups.
This proves the claim.
∎

The following theorem is the main result of this section.
The classes
\omega_{j}
are defined in (
1.15
),
and
a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}
is the pre-Euler class (
0.1
)
of the reduced adjoint representation of
\tilde{U}(j)
.

Theorem 2.16
.

For every
k\geq 1
, the relation

u_{k}\ =\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j}))

holds in
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
,
where
p_{2}\colon\tilde{U}(k)\longrightarrow\tilde{U}(k-j,j)
is the projection to the second block.

Proof.

We argue by induction over
k
.
For
k=1
, we have
\overline{\ad}(1)=0
, so the claim reduces to
u_{1}=\omega_{1}
,
which holds by Proposition
2.8
.
For the rest of the proof we assume that
k\geq 2
.

In the next step we show that the desired relation holds after restriction
to the subgroup
U(i,k-i)
for every
1\leq i\leq k-1
.
All groups involved in this part of the argument augment trivially to
C
,
so they act trivially on the sign representation
\sigma
.
We abbreviate

\bar{u}_{k}\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{U(k)}(u_{k})\text{\qquad and\qquad}w_{k}\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{U(k)}(a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}\cdot\omega_{k})\ ,

both classes lying in
\pi_{1}^{U(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
.
For
1\leq j\leq k
, the double coset formula for
\res^{U(k)}_{U(i,k-i)}\circ\tr_{U(k-j,j)}^{U(k)}

established in
[
26
, Proposition 1.3]
yields

\displaystyle\res^{U(k)}_{U(i,k-i)}

\displaystyle(\tr_{U(k-j,j)}^{U(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{0,i-j\leq d\leq i,k-j}\tr^{U(i,k-i)}_{U(d,i-d,k-j-d,j-i+d)}(\gamma_{d}^{*}(\res^{U(k-j,j)}_{U(d,k-j-d,i-d,j-i+d)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j}))))

in the group
\pi_{1}^{U(i,k-i)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
, where
\gamma_{d}
is a certain
permutation matrix in
U(k)
.
For
i-j<d<i
, we have
\overline{\ad}(j)^{U(i-d,j-i+d)}\neq 0
,
thus
\res^{\tilde{U}(j)}_{U(i-d,j-i+d)}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)})=0
, and hence

\res^{\tilde{U}(j)}_{U(i-d,j-i+d)}(w_{j})\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(j)}_{U(i-d,j-i+d)}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)})\cdot\res^{\tilde{U}(j)}_{U(i-d,j-i+d)}(\omega_{j})\ =\ 0\ .

So we can omit all summands with
i-j<d<i
.
For
d=i-j
, the summation bounds force
j\leq i
,
and
\gamma_{i-j}
is the matrix associated with the permutation
that shuffles the sets
\{i-j+1,\dots,k-j\}
and
\{k-j+1,\dots,k\}
; so

\tr^{U(i,k-i)}_{U(i-j,j,k-i)}(\gamma_{i-j}^{*}(\res^{U(k-j,j)}_{U(i-j,k-i,j)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j}))))\ =\ p_{1}^{*}(\tr_{U(i-j,j)}^{U(i)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))\ .

For
d=i
, the summation bounds force
j\leq k-i
, and

\gamma_{i}
is the identity matrix; so

\tr^{U(i,k-i)}_{U(i,k-j-i,j)}(\gamma_{i}^{*}(\res^{U(k-j,j)}_{U(i,k-j-i,j)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j}))))\ =\ p_{2}^{*}(\tr_{U(k-j-i,j)}^{U(k-i)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))\ .

We sum these relations over all
j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\res^{U(k)}_{U(i,k-i)}

\displaystyle(\tr_{U(k-j,j)}^{U(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq i}p_{1}^{*}(\tr_{U(i-j,j)}^{U(i)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))\ +\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k-i}p_{2}^{*}(\tr_{U(k-i-j,j)}^{U(k-i)}(p_{2}^{*}(w_{j})))

\displaystyle=\ p_{1}^{*}(\bar{u}_{i})\ +\ p_{2}^{*}(\bar{u}_{k-i})\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{U(i,k-i)}(u_{k})\ .

The second equation is the inductive hypothesis,
and the final equation is Proposition
2.15
.

In the next step we show that the desired relation holds after
restriction to the group
U(k)
.
The space
U(k)^{\ad}
is
U(k)
-equivariantly connected, in the sense that for every subgroup

G
of
U(k)
, the fixed point space
U(k)^{G}
, i.e., the centralizer of
G
in
U(k)
,
is path-connected. So by
[
24
, Theorem 3.3.15 (i)]
,
elements in
\pi_{1}^{U(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

are detected by geometric fixed points for all closed subgroups of
U(k)
.
In other words: we may show the desired relation after applying
\Phi^{G}

for all
G\leq U(k)
. If the subgroup
G
is subconjugate to
U(i,k-i)

for some
1\leq i\leq k-1
, then the relation holds after restriction
to
G
by the first step, and hence also after taking
G
-geometric fixed points.
If the subgroup
G
is not subconjugate to
U(i,k-i)

for any
1\leq i\leq k-1
, then the
G
-action on
\nu_{k}
is irreducible,
so the centralizer of
G
in
U(k)
equals the center of
U(k)
.
Then
\overline{\sa}(k)^{G}=\overline{\ad}(k)^{G}=0
, and hence
\Phi^{G}(a_{\overline{\sa}(k)})=\Phi^{G}(a_{\overline{\ad}(k)})=1
.
Since
G
is not subconjugate to
U(k-j,j)
for
1\leq j\leq k-1
, we have

\Phi^{G}\circ\tr_{U(k-j,j)}^{U(k)}=0
, see
[
24
, Theorem 3.4.2 (ii)]
. Thus

\displaystyle\Phi^{G}(u_{k})\ =\ \Phi^{G}(a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}\cdot u_{k})\

\displaystyle=\ \Phi^{G}(a_{\overline{\sa}(k)}\cdot a_{\overline{\ad}(k)}\cdot\omega_{k})

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\Phi^{G}(\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j})))\ .

The second equation is Theorem
2.14
.
This concludes the proof that the relation holds after restriction to
U(k)
.

Now we complete the argument.
The decomposition
\ad(k)\cong\overline{\ad}(k)\oplus\sigma
as
orthogonal
\tilde{U}(k)
-representations induces an isomorphism of based orthogonal
C
-spaces

(\mathbf{Gr}_{m})^{\ad(m)}\ \cong\ (\mathbf{Gr}_{m})^{\overline{\ad}(m)\oplus\sigma}\ \cong\ (\mathbf{Gr}_{m})^{\overline{\ad}(m)}\wedge S^{\sigma}\ .

So the global stable splitting (
1.1
) provides an isomorphism

\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\ \cong\ \bigoplus_{m\geq 1}\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{m})^{\ad(m)})\ \cong\ \bigoplus_{m\geq 1}\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}(\mathbf{Gr}_{m})^{\overline{\ad}(m)})\ .

Classes in
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

are thus detected by geometric fixed points for all closed subgroups
of
\tilde{U}(k)
. A caveat is that if
G\leq U(k)
, then
\sigma^{G}={\mathbb{R}}
,
and the geometric fixed points live in
\Phi_{1}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
;
and if
G
maps onto
C
, then
\sigma^{G}=0
, and the geometric fixed points
live in
\Phi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})
.

If the subgroup
G
is contained in
U(k)
, then the relations holds after restriction
to
G
by the previous step, and hence also after taking
G
-geometric fixed points.
If
G
is not contained in
U(k)
, then
\sigma^{G}=0
,
and thus
\Phi^{G}(a_{\sigma})=1
. Thus

\displaystyle\Phi^{G}(u_{k})\

\displaystyle=\ \Phi^{G}(a_{\sigma}\cdot u_{k})

(
2.13
)

\displaystyle=\ \Phi^{G}(q_{*}(v_{k}))\ =\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\Phi^{G}(a_{\sigma}\cdot\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j})))

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\Phi^{G}(\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j})))\ .

The third equation is Theorem
2.3
,
plus the fact that
q_{*}(\tau_{0})=0
, and the relation

q_{*}(1\times(a_{\ad(j)}\cdot\tau_{j}))\ =\ q_{*}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\ad(j)}\cdot\tau_{j}))\ =\ p_{2}^{*}(a_{\ad(j)}\cdot q_{*}(\tau_{j}))\ =\ a_{\sigma}\cdot p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j})

for all
j\geq 1
.
∎

3.
The interplay of the global splitting and the eigenspace morphism

In this section we establish a subtle connection between
two a priori unrelated features of the ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{U}}
,
namely its
C
-global stable splitting and
its preferred infinite delooping.
As we show in Theorem
3.3
,
the adjoint
\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}}
of the preferred infinite delooping

{\mathbf{U}}\sim\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})
from Theorem
B.49

annihilates all the higher terms
of the stable global splitting (
1.1
).
This fact is fundamental for all other results in this paper.

For an augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
and
a compact
G
-space
A
, we write
KR_{\alpha}(A)
for
the Real
\alpha
-equivariant K-group of
A
,
the Grothendieck group of Real
\alpha
-equivariant vector bundles,
see Construction
B.6
.
If
A
is endowed with a
G
-fixed basepoint, we write

\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)
for the reduced K-group, the kernel of restriction to the basepoint

KR_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow KR_{\alpha}(\ast)
. The multiplicative structure by tensor product
of Real-equivariant vector bundles makes the groups

KR_{\alpha}(A)
and
\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)
into modules over

KR_{\alpha}(\ast)=RR(\alpha)
, the Real representation ring of
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
.

As before we let
\tilde{T}^{k}=T^{k}\rtimes C
denote the
semi-direct product of the
k
-torus by the complex conjugation action,
augmented to
C
by the projection.
We call an
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module
Euler-torsion-free

if the Euler class of every irreducible Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
with trivial fixed points acts injectively on the module.

Proposition 3.1
.

For every
k\geq 1
and
l\geq 0
, the
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module

\widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{l\sigma})
is Euler-torsion-free.

Proof.

We start by showing that for every abelian group
M
, the
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes M
is Euler-torsion-free.
The ring
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
is a Laurent polynomial ring

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\ =\ {\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\dots,x_{k}^{\pm}]\ ,

with
x_{i}=p_{i}^{*}(\nu_{1})
for
p_{i}\colon\tilde{T}^{k}\longrightarrow\tilde{T}

the projection to the
i
-th factor.
Indeed, the forgetful ring homomorphism

\res^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{T^{k}}\ :\ RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\ \longrightarrow\ RU(T^{k})\ =\ {\mathbb{Z}}[x_{1}^{\pm},\dots,x_{k}^{\pm}]

is injective by
[
4
, page 13]
, and the ring

RU(T^{k})
is generated by the underlying unitary representations of the
Laurent monomials in the
x_{i}
. So this restriction map is an isomorphism.

The irreducible Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representations
\lambda
correspond to the
monomial units
x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{i_{k}}

for
i_{1},\dots,i_{k}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
; and
\lambda
has trivial fixed points
precisely when not all
i_{j}
equal to
0
.
Moreover, the Euler class of
\lambda
is the element

e_{\lambda}\ =\ 1-x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdot\ldots\cdot x_{k}^{i_{k}}\ .

So for
\lambda^{\tilde{T}^{k}}=0
, the underlying abelian group of
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})/(e_{\lambda})

is free, and hence
\text{Tor}(RR(\tilde{T}^{k})/(e_{\lambda}),M)=0
.
This means that multiplication by
e_{\lambda}
is injective on

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes M
, so
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes M
is Euler-torsion-free.

Now we turn to the proof of the proposition.
All irreducible Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representations
are 1-dimensional, and hence of real type, i.e., their only automorphisms
are scalars from
{\mathbb{R}}
.
So for every compact space
A
with trivial
\tilde{T}^{k}
-action,

[
4
, Proposition 8.1]
provides
an isomorphism

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes KO(A)\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ KR_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(A)\ .

Applying this to
A=S^{n}
and the restriction to its basepoint
yields a commutative square

\textstyle{RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes KO(S^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{KR_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{n})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes KO(\ast)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{KR_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\ast)}

in which both horizontal maps are isomorphisms.
So the upper map restricts to an isomorphism between the vertical kernels

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes\widetilde{KO}(S^{n})
and
\widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{n})
.

Now we choose an
m\geq 0
such that
l\leq 8m
.
The orthogonal
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
1\oplus\sigma

underlies a Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation (with trivial
T^{k}
-action).
So Bott periodicity
[
3
, Theorem (5.1)]

for the Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
l\cdot(1\oplus\sigma)

and the 8-fold Bott periodicity of Real equivariant K-theory
provide isomorphisms of
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-modules

RR(\tilde{T}^{k})\otimes\widetilde{KO}(S^{8m-l})\ \cong\ \widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{8m-l})\ \cong\ \widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{8m+l\sigma})\ \cong\ \widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{l\sigma})\ .

We showed above that this
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module is Euler-torsion-free,
so this completes the proof.
∎

The connective global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{ku}}
is defined in

[
24
, Construction 3.6.9]
,
generalizing a configuration space model of Segal
[
30
]

to the global equivariant context. It comes with a multiplicative
involution
\psi
by complex conjugation that enhances it
to the connective Real-global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}=({\mathbf{ku}},\psi)
.
We recall the definition in Construction
B.34
.
The
eigenspace morphism
of based orthogonal
C
-spaces

\eig\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})

is defined in
[
24
, (6.3.26)]
;
we recall the construction in (
B.36
).
Here ‘
\sh^{\sigma}
’ denotes the shift of an orthogonal
C
-spectrum
by the sign representation, see
[
24
, Construction 3.1.21]
.
Shifting by
\sigma
is Real-globally equivalent to suspending by
S^{\sigma}
,
see
[
24
, Proposition 3.1.25 (ii)]
.
And
\Omega^{\bullet}
is the functor from orthogonal
C
-spectra to based orthogonal
C
-spaces
that is right adjoint to the reduced suspension spectrum functor,
see
[
24
, Construction 4.1.6]
;
the orthogonal
C
-space
\Omega^{\bullet}X
models the underlying
‘Real-global infinite loop space’ of a Real-global spectrum
X
.
As the name suggest, the eigenspace morphism assigns to a unitary automorphism
the configuration of eigenvalues and eigenspaces;
the shift coordinate in
\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}
is the place that stores the eigenvalues.
We write

(3.2)

\eig^{\flat}\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}

for the adjoint of the eigenspace morphism.

The global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}

was introduced by Joachim
[
15
, Definition 4.3]

as a commutative orthogonal ring spectrum.
Joachim showed in
[
15
, Theorem 4.4]

that the genuine
G
-spectrum underlying the global spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}
represents

G
-equivariant complex K-theory; another proof can be found in

[
24
, Corollary 6.4.23]
.
Joachim’s orthogonal ring spectrum can be enhanced to an orthogonal
C
-ring spectrum
{\mathbf{KR}}

by suitably incorporating complex conjugation, see
[
12
, Section 6]
;
this is the
periodic Real-global K-theory spectrum
.
We review the definition in Construction
B.43
,
and we show in Theorem
B.52
that
{\mathbf{KR}}
represents
Real-equivariant K-theory for augmented Lie groups, justifying the name.
A morphism of commutative orthogonal
C
-ring spectra

j\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{KR}}

from connective to periodic Real-global K-theory
is defined in
[
24
, Corollary 6.4.13]
;
we review the definition in Construction
B.48
.

Theorem 3.3
.

For every
k\geq 2
, the morphism of orthogonal
C
-spectra

(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}}

annihilates the class
\omega_{k}\in\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

defined in (
1.15
).

Proof.

We recall that

\varrho\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\ast={\mathbb{S}}\text{\quad and\quad}q\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}

denote the morphisms induced on reduced suspension
C
-spectra
by the based maps
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow S^{0}
and
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}

that, respectively, map
{\mathbf{U}}
to the non-basepoint of
S^{0}
,
and are the identity on
{\mathbf{U}}
.
By Proposition
1.11
,
the class
\tau_{1}
in
\pi_{\ad(1)}^{\tilde{T}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})

belongs to the augmentation ideal, i.e.,
\varrho_{*}(\tau_{1})=0
.
So the class
\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1}
lies in the
k
-th power of
the augmentation ideal, where
k
is the number of factors.
Proposition
B.40
(ii) shows that
the map
(\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}
annihilates
the square of the augmentation ideal of
\pi_{\star}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
.
In particular,

(\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}(\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1})\ =\ 0

for
k\geq 2
.

We let
D\subset M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
denote the Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
of lower subdiagonal matrices (
1.19
),
and
a_{D}\in\pi_{-D}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbb{S}})
is its pre-Euler class (
0.1
).
We use the isomorphism (
1.20
) to identify
\ad(k)
and
k\sigma\oplus D

as orthogonal
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representations.
Using Theorem
1.21
we obtain the relation

\displaystyle a_{D}^{2}\cdot\eig^{\flat}_{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\omega_{k}))\ _{\eqref{eq:define_omega_k}}

\displaystyle=\ (\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}(a_{D}^{2}\cdot\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\tau_{k}))

\displaystyle=\ (\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}(a_{D}\cdot(\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1}))

\displaystyle=\ a_{D}\cdot((\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}(\tau_{1}\times\dots\times\tau_{1}))\ =\ 0

in
\pi_{k\sigma-D}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})
, for all
k\geq 2
.

The representation
D

inherits the structure of a Real
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation from
M_{k}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Real-equivariant Bott periodicity
[
3
, Theorem (5.1)]

provides an associated Bott class
\beta_{D}\in\pi_{D}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbf{KR}})

with corresponding Euler class
e_{D}=\beta_{D}\cdot a_{D}
in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbf{KR}})
.
Then

e_{D}^{2}\cdot\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{k}))\ =\ \beta_{D}^{2}\cdot(\sh^{\sigma}j)_{*}(a_{D}^{2}\cdot\eig^{\flat}_{*}(\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(\omega_{k})))\ =\ 0

in
\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{k\sigma+D}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})
.
Real-equivariant Bott periodicity for
D
also provides an isomorphism
of
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbf{KR}})
-modules

\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{k\sigma+D}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\ \cong\ \pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{k\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\ \cong\ \pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{(k-1)\sigma}({\mathbf{KR}})\ .

By Theorem
B.52
(ii),
the ring
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbf{KR}})
is isomorphic to the Real representation
ring
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
, in a way that identifies the

\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}^{k}}({\mathbf{KR}})
-module
\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{(k-1)\sigma}({\mathbf{KR}})

with the
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module
\widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{(k-1)\sigma})
.
This
RR(\tilde{T}^{k})
-module
is Euler-torsion-free by Proposition
3.1
.
The
\tilde{T}^{k}
-representation
D
has trivial fixed points,
and thus multiplication by the Euler class
e_{D}

is injective on
\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{(k-1)\sigma}({\mathbf{KR}})
. So we deduce that

\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{k}))\ =\ 0

in
\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{k\sigma+D}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\cong\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{(k-1)\sigma+D}({\mathbf{KR}})
.
We let
\overline{\ad}(k)
denote the reduced adjoint representation of
\tilde{U}(k)
,
i.e., the subrepresentation of
\ad(k)
of matrices with trivial trace.
Any isomorphism
\ad(k)\cong\sigma\oplus\overline{\ad}(k)
provides an identification

\pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\ad(k)}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\ \cong\ \pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\sigma\oplus\overline{\ad}(k)}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\ \cong\ \pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\overline{\ad}(k)}({\mathbf{KR}})\ .

The restriction homomorphism

\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}\ :\ \widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{U}(k)}(S^{\overline{\ad}(k)})\ \longrightarrow\ \widetilde{KR}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}(S^{\overline{\ad}(k)})

is split injective, see for example
[
3
, Proposition (5.2)]
.
So also the restriction homomorphism

\res^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{T}^{k}}\ :\ \pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\overline{\ad}(k)}({\mathbf{KR}})\ \longrightarrow\ \pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{\overline{\ad}(k)}({\mathbf{KR}})=\pi^{\tilde{T}^{k}}_{(k-1)\sigma+D}({\mathbf{KR}})

is injective.
Hence
((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{k})=0

in
\pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\ad(k)}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})\cong\pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\overline{\ad}(k)}({\mathbf{KR}})
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

The fact that the morphism

(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}}

annihilates the class
\omega_{k}
for all
k\geq 2
is crucial for all further results
in this paper.
We alert the reader that the eigenspace morphism

\eig^{\flat}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}
itself
does
not
annihilate the class
\omega_{k}
for any
k\geq 2
.
At this point one might want to recall that the name ‘connective global K-theory’
has to be taken with a grain of salt, in that the morphism
j\colon{\mathbf{kr}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}

is an equivariant connective cover for
finite
groups
by Theorems 6.3.27 and 6.4.21 of
[
24
]
, but not generally
for compact Lie groups of positive dimension.

In fact, the composite

\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\ \xrightarrow{\eig^{\flat}}\ \sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}\ \xrightarrow{\sh^{\sigma}\dim}\ \sh^{\sigma}(Sp^{\infty})

does not annihilate
\omega_{k}
for
k\geq 2
.
Here
Sp^{\infty}
denotes the orthogonal spectrum, with trivial
C
-action,
made from infinite symmetric products of spheres, compare
[
24
, Example 5.3.10]
.
And
\dim\colon{\mathbf{kr}}\longrightarrow Sp^{\infty}
is the dimension homomorphism to
the infinite symmetric product spectrum, defined in
[
24
, Example 6.3.36]
.
The spectrum
Sp^{\infty}
is
{{\mathcal{F}}in}
-globally equivalent to the Eilenberg–MacLane spectrum
for the constant global functor
\underline{{\mathbb{Z}}}
, see Propositions 5.3.9 and 5.3.12
of
[
24
]
. However, for compact Lie groups
G
of positive dimension,
the groups
\pi_{*}^{G}(Sp^{\infty})
are typically not concentrated in dimension 0,
and the ring
\pi_{0}^{G}(Sp^{\infty})
need not be isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}
.
For example,

\pi_{1}^{T}(Sp^{\infty})\cong{\mathbb{Q}}
, see
[
24
, Theorem 5.3.16]
,
and the abelian group
\pi_{0}^{SU(2)}(Sp^{\infty})
has rank 2,
see
[
23
, Example 4.16]
.

We show the non-vanishing for
k=2
, the other cases being similar but slightly more involved.
The class
u_{k}
from (
2.11
)
is represented by the suspension by
S^{\nu_{k}}
of the composite

\delta_{k}=\zeta^{k}_{*}\circ\partial\circ\mathfrak{c}\ :\ S^{\sigma}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))\ .

This map sends
x\in S^{\sigma}
to the unitary automorphism of
u(\nu_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}

that is multiplication by
\mathfrak{c}(x)
on the image of the
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear monomorphism

\zeta^{k}\colon\nu_{k}\longrightarrow u(\nu_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}
, and the identity on its orthogonal complement.
The morphism
\eig^{\flat}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}

extracts eigenvalues and eigenspaces, and turns the eigenvalues into a suspension coordinate
via
\mathfrak{c}^{-1}
;
and as the name suggests, the morphism
\dim\colon{\mathbf{kr}}\longrightarrow Sp^{\infty}

takes a configuration of vector spaces to the configuration of the dimensions.
So the class
((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(u_{k})

is represented by the map

S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sigma}\ \longrightarrow\ Sp^{\infty}(S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sigma})\ =\ \sh^{\sigma}(Sp^{\infty})(u(\nu_{k}))\ ,\quad x\ \longmapsto\ k\cdot x\ ,

the point
x\in S^{\nu_{k}\oplus\sigma}
with multiplicity
k
.
This map represents
\sh^{\sigma}(k\cdot 1)
,
the
k
-fold multiple of the shifted multiplicative unit
in
\pi^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}(Sp^{\infty}))
, and thus

((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(u_{k})\ =\ \sh^{\sigma}(k\cdot 1)\ .

Theorem
2.16
for
k=2
and the fact that
\omega_{1}=u_{1}
provide the relation

a_{\overline{\ad}(2)}\cdot\omega_{2}\ =\ u_{2}-\tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}(p_{2}^{*}(u_{1}))\ .

Thus

\displaystyle a_{\overline{\ad}(2)}\cdot((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{2})\

\displaystyle=\ ((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(u_{2})\ -\ \tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}(p_{2}^{*}(((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(u_{1})))

\displaystyle=\ \sh^{\sigma}\left(2-\tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}(1)\right)\ .

By the remark immediately before
[
23
, Example 4.16]
,
the classes
1
and
\tr_{U(1,1)}^{U(2)}(1)
are linearly independent in the group

\pi_{0}^{U(2)}(Sp^{\infty})
.
Hence the classes

1
and
\tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}(1)
are linearly independent in the group

\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(2)}(Sp^{\infty})
.
Thus the class
((\sh^{\sigma}\dim)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{2})
is non-zero.

4.
The global Segal–Becker splitting

In this section we construct the global Segal–Becker splitting

c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
in the unstable Real-global
homotopy category, see (
4.10
).
This morphism comes into existence as a
C
-global
\sigma
-loop map,
the delooping being the morphism

d\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))

defined in (
4.6
).
That the morphisms
d
and
c
are indeed sections to

\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

and to
\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})
, respectively,
is proved in Theorems
4.7
and
4.12
.
In Corollary
4.16
we show that the composite

c\circ h\colon{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

of the global Segal–Becker splitting with the morphism

h\colon{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}
that represents the inclusion of line bundles into
virtual vector bundles is the unit of the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+},\Omega^{\bullet})
.

Construction 4.1

(The Real-global ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{P}}
)
.

We recall the orthogonal
C
-space
{\mathbf{P}}
made from complex projective spaces,
compare
[
24
, (2.3.20)]
, a specific ultra-commutative model
for the Real-global classifying space of the extended circle group

\tilde{T}=\tilde{U}(1)=U(1)\rtimes C
.
In
[
24
]
, we use the notation
{\mathbf{P}}^{\mathbb{C}}
to distinguish this version
made from complex projective spaces from the real version.
In this paper, the real version plays no role, so we simplify notation
and drop the superscript ‘
{\mathbb{C}}
’.
The value of
{\mathbf{P}}
at the inner product space
V
is

{\mathbf{P}}(V)\ =\ P(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}))\ ,

the complex projective space of the symmetric algebra of the complexification.
The structure map
{\mathbf{P}}(\varphi)\colon\allowbreak{\mathbf{P}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{P}}(W)

induced by a linear isometric embedding
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow W

takes a complex line to its image under

\Sym(\varphi_{\mathbb{C}})\colon\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow\Sym(W_{\mathbb{C}})
.
The involution
\psi(V)\colon{\mathbf{P}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{P}}(V)

is induced by complex conjugation on
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
,
exploiting that also conjugate-linear maps take
{\mathbb{C}}
-subspaces to
{\mathbb{C}}
-subspaces.

As a Real-global classifying space for
\tilde{T}
,
the orthogonal
C
-space
{\mathbf{P}}
represents the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}
on the
unstable Real-global homotopy category.
We will later need to refer to the universal element,
the tautological class, so we recall it here.
We consider the
\tilde{T}
-invariant complex line

{\mathbb{L}}\ =\ {\mathbb{C}}\cdot(1\otimes 1-i\otimes i)\ =\ \im(\zeta^{1}\colon\nu_{1}\longrightarrow u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}})\ \subset\ u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}\ ,

where
\zeta^{1}
is defined in (
1.6
).
We also view
{\mathbb{L}}
as a complex line in the symmetric algebra via the embedding

u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\Sym(u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}})
as the linear summand.
Then
{\mathbb{L}}
is a
\tilde{T}
-fixed point of
P(\Sym(u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}))={\mathbf{P}}(u(\nu_{1}))
;
the unstable and stable
tautological classes

(4.2)

u_{\tilde{T}}\ \in\ \pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{P}})\text{\qquad and\qquad}e_{\tilde{T}}\ \in\ \pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

are, respectively, its homotopy class and
the class represented by the
\tilde{T}
-equivariant map

S^{\nu_{1}}\ \xrightarrow{-\wedge{\mathbb{L}}}\ S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge{\mathbf{P}}(u(\nu_{1}))_{+}\ =\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})(u(\nu_{1}))\ .

Then the pair
({\mathbf{P}},u_{\tilde{T}})
represent
the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}
on the unstable Real-global homotopy category;
and the pair
(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}},e_{\tilde{T}})

represent the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}
on the Real-global stable homotopy category.

Construction 4.3

(The morphism
\eta\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}
)
.

The morphism of non-equivariant spectra from
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}
to
KU

that classifies the tautological complex line bundle over
{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}

has a particularly nice and prominent Real-global refinement

\eta\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}
,
defined as a composite of two morphisms of ultra-commutative
C
-ring spectra

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \mu\ }\ {\mathbf{kr}}\ \xrightarrow{\ j\ }\ {\mathbf{KR}}\ .

The first morphism
\mu
is the inclusion of the ‘rank 1’ part
in the rank filtration, compare
[
24
, Construction 6.3.40]
;
its value at an inner product space
V
is the
C
-equivariant map

\mu(V)\ :\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})(V)=S^{V}\wedge P(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}))_{+}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V})={\mathbf{kr}}(V)\ ,\quad v\wedge L\longmapsto[L;v]\ .

We review the definition of the morphism
j\colon{\mathbf{kr}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}
in Construction
B.48
.

The underlying morphism of Real-global spectra of
\eta
classifies
the tautological
\tilde{T}
-representation:
under the preferred identification of
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{KR}})

with the Real representation ring
RR(\tilde{T})

given by Theorem
B.52
(ii),
the stable tautological class (
4.2
) maps to the class of the
tautological
\tilde{T}
-representation, i.e.,

\eta_{*}(e_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ [\nu_{1}]\quad\text{in $\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{KR}})\cong RR(\tilde{T})$.}

Because
\eta
is a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-ring spectra,
its effect on equivariant homotopy groups is not only compatible with restriction,
inflations and transfers,
but also with multiplicative power operations and norms.
In
[
27
]
, I establish a global equivariant generalization of
Snaith’s celebrated theorem
[
31
,
32
]
,
saying that
KU
can be obtained from
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}
by ‘inverting the Bott class’.

Construction 4.4

(The
\sigma
-deloop of the global Segal–Becker splitting)
.

A natural morphism
\lambda^{\sigma}_{X}\colon X\wedge S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}X

for orthogonal
C
-spectra is defined in
[
24
, (3.1.23)]
.
This morphism is a
C
-global equivalence by
[
24
, Theorem 3.1.25 (ii)]
.
Theorem
1.16
lets us define
C
-global morphisms from
\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}

by specifying
their values on the classes
\omega_{k}
in
\pi_{\ad(k)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

defined in (
1.15
).
So we let

(4.5)

d^{\flat}\ :\ \Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

be the unique morphism in the
C
-global stable homotopy category such that

d^{\flat}_{*}(\omega_{k})\ =\ \begin{cases}(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}(e_{\tilde{T}}\wedge S^{\sigma})&\text{ for $k=1$, and}\\
\quad 0&\text{ for $k\geq 2$.}\\
\end{cases}

For
k=1
we have implicitly identified the sign representation
with
\ad(1)
by sending
x\in\sigma
to
i\cdot x\in\ad(1)
.
Informally speaking, the morphism
d^{\flat}
is the projection onto
the summand
k=1
in the stable splitting (
1.1
).

As a Quillen adjoint functor pair for the
C
-global model structures,
the pair
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\bullet})
derives to an adjoint functor pair at the
level of
C
-global homotopy categories.
The stable morphism
d^{\flat}
from (
4.5
)
is thus adjoint to an unstable morphism in the homotopy
category of based
C
-global spaces

(4.6)

d\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\ .

This is our
\sigma
-deloop of the global Segal–Becker splitting.

We can now prove Theorem A from the introduction.

Theorem 4.7
.

The composite

{\mathbf{U}}\ \xrightarrow{\ d\ }\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\ \xrightarrow{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)}\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

equals the morphism
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})
,
and is thus a
C
-global equivalence.

Proof.

We claim that the following diagram commutes in the
C
-global stable homotopy category:

(4.8)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
