By Theorem
1.16
, it suffices to show that
both composites agree on the classes
\omega_{k}
for all
k\geq 1
.
For
k\geq 2
, we have
d^{\flat}_{*}(\omega_{k})=0
by definition,
and
((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{k})=0
by Theorem
3.3
.
So both composites in the diagram (
4.8
) annihilate the class
\omega_{k}
.

For
k=1
the following diagram of based
\tilde{T}
-maps commutes by inspection of definitions:

\textstyle{S^{\nu_{1}\oplus\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge\mathfrak{c}}
(
2.7
)
\scriptstyle{-\wedge{\mathbb{L}}\wedge-}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge U(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge\zeta^{1}_{*}}
(
1.7
)
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})(u(\nu_{1}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eig^{\flat}(u(\nu_{1}))}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{1}}\wedge{\mathbf{P}}(u(\nu_{1}))_{+}\wedge S^{\sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})(\nu_{1})}
\textstyle{S^{\nu_{1}\oplus\sigma}\wedge{\mathbf{P}}(u(\nu_{1})\oplus\sigma)_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\sh^{\sigma}\mu)(\nu_{1})}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{kr}}(u(\nu_{1})\oplus\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})(u(\nu_{1}))}

Indeed, both composites send
x\in S^{\nu_{1}\oplus\sigma}

to the one-element configuration

[{\mathbb{L}},x]\ \in\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym((u(\nu_{1})\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{\nu_{1}\oplus\sigma})\ =\ (\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})(u(\nu_{1}))\ ,

the point
x
labeled by the complex line

{\mathbb{L}}={\mathbb{C}}\cdot(1\otimes 1-i\otimes i)\subset u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}
,
embedded via
u(\nu_{1})_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow(u(\nu_{1})\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow\Sym((u(\nu_{1})\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}})
.
This diagram witnesses the relation

((\sh^{\sigma}\mu)\circ\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}(e_{\tilde{T}}\wedge S^{\sigma})\ =\ \eig^{\flat}_{*}(u_{1})

in the group
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{T}}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})
.
Since
\eta=j\circ\mu
by definition, we obtain

\displaystyle((\sh^{\sigma}\eta)\circ d^{\flat})_{*}(\omega_{1})\

\displaystyle=\ ((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ(\sh^{\sigma}\mu)\circ\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}(e_{\tilde{T}}\wedge S^{\sigma})\ =\ ((\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig^{\flat})_{*}(u_{1})\ .

Since
u_{1}=\omega_{1}
by Proposition
2.8
,
this concludes the proof that the diagram (
4.8
) commutes.
Passing to adjoints for the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\bullet})
,
turns the commutative diagram (
4.8
) into the desired relation

\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig=\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)\circ d\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})
.
The composite
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig

is a
C
-global equivalence by Theorem
B.49
,
hence so is
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)\circ d
.
∎

Now we construct the actual global Segal–Becker splitting

c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
by looping the morphism

d\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))
by the sign representation,
and exploiting the Real-global Bott periodicity equivalence
{\mathbf{BUP}}\sim\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
.

Construction 4.9

(The global Segal–Becker splitting)
.

The ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
{\mathbf{BUP}}

is defined in
[
24
, Example 2.4.33]
,
and we recall the construction in
B.3
.
In Theorem
B.24
we establish a Real-global form of
Bott periodicity, summarized by the Real-global equivalence of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

\gamma\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

defined in (
B.26
).
We then define the
global Segal–Becker splitting

(4.10)

c\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

as the unique morphism in the unstable based
C
-global homotopy category
that makes the following diagram commute:

(4.11)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 168.46695pt\raise-38.15pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 186.09195pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 186.09195pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})))}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

The unnamed isomorphism swaps the order in which the loops are taken,
and
\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{X}\colon X\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}X)

is the adjoint of
\lambda^{\sigma}_{X}\colon X\wedge S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}X
.

The next theorem verifies that the
global Segal–Becker splitting is indeed a splitting of the morphism

\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})
.
In Theorem
5.12
will we show that
our global Segal–Becker realizes the classical equivariant Segal–Becker splittings
at the level of cohomology theories, thereby justifying its name.

Theorem 4.12
.

The composite

{\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ c\ }\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ \xrightarrow{\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)}\ \Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})

equals the morphism
\Theta\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})

defined in (
B.51
), and is thus a
C
-global equivalence.

Proof.

Several definitions and a previous result assemble into a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{BUP}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}d}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig)\hskip 8.19447pt}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta))}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta))}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}}))}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{{\mathbf{KR}}})}
\scriptstyle{\sim}

The parts of the diagram involving the morphisms
\Theta
and
c

commute by the respective definitions (
B.51
) and (
4.11
).
The inner triangle starting at
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
commutes by Theorem
4.7
.
The right part of the diagram commutes by naturality of the
\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{X}
-morphisms.
The morphism
\Theta
is a
C
-global equivalence by Theorem
B.52
(i),
hence so is the composite
\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)\circ c
.
∎

Remark 4.13

(No section deloops twice)
.

Since
\Omega^{\bullet}(\eta)\colon\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})

is a Real-global infinite loop map with an unstable section,
one can wonder how often one can deloop an unstable section.
Our construction of the section
c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

presents it as a
C
-global
\sigma
-loop map, the deloop being the morphism

d\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))
.
If we forget the
C
-action and pass to underlying global morphisms,
these data witness the global Segal–Becker splitting as global loop map.
However, one cannot do better than this, not even non-equivariantly, as we now recall.

The H-space structure on the infinite unitary group
U
coming from Bott periodicity
coincides with the one from the group structure. Under the Pontryagin product,
the mod 2 homology
H_{*}(U;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})
is an exterior
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
-algebra on classes

a_{i}\in H_{2i+1}(U;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})
for
i\geq 0
.
In contrast,
H_{*}(\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}\wedge S^{1});{\mathbb{F}}_{2})

is a polynomial
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
-algebra on the iterated Kudo–Araki operations
on a basis of
\tilde{H}_{*}({\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}_{+}\wedge S^{1};{\mathbb{F}}_{2})
,
see for example
[
11
, Theorem 5.1]
.
So the epimorphism of commutative graded
{\mathbb{F}}_{2}
-algebras

(\Omega^{\infty}(\eta\wedge S^{1}))_{*}\ :\ H_{*}(\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}\wedge S^{1});{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\ \longrightarrow\ H_{*}(\Omega^{\infty}(KU\wedge S^{1});{\mathbb{F}}_{2})\ \cong\ H_{*}(U;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})

does not admit a multiplicative section. Hence the map

\Omega^{\infty}(\eta\wedge S^{1})\colon\Omega^{\infty}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbb{C}}P^{\infty}\wedge S^{1})\longrightarrow\Omega^{\infty}(KU\wedge S^{1})
does not have a section that is an H-map, much less a loop map.

The
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant linear embedding
\zeta^{k}\colon\nu_{k}\longrightarrow u(\nu_{k})_{\mathbb{C}}

was defined in (
1.6
).
Its image is a
\tilde{U}(k)
-invariant linear subspace of dimension
k
,
and thus a
\tilde{U}(k)
-fixed point of
Gr_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(u(\nu_{k})_{\mathbb{C}})=\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(u(\nu_{k}))
.
We write

\{\nu_{k}\}\ =\ [\im(\zeta^{k})]\ \in\ \pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k})\subset\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\mathbf{Gr})

for its homotopy class.
The next proposition determines the image of this class under the composite

\mathbf{Gr}\ \xrightarrow{\ i\ }\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ c\ }\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ .

Theorem 4.14
.

For every
k\geq 1
, the relation

(c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{k}\}\ =\ \tr_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}})

holds in the group
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
.

Proof.

The following diagram commutes by the definition (
4.11
) of the morphism

c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
from the morphism

d\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))
,
which in turn was defined as the adjoint of

d^{\flat}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
:

\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\mathbf{Gr})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\beta_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbf{BUP}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{c_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\gamma_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\wedge S^{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}\wedge S^{\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k)}}
\scriptstyle{d_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d^{\flat}_{*}}
\textstyle{\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))}

The map
\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k)}\colon\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbf{U}})\longrightarrow\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})

is the stabilization map
[
24
, (3.3.12)]
.

By inspection of definitions,
the map
\beta_{*}\colon\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\mathbf{Gr})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}})

defined in (
B.27
)
sends
\{\nu_{k}\}
to the homotopy class of the map

\delta_{k}=\zeta^{k}_{*}\circ\mathfrak{c}\circ\partial\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}(u(\nu_{k}))
.
The class
u_{k}
defined in (
2.11
)
is represented by the suspension by
S^{\nu_{k}}
of
\delta_{k}
, so

\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\beta_{*}\{\nu_{k}\})\ =\ u_{k}\ .

The commutative diagram thus shows the relation

(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}((c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{k}\}\wedge S^{\sigma})=d^{\flat}_{*}(u_{k})

in
\pi_{\sigma}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))
.
The defining property of the morphism
d^{\flat}
and Theorem
2.16
then yield

\displaystyle(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}((c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{k}\}\wedge S^{\sigma})\ =\ d^{\flat}_{*}(u_{k})\

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}d_{*}^{\flat}(\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot\omega_{j}))

\displaystyle=\ \sum_{1\leq j\leq k}\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-j,j)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}(a_{\overline{\ad}(j)}\cdot d_{*}^{\flat}(\omega_{j}))

\displaystyle=\ \tr_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(p_{2}^{*}((\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}(e_{\tilde{T}}\wedge S^{\sigma})))

\displaystyle=\ (\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}(\tr_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}})\wedge S^{\sigma})\ .

Suspension by
S^{\sigma}
and the map
(\lambda^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})_{*}

are bijective, so this proves the desired relation.
∎

The pair
({\mathbf{P}},u_{\tilde{T}})
represents the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}

on the unstable
C
-global homotopy category. So we can define

(4.15)

h\ :\ {\mathbf{P}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{BUP}}

as the unique morphism such that
h_{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})=i_{*}\{\nu_{1}\}

in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{BUP}})
.
The morphism
h
represents the inclusion of line bundles into
virtual vector bundles.

Corollary 4.16
.

The composite

{\mathbf{P}}\ \xrightarrow{\ h\ }\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ c\ }\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is the unit of the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+},\Omega^{\bullet})
.

Proof.

For
k=1
, Theorem
4.14

specializes to
(c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{1}\}=e_{\tilde{T}}
in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
.
So

(c\circ h)_{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ (c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{1}\}\ =\ e_{\tilde{T}}\ .

The adjunction unit also takes the unstable tautological
class
u_{\tilde{T}}
to the stable tautological class
e_{\tilde{T}}
.
Since
({\mathbf{P}},u_{\tilde{T}})
represents the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}
on the unstable

C
-global homotopy category, this proves the claim.
∎

We end this section with a discussion of the additivity, or rather the failure
thereof, of the global Segal–Becker splitting.
If we forget the Real direction and pass to underlying
‘non-Real’ global spaces, then the sign action on
S^{\sigma}
disappears,
and
\sigma
-loops become ordinary loops.
By the Eckmann–Hilton argument, the loop addition then coincides
with the abelian monoid structure from any ultra-commutative multiplication,
and loop maps are automatically additive.
In particular, the morphism of global spaces underlying
the Real-global Segal–Becker splitting is a global loop map,
and so the induced map
[A,c]^{G}\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{G}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{G}

is additive whenever
G
is a compact Lie group with trivial augmentation to
C
.

¨

However, the map
[A,c]^{\alpha}
induced by the Real-global Segal–Becker splitting
is
not
generally additive for surjective augmentations
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
.
In fact, additivity already fails for
G=C
with identity augmentation,
and for
A=\ast
, see Example
4.23
.
The rest of this section aims to explain this more carefully,
including some quantification of the failure of additivity
in Theorem
4.20
(ii).

Our tool to quantify the deviation from additivity
is a certain piece of natural structure on
\sigma
-loop objects.
In contrast to the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(X,x)=[S^{1},X]_{*}

in the non-equivariant context, the set
[S^{\sigma},X]_{*}^{C}

has no group structure that is natural for based
C
-equivariant maps in
X
.
Equivalently, the
\sigma
-sphere does not admit an
equivariant ‘pinch map’
S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}
,
i.e., such that the composite with each of the two projections is
equivariantly homotopic to the identity.
The
C
-map
m\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}
we are about to define
is a partial remedy of this defect.
If we forget the
C
-action, the underlying non-equivariant
homotopy class of
m
represents the element
xy^{-1}x
in the free group

\pi_{1}(S^{1}\vee S^{1},\ast)
, where
x
and
y
denote the classes of the left and right
wedge summand inclusions.

Construction 4.17

(A binary operation on
\Omega^{\sigma}X
)
.

We let
X
be a based orthogonal
C
-space.
We define a specific binary operation (
4.18
)
on the
\sigma
-loop space
\Omega^{\sigma}X
.
We consider the
C
-equivariant based map

\displaystyle m^{\prime}\ :\ U(1)\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ U(1)\vee U(1)\ ,\quad m^{\prime}(z)\ =\ \begin{cases}(\phantom{-}z^{4},1)&\text{ if $\text{Re}(z)\geq 0$, and}\\
(-z^{2},2)&\text{ if $\text{Re}(z)\leq 0$.}\end{cases}

The second coordinate in the formula for
m^{\prime}(z)
specifies in which of the two wedge summands
of
U(1)\vee U(1)
the respective point lies.
The map
m^{\prime}
is clearly
C
-equivariant for the action by complex conjugation on all
instances of
U(1)
.
We define a
C
-equivariant map
m\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}

by conjugating
m^{\prime}
with the Cayley transform (
2.7
), i.e., as the composite

S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{\ \mathfrak{c}\ }U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ m^{\prime}\ }\ U(1)\vee U(1)\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{\mathfrak{c}^{-1}\vee c^{-1}}\ S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}\ .

Precomposition with
m\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}
then yields a morphism

(4.18)

m^{*}\ :\ (\Omega^{\sigma}X)\times(\Omega^{\sigma}X)\ \cong\ \map_{*}(S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma},X)\ \xrightarrow{\map_{*}(m,X)}\ \map_{*}(S^{\sigma},X)=\Omega^{\sigma}X\ .

We write
\epsilon\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}

for the sign involution, i.e.,
\epsilon(x)=-x
.
For every based orthogonal
C
-space
X
, it induces an
involution
\epsilon^{*}\colon\Omega^{\sigma}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}X
by precomposition.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
X
be a based orthogonal
C
-space.
Then the composite

\Omega^{\sigma}X\ \xrightarrow{\ (\Id,\Id)\ }\ (\Omega^{\sigma}X)\times(\Omega^{\sigma}X)\ \xrightarrow{m^{*}}\ \Omega^{\sigma}X

is equivariantly homotopic to the identity, and the composite

\Omega^{\sigma}X\ \xrightarrow{\ (\ast,\Id)\ }\ (\Omega^{\sigma}X)\times(\Omega^{\sigma}X)\ \xrightarrow{m^{*}}\ \Omega^{\sigma}X

is equivariantly homotopic to
\epsilon^{*}\colon\Omega^{\sigma}X\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}X
.

Proof.

We exploit that
C
-equivariant self-maps of
U(1)
, for the action by complex conjugation,
are characterized up to equivariant based homotopy by their value on the fixed point
-1

and by the degree of the underlying non-equivariant map.
The composite

U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ m^{\prime}\ }\ U(1)\vee U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ \nabla\ }\ U(1)

fixes
-1
and has underlying degree
+1
, so it is
equivariantly based homotopic to the identity, where
\nabla
denotes the fold map.
The composite

U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ m^{\prime}\ }\ U(1)\vee U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ p_{2}\ }\ U(1)

fixes
-1
and has underlying degree
-1
, so it is
equivariantly based homotopic to complex conjugation, where
p_{2}
denotes the
projection to the second wedge summand.
After conjugation with the Cayley transform, these properties become that
facts that the two composites

S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ m\ }\ S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ \nabla\ }\ S^{\sigma}\text{\qquad and\qquad}S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ m\ }\ S^{\sigma}\vee S^{\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ p_{2}\ }\ S^{\sigma}

are equivariantly based homotopic to the identity and
to the sign involution
\epsilon\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}
, respectively.
The claim follows by applying
\map_{*}(-,X)
to the maps and homotopies.
∎

We abuse notation and also write
\epsilon\in\pi_{0}^{C}({\mathbb{S}})

for the
C
-equivariant stable homotopy class
of the sign involution
\epsilon\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}
.
This element satisfies
\epsilon^{2}=1
and
is related to the transfer by
\tr_{\{1\}}^{C}(1)=1-\epsilon
.
The equivariant homotopy sets
[A,E]^{\alpha}
are defined in (
A.3
).
Part (ii) of the next theorem refers to the
module structure of the group

[A,\Omega^{\bullet}Y]^{\alpha}\ \cong\ \pi_{0}^{G}(\map(A,\alpha^{*}(Y)))

over the ring
\pi_{0}^{G}({\mathbb{S}})
.

The following theorem is straightforward for trivially augmented Lie groups.
Indeed, in that case the
G
-map underlying
c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is a loop map, and thus induces an additive map

c_{*}\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{G}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{G}
.
Moreover,
\res^{C}_{\{1\}}(\epsilon)=-1
, so if
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is the trivial
homomorphism, then
\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)=-1
.
The formula of part (ii) of the following theorem thus becomes
c_{*}(2x+y)=2c_{*}(x)+c_{*}(y)
.
In contrast, if
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is surjective,
then
\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\neq-1
in
\pi_{0}^{G}({\mathbb{S}})
,
and
c_{*}\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

need not be additive, see Example
4.23
.

Theorem 4.20
.

Let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group, and let
A
be
a finite
G
-CW-complex.

(i)

Let
\psi\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
be a morphism in the based
C
-global homotopy category.
Then the map

(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}\ :\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}

satisfies the relation

(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(2x-y)\ =\ 2\cdot(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(x)-(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(y)

for all
x,y\in[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}
.

(ii)

The map

c_{*}\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

satisfies the relation

c_{*}(2x+y)\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot c_{*}(x)\ +\ c_{*}(y)

for all
x,y\in[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

We start with a preliminary observation.
We let
M
be an abelian group endowed with a group homomorphism
\mu\colon M\times M\longrightarrow M

such that
\mu(x,x)=x
for all
x\in M
.
Then

(4.21)

\mu(x,y)\ =\ \mu((x,x)-(0,x)+(0,y))\ =\ x-\mu(0,x)+\mu(0,y)

for all
x,y\in M
, by the homomorphism property.

(i) The binary operation
m^{*}
on
\Omega^{\sigma}X
defined in (
4.18
)
is natural for morphisms of Real-global spaces in
X
. In particular, it is
natural for the multiplication morphism
{\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
.
Hence the map

m^{*}\ =\ [A,m^{*}]^{\alpha}\ :\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\times[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}

is a homomorphism of abelian groups.
Proposition
4.19
shows that
m^{*}(x,x)=x
and

m^{*}(0,x)=[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}(x)
.
Relation (
4.21
) and Proposition
B.32

thus yield

m^{*}(x,y)\ =\ x-m^{*}(0,x)+m^{*}(0,y)\ =\ x-[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}(x)+[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}(y)\ =\ 2x-y

for all
x,y\in[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}
.
The map
(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}
is compatible with all operations
that are natural for
C
-global
\sigma
-loop spaces.
This includes in particular the binary operation
m^{*}
. So

\displaystyle(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(2x-y)\

\displaystyle=\ (\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(m^{*}(x,y))

\displaystyle=\ m^{*}((\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(x),(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(y))\ =\ 2\cdot(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(x)-(\Omega^{\sigma}\psi)_{*}(y)

for all
x,y\in[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}
.

(ii)
We claim that the map

[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}\colon[A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}

is multiplication by the class
\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\in\pi_{0}^{G}({\mathbb{S}})
,
for every orthogonal
C
-spectrum
Y
.
This is almost a tautology, and a special case of a more
general fact: for every orthogonal representation
V
of a compact Lie group
G
,
and for every continuous based
G
-map
f\colon S^{V}\longrightarrow S^{V}
,
precomposition with
f
and multiplication by
[f]\in\pi_{0}^{G}({\mathbb{S}})

coincide on
\pi_{V}^{G}(X)
for every orthogonal
G
-spectrum
X
.

Also for every orthogonal
C
-spectrum
Y
, the map

m^{*}\ =\ [A,m^{*}]^{\alpha}\ :\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}\times[A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}

is additive for the abelian group structure arising from stability.
Relation (
4.21
) and the claim above thus yield the relation

m^{*}(x,y)\ =\ x-m^{*}(0,x)+m^{*}(0,y)\ =\ x-[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}(x)+[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}(y)\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot x+\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\cdot y

for all
x,y\in[A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)]^{\alpha}
.
The map

(\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}=[A,\Omega^{\sigma}d]^{\alpha}\ :\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})))]^{\alpha}

is compatible with all operations that are natural for
C
-global
\sigma
-loop spaces.
This includes in particular the binary operation
m^{*}
. So

(4.22)

\displaystyle(\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(2x-y)\

\displaystyle=\ (\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(m^{*}(x,y))

\displaystyle=\ m^{*}((\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(x),(\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(y))

\displaystyle=\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot(\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(x)\ +\ \alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\cdot(\Omega^{\sigma}d)_{*}(y)

for all
x,y\in[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}
.

The global Segal–Becker splitting
c
was defined by the
commutative square (
4.11
). It induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A,c]^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{[A,\gamma]^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})]^{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A,\Omega^{\sigma}d]^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})))]^{\alpha}}

in which all objects are abelian groups, but the horizontal maps are

not
generally additive.
The morphism
\gamma
is a Real-global equivalence of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids,
so the induced bijection is additive.
The
C
-global equivalence
\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}})

arises from a
C
-global stable map,
so the induced bijection is additive and compatible with multiplication
by the class
\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)
.
So relation (
4.22
) for the lower horizontal map
implies the analogous relation for the upper horizontal map
c_{*}=[A,c]^{\alpha}
, i.e.,

c_{*}(2x-y)\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot c_{*}(x)\ +\ \alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\cdot c_{*}(y)

for all
x,y\in[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
.
Setting
x=0
yields
c_{*}(-y)=\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\cdot c_{*}(y)
, and thus

\displaystyle c_{*}(2x+y)\

\displaystyle=\ c_{*}(2x-(-y))

\displaystyle=\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot c_{*}(x)+\alpha^{*}(\epsilon)\cdot c_{*}(-y)\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot c_{*}(x)+c_{*}(y)\ .\qed

Example 4.23

(
c_{*}
is not additive)
.

In the special case of the group
C
augmented by the identity, and for
A=\ast
,
the global Segal–Becker splitting becomes a map

c_{*}\ :\ \pi_{0}^{C}({\mathbf{BUP}})\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{0}^{C}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ .

In this case the group
\pi_{0}^{C}({\mathbf{BUP}})\cong RR(C)
is infinite cyclic,
generated by the class
1=h_{*}(\res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(u_{\tilde{T}}))
,
where
u_{\tilde{T}}\in\pi^{\tilde{T}}_{0}({\mathbf{P}})

is the unstable tautological class (
4.2
),
and
h\colon{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}
is the ‘inclusion of line bundles’ defined in (
4.15
).
Moreover,
\pi_{0}^{C}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
is a free module of rank 1
over the ring
\pi_{0}^{C}({\mathbb{S}})={\mathbb{Z}}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-1)
,
also known as the Burnside ring of the group
C
.
We have
c_{*}(0)=0
and

c_{*}(1)\ =\ c_{*}(h_{*}(\res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(u_{\tilde{T}})))\ =\ \res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(c_{*}(h_{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})))\ =\ \res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(e_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ 1\ .

The third equation uses that
c\circ h\colon{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is the adjunction unit, see Corollary
4.16
.
Theorem
4.20
(ii) provides the relation

c_{*}(2)\ =\ (1-\epsilon)\cdot c_{*}(1)\ =\ 1-\epsilon\ \neq\ 2\ =\ 2\cdot c_{*}(1)\ .

In particular, the map
c_{*}
is
not
additive.

5.
The
G
-equivariant Segal–Becker splitting and explicit Brauer induction

In this section we show that our global Segal–Becker splitting

c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

rigidifies and globalizes the classical Segal–Becker
splittings at the level of equivariant cohomology theories,
and that it induces the Boltje–Symonds ‘explicit Brauer induction’ on equivariant homotopy groups.
The first fact is Theorem C of the introduction, and Theorem
5.12
below;
the second fact is Theorem D of the introduction, and Corollary
5.20
below.

Construction 5.1

(The equivariant Segal–Becker splitting)
.

We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group.
We recall the
\alpha
-equivariant Segal–Becker splitting
via equivariant transfers due to Iriye–Kono
[
14
, §3]
,
following Crabb’s presentation
[
10
]
.
Crabb only discusses the construction
for complex vector bundles, which corresponds to the special case where
the augmentation
\alpha
is trivial, and hence need not be mentioned.
The construction generalizes to general augmented Lie groups;
however, a subtlety arises when passing from vector bundles to virtual vector bundles,
due to the non-additivity of the Segal–Becker splitting for surjectively augmented Lie groups.

We let
\xi\colon E\longrightarrow A
be a Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
over a finite
G
-CW-complex. We denote by

(5.2)

P\xi\ :\ PE\ \longrightarrow\ A

the projectivized bundle; its fiber over
a\in A
is the projective space
of the complex vector space
E_{a}=\xi^{-1}(\{a\})
.
The total space
PE
inherits a continuous
G
-action, and the projection
to
A
is
G
-equivariant.
The projection (
5.2
) thus has an associated transfer,
a morphism in the homotopy category of
genuine
G
-spectra from
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}A
to
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}PE
.
Since
A
is a finite
G
-CW-complex, this transfer is represented by
a continuous based
G
-map

\tau(P\xi)\ :\ S^{V}\wedge A_{+}\ \longrightarrow\ S^{V}\wedge(PE)_{+}\ ,

for some orthogonal
G
-representation
V
.
The tautological complex line bundle over
PE

is a Real
\alpha
-line bundle, and thus classified by a continuous
G
-map

\kappa\ :\ PE\ \longrightarrow\ \alpha^{\flat}({\mathbf{P}}(W))

for some sufficiently large orthogonal
G
-subrepresentation
W
of
{\mathcal{U}}_{G}
.
By enlarging, if necessary, we may assume that
V=W
.
We write

\vartheta(\xi)\ \in\ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

for the class of the adjoint to the composite

S^{V}\wedge A_{+}\ \xrightarrow{\tau(P\xi)}\ S^{V}\wedge(PE)_{+}\ \xrightarrow{S^{V}\wedge\kappa_{+}}\ S^{V}\wedge\alpha^{\flat}({\mathbf{P}}(V))_{+}\ .

The class
\vartheta(\xi)
only depends on the isomorphism class of
the
\alpha
-vector bundle
\xi
. So the construction provides a well-defined map

(5.3)

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}\ :\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

that is natural for continuous
G
-maps in
A
, and for restriction along
morphisms of augmented Lie groups.

At this point a possibly unexpected feature appears for surjectively augmented Lie groups.
If the augmentation is trivial, then Real
\alpha
-vector bundles
are just complex
G
-vector bundles, and we are in the context discussed by
Crabb
[
10
]
.
In this case the map (
5.3
) is additive
for the Whitney sum of vector bundles, see
[
10
, Lemma 2.6]
.
It thus extends uniquely to an additive map
on the group completion
KU_{G}(A)
, the complex
G
-equivariant K-group of
A
.

¨

As we show in Remark
5.5
below, the equivariant Segal–Becker
splitting (
5.3
) is
not
additive
whenever the augmentation
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is surjective.
Consequently, one cannot appeal to the group completion property
to extend (
5.3
) from
\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
to
KR_{\alpha}(A)
.
We will show in Corollary
5.8
that the map
(
5.3
) enjoys a weakened form of additivity that still allows
to extend it meaningfully to the Grothendieck group, see (
5.11
).

The universal case
of a Real-equivariant vector bundle of dimension
k
is
the tautological
\tilde{U}(k)
-representation
\nu_{k}
,
considered as a Real
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant vector bundle over a point.
We will now identify the equivariant Segal–Becker splitting in this case.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
[\nu_{k}]\in\Vect_{\tilde{U}(k)}^{R}(\ast)
denote the class of the
tautological Real
\tilde{U}(k)
-representation on
{\mathbb{C}}^{k}
,
considered as a Real
\tilde{U}(k)
-equivariant vector bundle over a point.
Then

\vartheta_{\tilde{U}(k),\ast}[\nu_{k}]\ =\ \tr^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}})

in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
,
where
e_{\tilde{T}}\in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is the stable tautological class (
4.2
).

Proof.

The group
\tilde{U}(k)
acts transitively on the projective space
P(\nu_{k})
,
and the complex line

l\ =\ {\mathbb{C}}\cdot(0,\dots,0,1)

has stabilizer group
\tilde{U}(k-1,1)
.
The action on
l
thus identifies
P(\nu_{k})
with the coset space
\tilde{U}(k)/\tilde{U}(k-1,1)
.
So the equivariant transfer

\tau(P(\nu_{k}))\ :\ {\mathbb{S}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}P(\nu_{k})

associated with the unique
\tilde{U}(k)
-map
P(\nu_{k})\longrightarrow\ast

sends
1\in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}({\mathbb{S}})
to the class

\tr^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}[l])\ \in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}P(\nu_{k}))\ ,

where
[l]\in\pi_{0}(P(\nu_{k})^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)})

is the class of the
\tilde{U}(k-1,1)
-fixed point
l
, and

\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}\ :\ \pi_{0}(P(\nu_{k})^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)})\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}P(\nu_{k}))

is the stabilization map
[
24
, (3.3.12)]
.

The stabilizer group
\tilde{U}(k-1,1)
acts on the invariant line
l
through
the homomorphism
p_{2}\colon\tilde{U}(k-1,1)\longrightarrow\tilde{T}

that projects to the second block;
so the classifying
\tilde{U}(k)
-map

\kappa\ :\ P(\nu_{k})\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{P}}({\mathcal{U}}_{U(k)})

for the tautological line bundle satisfies

\kappa_{*}[l]=p_{2}^{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})
in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}({\mathbf{P}})
,
where
u_{\tilde{T}}\in\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{P}})

is the unstable tautological class (
4.2
). Thus

(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\kappa)_{*}(\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}[l])\ =\ \sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(\kappa_{*}[l])\ =\ \sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(p_{2}^{*}(u_{\tilde{T}}))\ =\ p_{2}^{*}(\sigma^{\tilde{T}}(u_{\tilde{T}}))\ =\ 1\times e_{\tilde{T}}\ .

Combining these observations yields

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\tilde{U}(k),\ast}[\nu_{k}]\

\displaystyle=\ ((\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\kappa)\circ\tau(P(\nu_{k})))_{*}(1)\ =\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\kappa)_{*}(\tr^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}[l]))

\displaystyle=\ \tr^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}((\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\kappa)_{*}(\sigma^{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}[l]))\ =\ \tr^{\tilde{U}(k)}_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}})\ .\qed

Remark 5.5

(Beware non-additivity)
.

We show that the map
\vartheta_{\alpha,A}
from (
5.3
)
is
not
additive for augmented Lie groups with non-trivial augmentation.
This is in contrast to trivially augmented Lie groups,
where the map is additive by
[
10
, Lemma 2.6]
.
We use the double coset formula
for
\res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}\circ\tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}
,
where
C
sits inside
\tilde{U}(2)
as complex conjugation.
The general double coset formula for
\res^{G}_{K}\circ\tr_{H}^{G}

can be found in
[
20
, IV Section 6]
or
[
24
, Theorem 3.4.9]
,
and we need to specialize it.
The coset space
\tilde{U}(2)/\tilde{U}(1,1)
is homeomorphic
to the projective space
P({\mathbb{C}}^{2})
, and the
C
-action on

\tilde{U}(2)/\tilde{U}(1,1)
by left translation corresponds to
the action on
P({\mathbb{C}}^{2})
by complex conjugation.
The double coset space
C\backslash\tilde{U}(2)/\tilde{U}(1,1)
is
thus homeomorphic to a 2-disc, stratified by its boundary
(coming from the
C
-fixed points) and the interior (corresponding to the points of

\tilde{U}(2)/\tilde{U}(1,1)
on which
C
acts freely).
The
C
-fixed points
(\tilde{U}(2)/\tilde{U}(1,1))^{C}\cong P({\mathbb{C}}^{2})^{C}
are homeomorphic to
S^{1}
,
thus have Euler characteristic 0, and do not contribute to the double coset formula.
The non-singular part is an open 2-disc, with internal Euler characteristic

\chi^{\sharp}(C\backslash P({\mathbb{C}}^{2})_{\text{free}})\ =\ \chi(D^{2})-\chi(\partial D^{2})\ =\ 1\ .

So the double coset formula becomes

\res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}\circ\tr_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(2)}\ =\ \tr_{\{1\}}^{C}\circ\res^{\tilde{U}(1,1)}_{\{1\}}\ .

Now we consider the group
C
, augmented over itself by the identity.
We show that the map

\vartheta_{C,\ast}\colon\Vect^{R}_{C}(\ast)\longrightarrow\pi_{0}^{C}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is not additive.
The ring
\pi_{0}^{C}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{Z}}[\epsilon]/(\epsilon^{2}-1)
,
for
\epsilon
the class of the sign involution of
S^{\sigma}
.
The monoid
\Vect^{R}_{C}(\ast)
of isomorphism classes of Real
C
-representations
is isomorphic to
{\mathbb{N}}
, generated by the class of
{\mathbb{C}}
with
C
-action by complex conjugation;
another name for this generator is
\res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}[\nu_{1}]
,
with
C
embedded in
\tilde{T}
as complex conjugation.
Proposition
5.4
thus yields

\vartheta_{C,\ast}[{\mathbb{C}}]\ =\ \vartheta_{C,\ast}(\res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}[\nu_{1}])\ =\ \res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(\vartheta_{\tilde{T},\ast}[\nu_{1}])\ =\ \res^{\tilde{T}}_{C}(e_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ 1\ .

We have
[{\mathbb{C}}]\oplus[{\mathbb{C}}]\cong\res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}(\nu_{2})

as Real
C
-representations. So by Proposition
5.4

and the double coset formula,

\displaystyle\vartheta_{C,\ast}(2\cdot[{\mathbb{C}}])\

\displaystyle=\ \vartheta_{C,\ast}(\res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}[\nu_{2}])\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}(\vartheta_{\tilde{U}(2),\ast}[\nu_{2}])\ =\ \res^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{C}(\tr^{\tilde{U}(2)}_{\tilde{U}(1,1)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}}))

\displaystyle=\ \tr_{\{1\}}^{C}(\res^{\tilde{U}(1,1)}_{\{1\}}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}}))\ =\ \tr_{\{1\}}^{C}(1)\ =\ 1-\epsilon\ \neq\ 2\ =\ 2\cdot\vartheta_{C,\ast}[{\mathbb{C}}]\ .

In Corollary
5.8
we show more generally
(but by a less direct argument)
that
\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2\cdot[\xi])=(1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot\vartheta_{\alpha,A}[\xi]
.

In Theorem
B.12
we exhibit a natural isomorphism
of abelian monoids

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ \Vect^{R}_{\alpha}(A)

for every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
and every finite
G
-CW-complex
A
.
The morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
i\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}
is
defined in (
B.4
).

Theorem 5.6
.

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
and every finite
G
-CW-complex
A
,
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\cong\ \eqref{eq:Gr_to_Vect}}
\scriptstyle{[A,i]^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\Vect^{R}_{\alpha}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta_{\alpha,A}}
\textstyle{[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[A,c]^{\alpha}}
\textstyle{[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}}

Proof.

Theorem
4.14

and Proposition
5.4
show that

(5.7)

(c\circ i)_{*}\{\nu_{k}\}\ =\ \tr_{\tilde{U}(k-1,1)}^{\tilde{U}(k)}(1\times e_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ \vartheta_{\tilde{U}(k),\ast}[\nu_{k}]\ .

The map
\langle-\rangle
from (
B.9
) takes
\{\nu_{k}\}
to the isomorphism
class of
\nu_{k}
, considered as a Real
\tilde{U}(k)
-vector bundle over a point.
So equation (
5.7
) shows that
the square commutes for
\alpha=\tilde{U}(k)
and
A=\ast

on the class
\{\nu_{k}\}
.

Now we compose both composites in the diagram with the map

[A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}
induced by the inclusion
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr}
.
Varying
\alpha
and
A
yields two
C
-global transformations
from
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
to
\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

in the sense of Definition
A.9
.
The orthogonal
C
-space
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
is a Real-global classifying space
for the augmented Lie group
\tilde{U}(k)
, and the class
\{\nu_{k}\}

is the tautological class
u_{\tilde{U}(k),\ast}
in the sense of
(
A.11
).
Since the
C
-global transformations coincide for
\alpha=\tilde{U}(k)
and
A=\ast

on the class of
\nu_{k}
, the two
C
-global transformations agree altogether
by the uniqueness part of
Theorem
A.12
. In other words: the diagram commutes for
all classes in the image of
[A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}
for some
k\geq 0
.

Now we suppose that
A
is ‘
G
-connected’ in the sense
that the group
\pi_{0}(G)
acts transitively on
\pi_{0}(A)
.
This ensures that every Real
\alpha
-vector bundle over
A
has constant rank,
and every class in
[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}
is in the image of
[A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}
for some
k\geq 0
.
Hence the diagram commutes for such
A
.

A general finite
G
-CW-complex decomposes as a disjoint union

A=A_{1}\amalg\ldots\amalg A_{m}
of
G
-connected
finite
G
-CW-complexes, indexed by the
\pi_{0}(G)
-orbits of
\pi_{0}(A)
.
We write
\iota_{j}\colon A_{j}\longrightarrow A
for the inclusion of the
j
-th summand.
Then the map

(\iota_{1}^{*},\dots,\iota_{m}^{*})\ :\ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A_{1},\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}\times\dots\times[A_{m},\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

is bijective. Hence it suffices to show that the diagram commutes after postcomposition
with
\iota_{j}^{*}
for each
1\leq j\leq m
. But this is the case by naturality
for
\iota_{j}\colon A_{j}\longrightarrow A
, and because the diagram commutes for
A_{j}

by the previous case.
∎

Theorem
4.20
(ii) and the additivity of
the map
i_{*}\colon[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}

provide the relation

(c\circ i)_{*}(2x+y)\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot(c\circ i)_{*}(x)\ +\ (c\circ i)_{*}(y)

in the group
[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}
, for all
classes
x,y\in[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}
.
Here
\epsilon\in\pi_{0}^{C}({\mathbb{S}})
is the class of the sign involution

\epsilon\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}
, so that

(1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\ =\ \begin{cases}\qquad 2&\text{ if $\alpha$ is trivial, and}\\
\tr_{\ker(\alpha)}^{G}(1)&\text{ if $\alpha$ is surjective.}\end{cases}

The map
\langle-\rangle\colon[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)

is an isomorphism of abelian monoids by Theorem
B.12
(ii).
So the commutative diagram just established in Theorem
5.6

implies:

Corollary 5.8
.

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C

and all Real
\alpha
-equivariant vector bundles
\xi
and
\zeta
over a
finite
G
-CW-complex
A
, the
\alpha
-equivariant
Segal–Becker splitting (
5.3
) satisfies the relation

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2\cdot[\xi]+[\zeta])\ =\ (1-\alpha^{*}(\epsilon))\cdot\vartheta_{\alpha,A}[\xi]\ +\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}[\zeta]\ .

Despite the fact that the Segal–Becker splitting (
5.3
)
it is not generally additive, we can now extend it meaningfully
and naturally
from the monoid
\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
to its group completion
KR_{\alpha}(A)
.

Construction 5.9
.

We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group,
and we let
A
be a finite
G
-CW-complex.
Corollary
5.8
in particular shows that the relation

(5.10)

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2x+y)\ =\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2x)\ +\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}(y)

holds for all classes
x,y\in\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
.
We define a map

(5.11)

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}\ :\ KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

by

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(x-y)\ =\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}(x+y)-\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2y)\ ,

for
x,y\in\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
.
For trivial augmentations, the Segal–Becker splitting (
5.3
)
is additive, and this formula is a slightly over-complicated way to define
the unique additive extension to the group completion.

We omit the straightforward verification, using the relation (
5.10
),
that this assignment is well-defined,
i.e., independent of how a class in
KR_{\alpha}(A)
is presented as the formal difference
of two Real vector bundles.
Given well-definedness, the map (
5.11
) is clearly an
extension of the earlier geometric construction (
5.3
),
which justifies that we give it the same name.
Like the map (
5.3
), the extension (
5.11
)
is additive whenever the augmentation is trivial, but it is
not
additive
whenever the augmentation is surjective.
In any case,
the extension (
5.11
) again satisfies the relation (
5.10
),
but now for all K-theory classes
x,y\in KR_{\alpha}(A)
.

¨

One might be tempted to define the extension (
5.11
)
by sending a formal difference
x-y
to

\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(x)-\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(y)
.
However, this assignment would not be well-defined when the augmentation is surjective.

Now we proceed to prove Theorem C of the introduction,
saying that our global Segal–Becker splitting
induces the classical equivariant Segal–Becker splitting
at the level of equivariant cohomology theories.
In Theorem
B.12
we exhibit a natural isomorphism
of abelian groups

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ KR_{\alpha}(A)

for every augmented Lie group and every finite equivariant CW-complex.

Theorem 5.12
.

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
and every finite
G
-CW-complex
A
,
the map
[A,c]^{\alpha}\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}

coincides with the composite

[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{\ \langle-\rangle\ }\ KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \xrightarrow{\vartheta_{\alpha,A}}\ \ [A,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\alpha}\ .

Proof.

The map
i_{*}\colon[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
is a group completion of abelian monoids by
Proposition
B.5
.
So every class in
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
is of the form
i_{*}(x)-i_{*}(y)

for some
x,y\in[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}
.
The commutative diagram established in Theorem
5.6

and the commutative diagram (
B.11
)
show that
c_{*}=[A,c]^{\alpha}
and
\vartheta_{\alpha,A}\circ\langle-\rangle

coincide on all classes in the image of
i_{*}
,
in particular on the classes
i_{*}(x+y)
and
i_{*}(2y)
. Hence

\displaystyle c_{*}(i_{*}(x)-i_{*}(y))+c_{*}(i_{*}(2y))\

\displaystyle=\ c_{*}(i_{*}(x+y))\ =\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}\langle i_{*}(x+y)\rangle

(
5.11
)

\displaystyle=\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}\langle i_{*}(x)-i_{*}(y)\rangle+\vartheta_{\alpha,A}(2\langle i_{*}(y)\rangle)

\displaystyle=\ \vartheta_{\alpha,A}\langle i_{*}(x)-i_{*}(y)\rangle+c_{*}(i_{*}(2y))\ .

The first equality is Theorem
4.20
(ii).
Subtracting
c_{*}(i_{*}(2y))
shows that the two maps in question coincide
on the generic element
i_{*}(x)-i_{*}(y)
of
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
.
∎

Remark 5.13
.

We augment the group
C=\Gal({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}})
by the identity, so that

KR_{C}(X)
becomes Atiyah’s Real K-theory of a
C
-space
X
.
Kono asserts in
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]

(in different notation) that the map

\eta_{*}\ :\ [\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X,(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{C}\ \longrightarrow\ [\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X,{\mathbf{KR}}]^{C}\ \cong\ KR_{C}(X)

is a split epimorphism whenever
X
is a compact
C
-space.
Shortly thereafter, Iriye and Kono considered the more general situation
of the semi-direct product
\tilde{G}=G\rtimes_{\tau}C
of
a finite group
G
by a multiplicative involution
\tau
,
augmented to
C
by the projection; they assert in

[
14
, Theorem 1’]

(again translated into our notation)
that for compact
\tilde{G}
-spaces
X
‘there exists a split epimorphism’

(5.14)

\eta_{*}\ :\ [\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X,(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{\tilde{G}}\ \longrightarrow\ [\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}X,{\mathbf{KR}}]^{\tilde{G}}\ \cong\ KR_{\tilde{G}}(X)\ .

¨

The usual interpretation of ‘split epimorphism’ is that of a homomorphism
that admits an
additive
section.
With that interpretation, the proofs of
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]

and
[
14
, Theorem 1’]
are flawed.
Indeed, the transfer techniques employed by Kono in
[
19
]

and by Iriye and Kono in §2 of their paper are just different expositions
of special cases of the transfer construction (
5.3
);
but we show in Remark
5.5
that this construction is
not

additive whenever there is a Real direction, i.e., for surjectively
augmented Lie groups. This issue is already present in the simplest Real situation,
i.e., for
C
augmented by the identity.

The proofs of
[
19
, Theorem 1.1]

and of
[
14
, Theorem 1]

both start with an illegitimate reduction step:
we find the statements “we may assume
\xi
is an
n
-dimensional Real vector bundle over
X
”
and
“So we may assume that
x\in K_{G}(X)
is an
n
-dimensional
G
-vector bundle over
X
”,
respectively.
To be able to pass from vector bundles of fixed rank
to general elements in the Grothendieck group,
some
argument is needed.
When dealing with additive constructions, the universal property of the
Grothendieck construction kicks in, often even without explicit mentioning.
But for non-additive constructions such as the present one (
5.3
),
some other justification is needed, for example
the weak form of additivity from Corollary
5.8
;
and clearly, if one extends a non-additive map, the extension will not be additive, either.

Our Real-global splitting in Theorem
4.12

in particular shows that the map (
5.14
) is surjective.
I cannot rule out the possibility that there might exist sections to this map,
different from ours, that are actually additive,
possibly even with some naturality properties in
(G,X)
,
so that the
statement
of
[
14
, Theorem 1’]

might be correct. However, I have no evidence supporting such an expectation.

We conclude this section with the proof of Theorem D from the introduction,
saying that the effect of the global Segal–Becker splitting

c\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{*}{\mathbf{P}})
on equivariant homotopy
groups is the
explicit Brauer induction
of Boltje and Symonds.

Remark 5.15

(Explicit Brauer induction)
.

Brauer showed in
[
8
, Theorem I]

that the complex representation ring of a finite group
is generated, as an abelian group, by representations that are induced from
1-dimensional representations of subgroups.
Segal generalized this result
to compact Lie groups in
[
28
, Proposition 3.11 (ii)]
,
where ‘induction’ refers to smooth induction.
We write
{\mathbf{A}}(T,G)
for the free abelian group with a basis the symbols
[H,\chi]
,
where
H
runs over all conjugacy classes
of closed subgroups of
G
with finite Weyl group,
and
\chi\colon H\longrightarrow T=U(1)
runs over all characters of
H
.
The Brauer–Segal theorem can then be paraphrased as the fact that
for every compact Lie group
G
, the map

(5.16)

{\mathbf{A}}(T,G)\ \longrightarrow\ R(G)

that sends
[H,\chi]
to
\tr_{H}^{G}(\chi^{*}[\nu_{1}])
is surjective,
where
[\nu_{1}]\in R(T)

is the class of the tautological
T
-representation on
{\mathbb{C}}
.
Informally speaking, an ‘explicit Brauer induction’ is a
collection of sections to the maps (
5.16
)
that are specified by a direct recipe, for example an explicit formula,
and with naturality properties as the group
G
varies.
So such maps give an ‘explicit and natural’ way to write virtual representations
as linear combinations of induced representations of 1-dimensional representations.
What qualifies as ‘explicit’ is, of course, in the eye of the beholder.

The first explicit Brauer induction
was Snaith’s formula
[
33
, Theorem 2.16]
;
however, Snaith’s maps are not additive and not compatible with restriction to subgroups.
Boltje
[
7
]
specified a different explicit Brauer induction
formula for finite groups by purely algebraic means;
Symonds
[
34
]
gave a topological interpretation of
the same section in the context of compact Lie groups.
Symonds’ construction
[
34
, §4]
of the sections

(5.17)

b_{G}\ :\ R(G)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{A}}(T,G)

is designed so that

(5.18)

b_{U(k)}[\nu_{k}]\ =\ [U(k-1,1),p_{2}]\text{\qquad in\quad}{\mathbf{A}}(T,U(k))

for all
k\geq 1
, where
p_{2}\colon U(k-1,1)\longrightarrow U(1)=T
is the
projection to the second block.
The Boltje–Symonds maps are additive and natural for
restriction along continuous group homomorphisms;
and the value of
b_{G}
at a 1-dimensional representation with
character
\chi\colon G\longrightarrow T
is given by

b_{G}[\chi]\ =\ [G,\chi]\ \in\ {\mathbf{A}}(T,G)\ .

The Boltje–Symonds maps (
5.17
) are not (and in fact cannot be)
in general compatible with transfers.

Since the orthogonal space
{\mathbf{P}}
is a global classifying space
for the circle group
T
, the global functor
{\underline{\pi}}_{0}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is represented by
T
, see
[
24
, Proposition 4.2.5]
.
In more down-to-earth terms, this means that the abelian group
\pi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

is free, with a basis given by the classes
\tr_{H}^{G}(\chi^{*}(e_{T}))
,
for
(H,\chi)
ranging over all conjugacy classes of closed subgroups
H
of
G

with finite Weyl group, and all continuous homomorphisms
\chi\colon H\longrightarrow T
;
see
[
24
, Corollary 4.1.13]
.
The group
{\mathbf{A}}(T,G)
was defined as a free abelian group with a corresponding basis,
so the map

(5.19)

{\mathbf{A}}(T,G)\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ ,\quad[H,\chi]\ \longmapsto\ \tr_{H}^{G}(\chi^{*}(e_{T}))

is an isomorphism of abelian groups.

The Boltje–Symonds map
b_{G}\colon R(G)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{A}}(T,G)
is the special case
of the equivariant Segal–Becker splitting (
5.11
) for
A=\ast
,
in the sense that the composite

R(G)=KU_{G}(\ast)\ \xrightarrow{\vartheta_{G,\ast}}\ [*,\Omega^{\bullet}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})]^{G}\ =\ \pi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ \cong_{\eqref{eq:A(T)_rep}}\ {\mathbf{A}}(T,G)

agrees with (
5.17
).
Indeed,
b_{G}
and
\vartheta_{G,\ast}
coincide for
G=U(k)

on the class of the tautological representation
\nu_{k}
,
by (
5.18
) and Proposition
5.4
.
So they agree on arbitrary unitary representations by naturality,
and on virtual representations by additivity.
So the next theorem becomes a special case of Theorem
5.12

for
A=\ast
.

Corollary 5.20
.

For every compact Lie group
G
,
the map
\pi_{0}^{G}(c)\colon\pi^{G}_{0}({\mathbf{BUP}})\longrightarrow\pi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})

equals the composite

\pi^{G}_{0}({\mathbf{BUP}})\ \cong_{\eqref{eq:BUP_to_KR_alpha}}\ R(G)\ \xrightarrow[\eqref{eq:define_b_G}]{\ b_{G}\ }\ {\mathbf{A}}(T,G)\ \cong_{\eqref{eq:A(T)_rep}}\ \pi_{0}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}})\ .

6.
Global Adams operations

In this section we use the global Segal–Becker splitting
to construct Real-global rigidifications (
6.5
) of
the unstable Adams operations in equivariant K-theory.

Construction 6.1

(Adams operations in equivariant K-theory)
.

We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group.
We recall the construction of the
\lambda
-operations and Adams operations
on the Grothendieck ring
KR_{\alpha}(A)
of Real
\alpha
-equivariant vector bundles
over a compact
G
-space
A
.
For all Real
\alpha
-vector bundles
\xi
and
\zeta
over
A
,
the Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
\Lambda^{n}(\xi\oplus\zeta)
is isomorphic
to
\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\Lambda^{i}(\xi)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}\Lambda^{n-i}(\zeta)
.
So the map

\Lambda\ :\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ KR_{\alpha}(A)\llbracket t\rrbracket\ ,\quad[\xi]\ \longmapsto\ {\sum}_{n\geq 0}\,[\Lambda^{n}(\xi)]\cdot t^{n}

takes addition in the abelian monoid of isomorphism classes of
\alpha
-vector bundles
to multiplication in the power series ring
KR_{\alpha}(A)\llbracket t\rrbracket
. All these power series
moreover have constant term
\Lambda^{0}(\xi)=1
, and are thus invertible.
So
\Lambda
defines a monoid homomorphism from

\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
to the multiplicative group of the ring
KR_{\alpha}(A)\llbracket t\rrbracket
.
The universal property of the Grothendieck construction thus yields
an extension to a group homomorphism

\Lambda\ :\ KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ (KR_{\alpha}(A)\llbracket t\rrbracket)^{\times}\ .

The
\lambda
-operations

\lambda^{i}\colon KR_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow KR_{\alpha}(A)

are then defined by

\Lambda(x)\ =\ {\sum}_{i\geq 0}\,\lambda^{i}(x)\cdot t^{i}\ .

By design, these operations extend the exterior powers
on classes of actual Real vector bundles.
The
\lambda
-operations
make the ring
KR_{\alpha}(A)
into a special
\lambda
-ring,
see
[
1
, Theorem 1.5 (i)]
.
Every special
\lambda
-ring supports
Adams operations
,
i.e., ring endomorphisms
\psi^{n}
for
n\geq 1

that satisfy
\psi^{m}\circ\psi^{n}=\psi^{mn}
for all
m,n\geq 1
,
as well as the congruence
\psi^{p}(x)\equiv x^{p}
modulo
(p)
for every prime
p
,
see
[
1
, §5]
.
We are particularly interested in these Adams operation

\psi^{n}\ :\ KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ KR_{\alpha}(A)

in the case of Real-equivariant K-theory. One key property
is that on the class of a Real line bundle
\xi
, the Adams operation is given by

\psi^{n}[\xi]\ =\ [\xi^{\otimes n}]\ .

Taking exterior power of vector bundles is natural both for
G
-maps in
A
,
and for restriction along continuous homomorphisms of augmented Lie groups.
Hence the
\lambda
-operations and the Adams operations inherit both
kinds of naturalities.

Example 6.2
.

The second Adams operation
\psi^{2}\colon KR_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow KR_{\alpha}(A)
is given
on the class of a Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
\xi
by the formula

\psi^{2}[\xi]\ =\ [\Sym^{2}(\xi)]\ -\ [\Lambda^{2}(\xi)]\ ,

the formal difference of
the second symmetric and exterior power of
\xi
.
Indeed, this formula has the correct behavior on line bundles, is additive
for Whitney sum in
\xi
, and natural in
(\alpha,A)
. So the naturality
properties force
\psi^{2}
to be given by this formula.
The formula shows that the Adams operations do not generally send vector bundles
(other than line bundles) to vector bundles, but rather to virtual vector bundles.

We shall now use the power endomorphisms
of the ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{P}}
and our global Segal–Becker splitting
to define the global Adams operations on
{\mathbf{BUP}}
.

Construction 6.3

(Global Adams operations)
.

For
n\geq 1
, we write

\mu_{n}\ :\ T\ \longrightarrow\ T\ ,\quad\mu_{n}(\lambda)=\lambda^{n}\text{\qquad and\qquad}\tilde{\mu}_{n}=\mu_{n}\rtimes C\ :\ \tilde{T}\ \longrightarrow\ \tilde{T}

for the
n
-th power homomorphism, and for
its extension to the extended circle group
\tilde{T}=T\rtimes C
, respectively.
Since the pair
({\mathbf{P}},u_{\tilde{T}})
represents the functor
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}
,
we can define a morphism
\phi^{n}\colon{\mathbf{P}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{P}}
in the unstable

C
-global homotopy category by the requirement that

\phi^{n}_{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})\ =\ \tilde{\mu}_{n}^{*}(u_{\tilde{T}})

in
\pi_{0}^{\tilde{T}}({\mathbf{P}})
. The morphism
\phi^{n}
then
represents raising a line bundle to its
n
-th power.
We define

(6.4)

\kappa^{n}\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}

as the unique morphism in the based unstable
C
-global homotopy category making the
following diagram commute:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{\kappa^{n}}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)\circ d}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\phi^{n}))}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{P}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}\eta)}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})}

The
C
-global equivalence
\gamma\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

is defined in (
B.26
).
The
n
-th global Adams operation

(6.5)

\Upsilon^{n}\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{BUP}}

is the unique morphism in the based unstable
C
-global homotopy category making the
following diagram commute:

(6.6)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
