0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 101.98695pt\raise-33.3789pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\alpha^{\flat}(\map(EC,X({\mathcal{U}}_{G})))^{G}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

is a weak equivalence.
Because the functor
\map(EC,-)
preserves the class of
(C\times G)
-maps
that are graph subgroup equivalences, the right vertical map is a weak equivalence, too.

(i)
\Longrightarrow
(ii)
Because
X
is coinduced and
C
-fibrant,
Proposition
A.16
shows that
the morphism
\map(p,X)\colon\allowbreak X\longrightarrow\map(EC,X)

is a
C
-global equivalence.
Because
X
is
C
-fibrant, so is
\map(EC,X)
.
As a
C
-global equivalence between
C
-fibrant objects, the morphism

\map(p,X)
is a
C
-level equivalence, see
[
5
, Lemma A.19]
.
So the upper horizontal map in the diagram (
A.18
) is
a weak equivalence.
Hence the lower horizontal map in (
A.18
) is a
weak equivalence, too, proving condition (ii).

(ii)
\Longrightarrow
(i)
We turn the previous argument around.
Because the lower horizontal map in the diagram (
A.18
) is a
weak equivalence, so is the upper horizontal map,
for every faithful
G
-representation
V
.
So the morphism
\map(p,X)\colon X\longrightarrow\map(EC,X)

is a
C
-level equivalence, and hence a
C
-global equivalence.
Because
X
is
C
-fibrant,
Proposition
A.16
shows that
X
is coinduced.
∎

Proposition A.19
.

Let
X
be a pointed orthogonal
C
-space whose underlying orthogonal
C
-space
is coinduced. Then for every finite based
C
-CW-complex
A
,
also
\map_{*}(A,X)
has a coinduced underlying orthogonal
C
-space.

Proof.

Since the functor
\map_{*}(A,-)
preserves
C
-global equivalences between
based orthogonal
C
-spaces, we can assume without loss of generality
that
X
is
C
-fibrant.
Then the morphism
p^{*}\colon X\longrightarrow\map(EC,X)

is a
C
-global equivalence by Proposition
A.16
.
Since
\map_{*}(A,-)
preserves
C
-global equivalences, also the morphism

\map_{*}(A,p^{*})\ :\ \map_{*}(A,X)\ \longrightarrow\ \map_{*}(A,\map(EC,X))

is a
C
-global equivalence.
The morphism
p^{*}\colon\map_{*}(A,X)\longrightarrow\map(EC,\map_{*}(A,X))

is isomorphic to
\map_{*}(A,p^{*})
, and hence a
C
-global equivalence.
Since
X
is
C
-fibrant, so is
\map_{*}(A,X)
,
so another application of Proposition
A.16

shows that
\map_{*}(A,X)
is coinduced.
∎

Appendix B
Real-global K-theory

In this appendix we extend various features
of global K-theory to the Real-global context.
In particular, we provide Real-global generalizations of many
results in Sections 2.5, 6.3 and 6.4 of
[
24
]
.
In Theorem
B.12
we show that
the Real-global space
{\mathbf{BUP}}
represents Real-global K-theory.
In Theorem
B.24
,
we establish Real-global Bott periodicity,
an equivalence of Real-global ultra-commutative monoids between
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
.
We show in Theorem
B.52
that
the
Real-global K-theory spectrum

{\mathbf{KR}}
deserves its name:
for every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C=\Gal({\mathbb{C}}/{\mathbb{R}})
,
the genuine
G
-spectrum
\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}})
represents

\alpha
-equivariant Real K-theory
KR_{\alpha}
.
Theorem
B.49
shows that (and how) the Real-global space

{\mathbf{U}}
is the Real-global infinite loop space of
{\mathbf{KR}}\wedge S^{\sigma}
.
Consequently, the Real-global space
{\mathbf{BUP}}\sim\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

‘is’ the Real-global infinite loop space underlying
{\mathbf{KR}}
,
see Theorem
B.52
(i).

B.1.
Unstable Real-global K-theory

In this subsection we extend various unstable features of global K-theory
to the Real-global context.
The main results are that the orthogonal
C
-space
{\mathbf{BUP}}

represents Real-global K-theory, see Theorem
B.12
,
and Real-global Bott periodicity, an equivalence
of Real-global ultra-commutative monoids between
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
,
see Theorem
B.24
.
Along the way, we show that the orthogonal
C
-spaces

{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
{\mathbf{U}}
are coinduced, see Theorem
B.22
.

Construction B.1

(
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
)
.

We recall the Grassmannian model
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}

for the Real-global classifying space of
the extended unitary group
\tilde{U}(k)=U(k)\rtimes C
,
augmented by the projection
U(k)\rtimes C\longrightarrow C
.
We deviate slightly from the notation of
[
24
, Section 2.3]
,
where
\mathbf{Gr}
without a superscript is used for the real version
of the additive Grassmannian, and where the complex version is denoted
\mathbf{Gr}^{\mathbb{C}}
;
also, the homogeneous summand
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
is written
\mathbf{Gr}^{{\mathbb{C}},[k]}
.
In the present paper, we shall exclusively work with complex Grassmannians,
so they will be referred to by the simpler name without superscript.
The value of
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
at a euclidean inner product space
V
is

\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)\ =\ Gr_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ ,

the Grassmannian of complex
k
-planes in the complexification
V_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{{\mathbb{R}}}V
.
The structure map
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(\varphi)\colon\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(W)

induced by a linear isometric embedding
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow W

takes takes the images under the complexified linear isometric embedding

\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow W_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The complexification of an
{\mathbb{R}}
-vector space
V

comes with a preferred
{\mathbb{C}}
-semilinear involution

(B.2)

\psi_{V}\ :\ V_{\mathbb{C}}\ \longrightarrow\ V_{\mathbb{C}}\ ,\quad\lambda\otimes v\ \longmapsto\ \bar{\lambda}\otimes v\ .

The involution

\psi(V)\ :\ \mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)\ \longrightarrow\ \mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)

that makes it an orthogonal
C
-space takes a
{\mathbb{C}}
-subspace
L\subset V_{\mathbb{C}}

to the conjugate subspace
\bar{L}=\psi_{V}(L)
.

By
[
24
, Proposition A.31]
,
for every augmented Lie group
\beta\colon K\longrightarrow C

and every complete
K
-universe
{\mathcal{U}}_{K}
, the complex Stiefel manifold

{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\nu_{k},{\mathcal{U}}_{K}^{\mathbb{C}})
with its
(K\times_{C}\tilde{U}(k))
-action by

(k,A)\cdot\varphi\ =\ l_{k}\circ\varphi\circ l_{A}^{-1}

is a universal
(K\times_{C}\tilde{U}(k))
-space for the family
of those closed subgroups that intersect
1\times U(k)
trivially.
A consequence spelled out in
[
24
, Theorem A.33 (i)]

is that
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}
receives a
C
-global equivalence from

B_{\gl}\tilde{U}(k)\ =\ C\times_{\tilde{U}(k)}{\mathbf{L}}(u(\nu_{k}),-)\ ,

a
C
-global classifying space, in the sense of Construction
A.10
,
of the augmented Lie group
\tilde{U}(k)
.

Construction B.3

(
\mathbf{Gr}
and
{\mathbf{BUP}}
)
.

The orthogonal
C
-space

\mathbf{Gr}\ =\ \coprod_{k\geq 0}\,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}

is the disjoint union of the Grassmannians from Construction
B.1
.
So the value of
\mathbf{Gr}
at an inner product space
V

is the disjoint union of all complex Grassmannians in the complexification
V_{\mathbb{C}}
.
Direct sum of subspaces, plus identification
along the isomorphism
V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}}\cong(V\oplus W)_{\mathbb{C}}

provide an ultra-commutative multiplication on
\mathbf{Gr}
, compatible
with the involution by complex conjugation.
This structure makes
\mathbf{Gr}
an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid.

The ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{BUP}}
is the Real-global analog of
the ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{BOP}}
introduced in
[
24
, Example 2.4.1]
,
and its underlying global space is the complex periodic Grassmannian
with the same name from
[
24
, Example 2.4.33]
.
The values of
{\mathbf{BUP}}
are

{\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\ =\ {\coprod}_{n\geq 0}Gr_{n}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\ ,

the full Grassmannian of complex subspaces of
V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. The structure map

{\mathbf{BUP}}(\varphi)\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}(W)
associated with a linear isometric
embedding
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow W
is given by

{\mathbf{BUP}}(\varphi)(L)\ =\ \varphi_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}(L)\ +\ ((W-\varphi(V))_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0)\ .

While the
C
-spaces
{\mathbf{BUP}}(V)
and
\mathbf{Gr}(V^{2})
are equal,
their structure maps are different, making them distinct Real-global homotopy types.

An ultra-commutative multiplication of
{\mathbf{BUP}}
is given by

\mu_{V,W}\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\times{\mathbf{BUP}}(W)\ \longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}(V\oplus W)\ ,\quad\mu_{V,W}(L,L^{\prime})\ =\ \kappa_{V,W}(L\oplus L^{\prime})\ ,

where
\kappa_{V,W}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\cong(V\oplus W)_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is
the shuffle isomorphism
\kappa_{V,W}(v,v^{\prime},w,w^{\prime})=(v,w,v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
.
An involution

\psi(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{BUP}}(V)

is defined in the same way as for
\mathbf{Gr}
by applying the complex
conjugation involution
\psi_{V}^{2}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
to complex subspaces.
All these data make
{\mathbf{BUP}}
an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid.

We define a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

(B.4)

i\ :\ \mathbf{Gr}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{BUP}}

at a euclidean inner product space
V
by

\displaystyle\mathbf{Gr}(V)\ =\ {\coprod}_{k\geq 0}\,Gr_{k}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}})\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ {\coprod}_{n\geq 0}\,Gr_{n}^{\mathbb{C}}(V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}})={\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\ ,\quad L\ \longmapsto\ V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus L\ .

In
[
24
, Proposition 2.4.5]
, we show
in the real (with small ‘r’) context,
that for every compact Lie group
G
and every
G
-space
A
, the homomorphism

[A,i^{\mathbb{R}}]^{G}\colon[A,\mathbf{Gr}^{\mathbb{R}}]^{G}\longrightarrow[A,{\mathbf{BOP}}]^{G}

is a group completion of abelian monoids.
All arguments in the proof carry over almost literally to our presents
Real-global context, and thereby show the following result:

Proposition B.5
.

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
and
every
G
-space
A
, the homomorphism

[A,i]^{\alpha}\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}

is a group completion of abelian monoids.

Construction B.6

(Real-equivariant vector bundles)
.

We recall the notion of Real-equivariant vector bundles
for an augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
. This concept encompasses
real and complex equivariant vector bundles, and Atiyah’s Real vector bundles
[
2
]
.
A
Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
over a
G
-space
A
is the data of

•

a complex vector bundle
\xi\colon E\longrightarrow A
,

•

a continuous
G
-action on the total space
E
,

such that the projection
\xi
is
G
-equivariant, and for all

(g,a)\in G\times A
, the translation map
g\cdot-\colon E_{a}\longrightarrow E_{ga}

is
\alpha(g)
-linear.
In other words, translation by
g
is
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear if
\alpha(g)=1
;
and translation by
g
is conjugate-linear if
\alpha(g)\neq 1
.

A key example is the tautological vector bundle
\gamma_{V}
over the Grassmannian

\mathbf{Gr}(V)=\coprod_{k\geq 0}Gr^{\mathbb{C}}_{k}(V_{\mathbb{C}})
for an orthogonal
G
-representation
V
.
Here
G
acts diagonally on
\mathbf{Gr}(V)
,
through the complexification of the given action on
V
,
and through complex conjugation along the augmentation
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
.
In other words, for a complex subspace
L
of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
, we set

g\cdot L\ =\ \begin{cases}\quad l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(L)&\text{ for $\alpha(g)=1$, and}\\
\psi_{V}(l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(L))=l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(\psi_{V}(L))&\text{ for $\alpha(g)\neq 1$.}\end{cases}

Here
l_{g}\colon V\longrightarrow V
is translation by
g\in G
, and

l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}
is its complexification.

The total space of the tautological bundle
\gamma_{V}
over
\mathbf{Gr}(V)
is

\{(w,L)\ \in V_{\mathbb{C}}\times\mathbf{Gr}(V)\colon w\in L\}\ ;

this bundle does not have constant rank.
The group
G
acts on the total space by

g\cdot(w,L)\ =\ \begin{cases}(l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(w),l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(L))&\text{ for $\alpha(g)=1$, and}\\
(\psi_{V}(l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(w)),\psi_{V}(l_{g}^{\mathbb{C}}(L)))&\text{ for $\alpha(g)\neq 1$.}\end{cases}

These data make
\gamma_{V}
a Real
\alpha
-vector bundle over
\mathbf{Gr}(V)
.

We recall in Examples
B.13
and
B.14

how Real-equivariant vector bundles specialize to real and complex
equivariant vector bundles for trivial and product augmentations, respectively.
For the group
C
augmented by the identity,
Real-equivariant vector bundles specialize to Atiyah’s Real vector bundles
from
[
2
]
.
In
[
3
, Section 5]
and

[
4
, Section 6]
, Atiyah and Segal
consider what they call ‘Real Lie groups’, i.e., Lie groups
G
equipped with a multiplicative
involution
\tau\colon G\longrightarrow G
; this corresponds to case of a semi-direct
product
G\rtimes_{\tau}C
, augmented by the projection to the second factor.
The case of general augmented Lie groups is considered by Karoubi
in
[
17
,
18
]
.

Now we explain in which sense the Real-global space
\mathbf{Gr}
represents
Real-equivariant vector bundles, and
in which sense the Real-global space
{\mathbf{BUP}}
represents
Real-equivariant K-theory. The constructions and theorems are adaptations
of results from
[
24
, Section 2.4]
from the
global to the Real-global context.

Construction B.7
.

We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group, and
k\geq 0
.
We write
\Vect_{\alpha}^{k,R}(A)
for the set of isomorphism classes
of Real
\alpha
-vector bundles of rank
k
over a
G
-space
A
.
We define a map

(B.8)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}\ =\ \colim_{V\in s({\mathcal{U}}_{G})}\,[A,\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V))]^{G}\ \longrightarrow\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{k,R}(A)

as follows.
We let
f\colon A\longrightarrow\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V))
be a continuous
G
-map, for some
orthogonal
G
-representation
V
.
We pull back the tautological Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
\gamma_{V}^{k}
over
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)

and obtain a Real
\alpha
-vector bundle

f^{\ast}(\gamma_{V}^{k})\colon E\longrightarrow A
of rank
k
.
Since the base
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)
of the tautological bundle is compact,
the isomorphism class of the bundle
f^{\ast}(\gamma_{V}^{k})

depends only on the
G
-homotopy class of
f
.
So the construction yields a well-defined map

[A,\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V))]^{G}\ \longrightarrow\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{k,R}(A)\ ,\quad[f]\ \longmapsto\ [f^{\ast}(\gamma_{V}^{k})]\ .

If
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow W
is a linear isometric embedding of
orthogonal
G
-representations,
then the restriction along
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(\varphi)\colon\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(W)

of the tautological Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
\gamma_{W}
over
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(W)

is isomorphic to the tautological Real
\alpha
-vector bundle
\gamma_{V}
over
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V)
.
So the two
\alpha
-vector bundles
f^{\ast}(\gamma_{V})

and
(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(\varphi)\circ f)^{\ast}(\gamma_{W})
over
A
are isomorphic.
We can thus pass to the colimit over the poset
s({\mathcal{U}}_{G})

of finite-dimensional
G
-subrepresentations of
{\mathcal{U}}_{G}
,
and get a well-defined map (
B.8
).

We let
\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
denote the commutative monoid, under Whitney sum,
of isomorphism classes of Real
\alpha
-vector bundles over
A
.
We define a monoid homomorphism

(B.9)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)

in much the same way as the map (
B.8
), with the main difference
that now the vector bundles need not have constant rank.
The map (
B.9
) is a monoid homomorphism
because all additions in sight arise from direct sum of inner product spaces.

Now we group complete the picture.
We denote by
KR_{\alpha}(A)
the
\alpha
-equivariant Real K-group of
A
, i.e.,
the group completion (Grothendieck group) of the abelian monoid
\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)
.
In some other references, the Real K-group
KR_{\alpha}(A)
is denoted
KR_{G}(A)
,
i.e., only the group
G
, but not the augmentation
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
,
is recorded in the notation.
The composite

[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow[\eqref{eq:Gr_to_Vect}]{\ \langle-\rangle\ }\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)\ \longrightarrow\ \ KR_{\alpha}(A)

is a monoid homomorphism to an abelian group.
The morphism
[A,i]^{\alpha}\colon[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}

is a group completion of abelian monoids by Proposition
B.5
,
where
i\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}
was defined in (
B.4
).
So there is a unique homomorphism of abelian groups

(B.10)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ KR_{\alpha}(A)

such that the following square commutes:

(B.11)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 26.70149pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-20.65492pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-26.70149pt\raise-16.22806pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{[A,i]^{\alpha}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.45613pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.42291pt\raise 8.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle-\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.66318pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 54.66318pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.90738pt\raise-24.45613pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-26.21043pt\raise-32.45613pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.35902pt\raise-40.45613pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\langle-\rangle}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.97986pt\raise-32.45613pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.97986pt\raise-32.45613pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{KR_{\alpha}(A)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

The next theorem generalizes
[
24
, Theorem 2.4.10]

to the Real-equivariant context.

Theorem B.12
.

Let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group, and let
A
be
a finite
G
-CW-complex.

(i)

For every
k\geq 0
, the map (
B.8
)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ \Vect_{\alpha}^{k,R}(A)

is bijective.

(ii)

The monoid homomorphism (
B.9
)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ \Vect^{R}_{\alpha}(A)

is an isomorphism.

(iii)

The group homomorphism (
B.10
)

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ KR_{\alpha}(A)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

(i) This statement is proved by essentially the same arguments
as its real (with small ‘r’) predecessor in
[
24
, Theorem 2.4.10]
,
mutatis mutandis.
The argument uses that the complex Stiefel manifold

{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\nu_{k},{\mathcal{U}}_{G}^{\mathbb{C}})
with its
(G\times_{C}\tilde{U}(k))
-action by

(g,A)\cdot\varphi\ =\ l_{g}\circ\varphi\circ l_{A}^{-1}

is a universal
(G\times_{C}\tilde{U}(k))
-space for the family
of those closed subgroups that intersect
1\times U(k)
trivially,
see
[
24
, Proposition A.31]
. This, in turn, implies
that the
G
-space
\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}({\mathcal{U}}_{G}^{\mathbb{C}}))={\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(\nu_{k},{\mathcal{U}}_{G}^{\mathbb{C}})/U(k)

is a classifying space for rank
k
Real
\alpha
-equivariant vector bundles
over compact
G
-spaces.

(ii) We suppose first that
A
is
G
-connected, i.e.,
the group
\pi_{0}(G)
acts transitively on
\pi_{0}(A)
.
This ensures that every Real
\alpha
-vector bundle over
A
has constant rank,
and that every continuous
G
-map
A\longrightarrow\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}(V))
factors through

\alpha^{\flat}(\mathbf{Gr}_{k}(V))
for some
k\geq 0
. Hence both vertical maps in the
following commutative square, induced by the inclusions
\mathbf{Gr}_{k}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Gr}
, are bijective:

\textstyle{\coprod_{k\geq 0}\,[A,\mathbf{Gr}_{k}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
(
B.8
)
\scriptstyle{\langle-\rangle}
\textstyle{\coprod_{k\geq 0}\Vect^{k,R}_{\alpha}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle-\rangle}
(
B.9
)
\textstyle{\Vect^{R}_{\alpha}(A)}

The upper horizontal map is bijective by (i), hence the lower horizontal map is bijective.

In general
A=A_{1}\amalg\ldots\amalg A_{m}
is the disjoint union of
G
-connected
finite
G
-CW-complexes, indexed by the
\pi_{0}(G)
-orbits of
\pi_{0}(A)
.
Since both functors
[-,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}
and
\Vect_{\alpha}^{R}

take finite disjoint unions to products, the general case follows.

Part (iii) follows from (ii) because in the commutative
square (
B.11
) both vertical maps are group
completions of abelian monoids, by
Proposition
B.5
and by definition, respectively.
∎

As we shall explain in the next two examples,
the isomorphism
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\cong KR_{\alpha}(A)
generalizes
analogous isomorphisms for real K-groups and for complex K-groups
that are already discussed in
[
24
, Section 2.4]
.

Example B.13

(Trivially augmented Lie groups)
.

For a compact Lie group
G
, we write
G^{\tr}
for the trivially augmented
Lie group, i.e.,
G
endowed with the trivial homomorphism to
C
.
Then Real
G^{\tr}
-vector bundles are nothing but complex vector bundles,
and thus

KR_{G^{\tr}}(A)\ =\ KU_{G}(A)\ .

The underlying orthogonal space of
{\mathbf{BUP}}
is
the complex periodic Grassmannian with the same name from
[
24
, Example 2.4.33]
.
For trivially augmented Lie groups, Theorem
B.12

thus specializes to an isomorphism

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{G^{\tr}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ KU_{G}(A)\ ,

the complex analog of the isomorphism from
[
24
, Theorem 2.4.10]
.

Example B.14

(Product augmented Lie groups)
.

We augment the product of a compact Lie group
G
with the Galois group
C

by the projection
G\times C\longrightarrow C
to the second factor.
In the following, we shall leave the projection implicit
and simply write
G\times C
for this augmented Lie group.

We consider a
G
-space
A
, and we let the group
C
act trivially on
A
.
Then a Real
(G\times C)
-vector bundle over
A
is nothing but a real

G
-vector bundle (with small ‘r’). More precisely,
the action of the element
(1,\psi)\in G\times C

on the fiber
E_{a}=\xi^{-1}(a)
of a Real
(G\times C)
-vector bundle
is a conjugate linear involution on the complex vector space
E_{a}
, i.e.,
a real structure. The
{\mathbb{R}}
-subspaces
(E_{a})^{\psi}
fixed by
this involution form a real
G
-vector subbundle

\xi^{\psi}\ =\ \ker(\Id-(1,\psi)\cdot-\colon\xi\longrightarrow\xi)

of
\xi
, and
\xi
is naturally isomorphic to the complexification of
\xi^{\psi}
.
This construction implements an equivalence of categories between
Real
(G\times C)
-vector bundles and real
G
-vector bundles over
any
G
-space with trivial
C
-action.
The equivalence induces an isomorphism

(B.15)

KR_{G\times C}(A)\ \cong\ KO_{G}(A)\ .

The orthogonal subspace of
{\mathbf{BUP}}
fixed by the involution ‘is’
the periodic Grassmannian
{\mathbf{BOP}}
from
[
24
, Example 2.4.1]
.
More precisely, the complexification maps

{\mathbf{BOP}}(V)\ =\ \amalg_{n\geq 0}\,Gr_{n}^{\mathbb{R}}(V^{2})\ \longrightarrow\ \amalg_{n\geq 0}\,Gr^{\mathbb{C}}_{n}(V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}})\ =\ {\mathbf{BUP}}(V)\ ,\quad L\ \longmapsto\ {\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}L

form an isomorphism of ultra-commutative monoids to the
\psi
-fixed subobject
{\mathbf{BUP}}^{\psi}
.
For any
G
-space
A
endowed with trivial
C
-action, it thus induces an isomorphism

(B.16)

[A,{\mathbf{BOP}}]^{G}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{G\times C}\ .

Under the identifications (
B.15
) and (
B.16
),
Theorem
B.12
thus specializes to the isomorphism

\langle-\rangle\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BOP}}]^{G}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ KO_{G}(A)

from
[
24
, Theorem 2.4.10]
.

Construction B.17

(The ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
{\mathbf{U}}
)
.

We recall the ultra-commutative monoid
{\mathbf{U}}
made from unitary groups,
compare
[
24
, Example 2.37]
.
The euclidean inner product
\langle-,-\rangle
on
V
induces a hermitian
inner product
(-,-)
on the complexification
V_{\mathbb{C}}={\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}V
,
defined as the unique sesquilinear form that satisfies

(1\otimes v,1\otimes w)=\langle v,w\rangle

for all
v,w\in V
.
The value of the orthogonal space
{\mathbf{U}}
on
V
is

{\mathbf{U}}(V)\ =\ U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ ,

the unitary group of the complexification of
V
.
The complexification of every
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear isometric embedding
\varphi\colon V\longrightarrow W

preserves the hermitian inner products,
so we can define a continuous monomorphism

{\mathbf{U}}(\varphi)\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(V)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}(W)

by conjugation with
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow W_{\mathbb{C}}

and the identity on the orthogonal complement of the image of
\varphi_{\mathbb{C}}
.
The commutative multiplication of
{\mathbf{U}}
is given by the direct sum
of unitary automorphisms

{\mathbf{U}}(V)\times{\mathbf{U}}(W)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{U}}(V\oplus W)\ ,\quad(A,B)\ \longmapsto\ A\oplus B\ ,

where we implicitly used the preferred complex isometry
V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus W_{\mathbb{C}}\cong(V\oplus W)_{\mathbb{C}}
.
An involutive automorphism of ultra-commutative monoids
\psi\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}

is given at
V
by the map

\psi(V)\ :\ U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ ,\quad A\ \longmapsto\ \psi_{V}\circ A\circ\psi_{V}\ ,

where
\psi_{V}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}

is the
{\mathbb{C}}
-semilinear conjugation involution (
B.2
).
This involution makes
{\mathbf{U}}
an orthogonal
C
-space, representing
an unstable Real-global homotopy type.

We write
{\mathbf{BU}}
for the orthogonal
C
-subspace of
{\mathbf{BUP}}
with values

{\mathbf{BU}}(V)\ =\ Gr_{|V|}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\ ,

where
|V|=\dim_{\mathbb{R}}(V)
.
This subobject is the homogeneous summand of degree 0 in the

{\mathbb{Z}}
-grading on
{\mathbf{BUP}}
, and closed under the ultra-commutative multiplication.
Our next aim is to show that the ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
{\mathbf{BU}}
is
a
C
-global deloop of
{\mathbf{U}}
, as the notation suggests. The delooping
will be witnessed by a zigzag of two Real-global equivalences.

Construction B.18
.

We define an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
F
and morphisms of
ultra-commutative monoids

{\mathbf{U}}\ \xrightarrow{\ g\ }\ F\ \xleftarrow{\ h\ }\ \Omega({\mathbf{BU}})\ .

To this end we introduce an auxiliary ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
{\mathcal{L}}
.
Its value at an inner product space is the Stiefel manifold

{\mathcal{L}}(V)\ =\ {\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

of
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear isometric embeddings of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
into
V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. The groups
O(V)

and the non-identity element
\psi\in C
act by conjugation, i.e., by

{{}^{A}\varphi}\ =\ A_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\circ\varphi\circ A_{\mathbb{C}}^{-1}\text{\qquad and\quad}{{}^{\psi}\varphi}\ =\ \psi_{V}^{2}\circ\varphi\circ\psi_{V}

for
(A,\varphi)\in O(V)\times{\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
.
An ultra-commutative multiplication is defined by

{\mathcal{L}}(V)\times{\mathcal{L}}(W)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathcal{L}}(V\oplus W)\ ,\quad(\varphi,\phi)\ \longmapsto\ \kappa_{V,W}\circ(\varphi\oplus\phi)\ ,

where
\kappa_{V,W}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\oplus W^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow(V\oplus W)_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
is

\kappa_{V,W}(v,v^{\prime},w,w^{\prime})=(v,w,v^{\prime},w^{\prime})
.
The unit is
i_{1}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
,
i_{1}(v)=(v,0)
,
which is
(O(V)\times C)
-fixed and multiplicative.

A morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
\im\colon{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BU}}(V)

sends
\varphi\in{\mathbf{L}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})
to its image
\im(\varphi)\in Gr_{|V|}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})={\mathbf{BU}}(V)
.
We define the ultra-commutative monoid
F
as the homotopy fiber
of the morphism
\im\colon{\mathcal{L}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BU}}(V)
over the unit of
{\mathbf{BU}}
, i.e., by a pullback diagram in
the category of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids:

(B.19)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 49.56319pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.90973pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.91634pt\raise-16.17805pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-22.97719pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.32245pt\raise 5.1875pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{p}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.85753pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 111.85753pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{L}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.98253pt\raise-16.17805pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.29762pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\im}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 117.98253pt\raise-24.995pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-49.56319pt\raise-32.35611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0\}\times_{{\mathbf{BU}}}{\mathbf{BU}}^{[0,1]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.13339pt\raise-38.25888pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.40277pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\ev_{1}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.46869pt\raise-32.35611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.46869pt\raise-32.35611pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{BU}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Explicitly,
F(V)
is the space of all pairs
(\omega,\varphi)\in{\mathbf{BU}}(V)^{[0,1]}\times{\mathcal{L}}(V)

consisting of a path
\omega\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BU}}(V)=Gr_{|V|}(V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

and a linear isometric embedding
\varphi\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
such that

\omega(0)\ =\ V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0\text{\quad and\quad}\omega(1)\ =\ \im(\varphi)\ .

As a pullback, the
O(V)
-action, structure maps, involution, multiplication and unit are
all inherited from
{\mathbf{BU}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}
.

A morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
f\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow F
is given at
V
by

f(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(V)=U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ F(V)\ ,\quad A\ \longmapsto\ (\text{const}_{V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0},i_{1}\circ A)\ .

Here
\text{const}_{V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0}\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BU}}(V)

is the constant path at
V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0
, and
i_{1}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

is the embedding as the first summand.
A morphism of ultra-commutative monoids
g\colon\Omega({\mathbf{BU}})\longrightarrow F
is given at
V
by

g(V)\ :\ \map_{*}(S^{1},{\mathbf{BU}}(V))\ \longrightarrow\ F(V)\ ,\quad\omega\ \longmapsto\ (\omega\circ t,i_{1})\ ,

where
t\colon[0,1]\longrightarrow S^{1}
is the continuous map
t(x)=(2x-1)/(x(1-x))

that factors through a homeomorphism
[0,1]/\{0,1\}\cong S^{1}
.

Proposition B.20
.

The morphisms

f\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow F\text{\hskip 20.44434ptand\hskip 20.44434pt}g\ :\ \Omega({\mathbf{BU}})\ \longrightarrow\ F

are
C
-global equivalences of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids.

Proof.

We start with the morphism
f
, which we show is even a
C
-level equivalence.
We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group,
with graph
\Gamma=\{(\alpha(\gamma),\gamma)\colon\gamma\in G\}
.
We let
V
be an orthogonal
G
-representation.
The map

(B.21)

\im(V)^{\Gamma}\ :\ {\mathcal{L}}(V)^{\Gamma}\ =\ ({\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}))^{\Gamma}\ \longrightarrow\ (Gr_{|V|}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}))^{\Gamma}\ =\ {\mathbf{BU}}(V)^{\Gamma}

is a disjoint union of projections from Stiefel manifolds
to the associated Grassmannian manifolds.
Hence this map is a locally trivial fiber bundle, and thus a Serre fibration.
So the map from the strict fiber of (
B.21
)
over
V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0
to the homotopy fiber
is a weak homotopy equivalence.
The right action of
{\mathbf{U}}(V)^{\Gamma}=U(V_{\mathbb{C}})^{\Gamma}
on
i_{1}\colon V_{\mathbb{C}}\longrightarrow V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

by precomposition
identifies
{\mathbf{U}}(V)^{\Gamma}
with the strict fiber of (
B.21
).
So the map

f(V)^{\Gamma}\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(V)^{\Gamma}\ \longrightarrow\ \{V_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus 0\}\times_{{\mathbf{BU}}(V)^{\Gamma}}({\mathbf{BU}}(V)^{\Gamma})^{[0,1]}\times_{{\mathbf{BU}}(V)^{\Gamma}}{\mathcal{L}}(V)^{\Gamma}\ =\ F(V)^{\Gamma}

is a weak homotopy equivalence.
This shows that the morphism
f\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow F
is a
C
-level equivalence in
the sense of
[
5
, Theorem A.2]
, and hence in particular a
C
-global equivalence.

To deal with the morphism
g
we show first that the orthogonal
C
-space

{\mathcal{L}}
is
C
-globally trivial.
For every inner product space
V
, the homotopy

\displaystyle H\ :\ {\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\times[0,\pi/2]\

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\oplus V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

\displaystyle H(\varphi,t)(w,w^{\prime})\

\displaystyle\ =\ (\cos(t)\cdot\varphi(w),(w^{\prime},\sin(t)\cdot w))

witnesses that the map

-\oplus i_{1}\ :\ {\mathcal{L}}(V)={\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{L}}^{\mathbb{C}}(V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}},V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\oplus V_{\mathbb{C}}^{2})

is
(O(V)\times C)
-equivariantly homotopic to a constant map.
The structure map

{\mathcal{L}}(i_{1})\ :\ {\mathcal{L}}(V)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathcal{L}}(V^{2})

is the composite of
-\oplus i_{1}
and the homeomorphism induced by

\kappa_{V,V}\colon V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\oplus V^{2}_{\mathbb{C}}\cong(V\oplus V)_{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
,
so the structure map
{\mathcal{L}}(i_{1})
is also

(O(V)\times C)
-equivariantly null-homotopic.
Hence the underlying orthogonal
C
-space of
{\mathcal{L}}
is
C
-globally contractible.

The lower horizontal evaluation morphism in the defining pullback square (
B.19
)
is a
C
-level fibration.
So also its base change
p\colon F\longrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}
is a
C
-level fibration.
Moreover, the right vertical morphism in the pullback square

\textstyle{\Omega({\mathbf{BU}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{\ast\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathcal{L}}

is a
C
-global equivalence by the above. The pullback of a
C
-global equivalence
along a
C
-level fibration is again a
C
-global equivalence,
see
[
5
, Lemma A.18]
.
So
g\colon\Omega({\mathbf{BU}})\longrightarrow F
is a
C
-global equivalence.
∎

Coinduced orthogonal
C
-spaces were introduced in Definition
A.14
.
As we shall now show, the representing
C
-space
{\mathbf{BUP}}
for Real-global K-theory
is an example. This is useful for our purposes because

C
-global equivalences between coinduced objects can
be detected on underlying global spaces, see Proposition
A.15
.

Theorem B.22
.

The orthogonal
C
-spaces
{\mathbf{BUP}}
,
{\mathbf{U}}
and
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

are coinduced.

Proof.

For showing that
{\mathbf{BUP}}
is coinduced we will exploit that it represents
Real-equivariant K-theory, by Theorem
B.12
(iii).
We abuse notation and simply write
C
for augmented Lie group
\Id_{C}\colon C\longrightarrow C
.
We let
\beta\in\widetilde{KR}_{C}(S^{1+\sigma})
denote
the Bott class associated with the 1-dimensional Real
C
-representation on
{\mathbb{C}}
,
see for example
[
2
, Theorem 2.3]
or
[
3
, Theorem 5.1]
.
Then
i^{*}(\beta)
is a class in
\widetilde{KR}_{C}(S^{1})
, where
i\colon S^{1}\longrightarrow S^{1+\sigma}

is the inclusion of the
C
-fixed points.
Hence
(i^{*}(\beta))^{3}
lies in the group
\widetilde{KR}_{C}(S^{3})
, which is isomorphic to

\widetilde{KO}(S^{3})\cong\pi_{3}(KO)
, and hence trivial.

Now we let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group.
We abuse notation and also write
\sigma
for
the restriction of the sign
C
-representation along the augmentation.
Inflation along
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is compatible
with products in Real-equivariant K-theory, so

\alpha^{*}(i^{*}(\beta)^{3})=(i^{*}(\beta_{\alpha^{*}({\mathbb{C}})}))^{3}=0

in the group
\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(S^{3})
, where

\beta_{\alpha^{*}({\mathbb{C}})}\in\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(S^{1+\sigma})

is the Bott class of the Real
\alpha
-representation
\alpha^{*}({\mathbb{C}})
.
So external multiplication by the class
\alpha^{*}(i^{*}(\beta)^{3})
is
trivial on reduced
KR_{\alpha}
-cohomology of every based finite
G
-CW-complex.
An instance of such a multiplication is the composite

\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S^{3\sigma})\ \xrightarrow{\ i_{3}^{*}\ }\ \widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)\ \xrightarrow{\beta_{\alpha^{*}({\mathbb{C}})}^{3}\cdot}\ \widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S^{3(1+\sigma)})\ ,

where
i_{3}\colon S^{0}\longrightarrow S^{3\sigma}
is the inclusion of the points
\{0,\infty\}
.
Multiplication by
\beta_{\alpha^{*}({\mathbb{C}})}
is an isomorphism,
see
[
3
, Theorem (5.1)]
, so the restriction map
i^{*}_{3}

above is trivial.
Thus for every
n\geq 3
, the long exact sequence in
KR_{\alpha}
-cohomology of the cofiber sequence

A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+}\ \xrightarrow{A\wedge p_{+}}\ A\ \xrightarrow{A\wedge i_{n}}\ A\wedge S^{n\sigma}

splits off a short exact sequence:

0\ \longrightarrow\ \widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)\ \xrightarrow{(A\wedge p)^{*}}\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+})\xrightarrow{\ \partial\ }\ \widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}^{1}(A\wedge S^{n\sigma})\ \longrightarrow\ 0

These exact sequences for
n
and
n+3
participate in a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S((n+3)\sigma)_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\scriptstyle{(A\wedge j_{+})^{*}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}^{1}(A\wedge S^{(n+3)\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(A\wedge S^{n\sigma}\wedge i_{3})^{*}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial}
\textstyle{\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}^{1}(A\wedge S^{n\sigma})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Here
j\colon S(n\sigma)\longrightarrow S((n+3)\sigma)
is
j(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},0,0,0)
.
The right vertical map is another instance of a restriction map
i^{*}_{3}
, and hence trivial.
So the middle vertical map
(A\wedge j_{+})^{*}
factors through the lower left
horizontal map
(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}
. This shows that the maps
(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}
form a pro-isomorphism
from the constant tower with value
\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)
to the tower

\dots\longrightarrow\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S((n+1)\sigma)_{+})\longrightarrow\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+})\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow\widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A\wedge S(\sigma)_{+})\ .

The natural isomorphism
\langle-\rangle\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\cong KR_{\alpha}(A)

from Theorem
B.12

passes to reduced groups, and provides a natural isomorphism

[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\ \cong\ \widetilde{KR}_{\alpha}(A)\ ,

where
A
is any finite based
G
-CW-complex.
So the maps
(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}\colon[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\longrightarrow[A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}
form a pro-isomorphism
from the constant tower with value
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}
to the tower

\dots\longrightarrow[A\wedge S((n+1)\sigma)_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\longrightarrow[A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\longrightarrow\dots\longrightarrow[A\wedge S(\sigma)_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\ .

In particular,
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}
maps isomorphically to the inverse limit

[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ \lim_{n}\ [A\wedge S(n\sigma)_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}_{*}\ ,

and the derived inverse limit of the tower vanishes.
The
C
-space
\bigcup_{n\geq 0}S(n\sigma)
is a model
for the universal free
C
-space
EC
.
So the group
[A\wedge EC_{+},\alpha^{\flat}({\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G}))]^{G}_{*}

participates in a Milnor short exact sequence
with the inverse limit and vanishing derived inverse limit.
We conclude that the map

(A\wedge p_{+})^{*}\ :\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G})]^{\Gamma}_{*}\ \longrightarrow\ [A\wedge EC_{+},{\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G})]^{\Gamma}_{*}\ \cong\ [A,\map(EC,{\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G}))]^{\Gamma}_{*}

is an isomorphism for every finite based
G
-CW-complex
A
,
where
\Gamma
is the graph of
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
.
Taking
A=S^{k}
, for
k\geq 0
and with trivial
G
-action shows that the map

(p^{*})^{\Gamma}\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G})^{\Gamma}\ \longrightarrow\ \map^{\Gamma}(EC,{\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G}))

induces a bijection on
\pi_{0}
and isomorphisms of homotopy groups
at the distinguished basepoint. Since
{\mathbf{BUP}}({\mathcal{U}}_{G})
is a group-like
G
-
E_{\infty}
-space,

(p^{*})^{\Gamma}
is a map of non-equivariant group-like
E_{\infty}
-spaces,
and thus a weak homotopy equivalence.
Proposition
A.17
shows that the orthogonal
C
-space

{\mathbf{BUP}}
is coinduced.

Since
{\mathbf{BUP}}
is coinduced, so is
\Omega({\mathbf{BUP}})=\Omega({\mathbf{BU}})

by Proposition
A.19
.
Since
{\mathbf{U}}
is
C
-globally equivalent to
\Omega({\mathbf{BU}})

by Proposition
B.20
, it is coinduced.
Then
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
is coinduced by Proposition
A.19
.
∎

In
[
24
, Theorem 2.5.41]
, we establish a global equivariant
form of Bott periodicity by exhibiting a zigzag of two global equivalences
of ultra-commutative monoids between
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
\Omega{\mathbf{U}}
.
We shall now lift this to a Real-global equivalence
between
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
.

Construction B.23
.

We endow
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}=\map_{*}(S^{\sigma},{\mathbf{U}})
with the involution
that is diagonal from the sign action on
S^{\sigma}
and the complex conjugation
involution of
{\mathbf{U}}
. This becomes an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
via the pointwise multiplication inherited from
{\mathbf{U}}
.
We let
\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}}=\sh_{\otimes}^{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}{\mathbf{U}}
denote the ‘multiplicative shift’
of
{\mathbf{U}}
by
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
in the sense of
[
24
, Example 1.1.11]
.
The values of this orthogonal space are thus given by

(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})(V)\ =\ {\mathbf{U}}(V\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{2})\ =\ U((V\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{2})_{\mathbb{C}})\ .

The structure maps, ultra-commutative multiplication and an involution are
inherited from
{\mathbf{U}}
, making
\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}}
an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid.
The embeddings

j(V)\ =\ -\otimes(1,0)\ :\ V\ \longrightarrow\ V\otimes{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

as the first summand induce a morphism of ultra-commutative monoids

{\mathbf{U}}\circ j\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}}\ .

On
[
24
, page 224]
we define a morphism of ultra-commutative monoids

\bar{\beta}\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})
.
At that point we were not taking any involutions into account.
However, inspection of all definitions shows that this morphism
is
C
-equivariant with respect to the complex conjugation involutions
on
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
{\mathbf{U}}
, and the sign involution in the loop coordinate in the target;
the sign involution arises because the Cayley transform
\mathfrak{c}\colon S^{1}\longrightarrow U(1)

is
C
-equivariant for the sign involution on the source and complex conjugation on the target.
So
\bar{\beta}
is also a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

\bar{\beta}\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})\ .

The following result generalizes
[
24
, Theorem 2.5.41]

to the Real-global context.

Theorem B.24

(Real-global Bott periodicity)
.

The morphisms of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

{\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \bar{\beta}\ }\ \Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})\ \xleftarrow{\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)}\ \Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

are Real-global equivalences.

Proof.

The morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
{\mathbf{U}}\circ j\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}}

is a Real-global equivalence by
[
5
, Lemma 3.8]
. Hence

\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)\colon\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})

is a Real-global equivalence, too.
The orthogonal
C
-spaces
{\mathbf{BUP}}
and
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

are coinduced by Theorem
B.22
.
The morphism
\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)
is a Real-global equivalence,
so
\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})
is also coinduced.
The morphism
\bar{\beta}\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})

is a global equivalence of underlying non-Real orthogonal spaces
by
[
24
, Theorem 2.5.41]
.
So
\bar{\beta}
is a Real-global equivalence
by Proposition
A.15
.
∎

Proposition
B.20
and Theorem
B.24

provide Real-global equivalences of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

\Omega^{\sigma+1}({\mathbf{BUP}})\ =\ \Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega({\mathbf{BUP}}))\ =\ \Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega({\mathbf{BU}}))\ \sim\ \Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ \sim\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ .

These Real-global equivalences implement a highly structured
refinement of the
(\sigma+1)
-periodicity, also called
(2,1)
-periodicity,
of Real-equivariant K-theory.

Construction B.25
.

We denote by

(B.26)

\gamma\ :\ {\mathbf{BUP}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

the unique morphism in the homotopy category of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
such that
\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)\circ\gamma=\bar{\beta}
.
A morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
\beta\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

is defined in
[
24
, (2.5.38)]
; its value

(B.27)

\beta(V)\ :\ \mathbf{Gr}(V)=Gr^{\mathbb{C}}(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\sigma}(U(V_{\mathbb{C}}))=(\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}})(V)

at an inner product space
V
sends a complex subspace
L\subset V_{\mathbb{C}}

to the loop
\beta(V)(L)\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow U(V_{\mathbb{C}})
such that
\beta(V)(L)(x)
is
multiplication by
\mathfrak{c}(x)\in U(1)
on
L
,
and the identity on the orthogonal complement of
L
.
The morphism
\beta
is
C
-equivariant with respect
to the complex conjugation involutions on
\mathbf{Gr}
and
{\mathbf{U}}
,
and the sign involution in the loop coordinate in the target,
one more time because the Cayley transform
\mathfrak{c}\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow U(1)

is
C
-equivariant for the sign involution on the source and complex conjugation on the target.
Inspection of definitions shows that the following square commutes:

\textstyle{\mathbf{Gr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{BUP}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh_{\otimes}{\mathbf{U}})}

So

\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)\circ\gamma\circ i\ =\ \bar{\beta}\circ i\ =\ \Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)\circ\beta\ .

Since
\Omega^{\sigma}({\mathbf{U}}\circ j)
is a Real-global equivalence, this implies the relation

(B.28)

\gamma\circ i\ =\ \beta\ :\ \mathbf{Gr}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

as morphisms in the homotopy category of Real-global ultra-commutative monoids.

Construction B.29

(The inverse morphism of
{\mathbf{U}}
)
.

The inverse maps

\Im(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(V)=U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ U(V_{\mathbb{C}})={\mathbf{U}}(V)\ ,\quad\Im(V)(A)=A^{-1}

are compatible with complex conjugation and make the following diagrams commute:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(V)\times{\mathbf{U}}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Im(V)\times\Im(W)}
\scriptstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(V)\times{\mathbf{U}}(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(V\oplus W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Im(V\oplus W)}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{U}}(V\oplus W)}

So they form a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids
\Im\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}

that models the inverse map in the ultra-commutative addition.
Indeed, composition of unitary automorphism
is a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

\circ\colon{\mathbf{U}}\times{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
that makes the following diagram commute:

(B.30)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern Here
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon{\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
are the projections to the two factors;
the combined morphism

(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\colon{\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}\times{\mathbf{U}}
is a
C
-global equivalence
by
[
5
, Proposition 3.9]
.
The lower horizontal composite is constant with value the identity
automorphism, and hence the zero morphism in the ultra-commutative multiplication.
So this diagram witnesses that
\Im
is the inverse morphism.

As before, we write
\epsilon\colon S^{\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}
for the sign involution,
\epsilon(x)=-x
.
Precomposition with
\epsilon
is an involution

\epsilon^{*}\colon\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
.
Under the Cayley transform (
2.7
), the sign involution

\epsilon
becomes complex conjugation on
U(1)
.
So the unitary automorphisms
\beta(V)(L)(x)\in{\mathbf{U}}(V)
and
\beta(V)(L)(\epsilon(x))

have the same eigenspace
L
, but inverse eigenvalues.
Hence
\beta(V)(L)(x)
and
\beta(V)(L)(\epsilon(x))
are inverse to each other.
This shows that the following diagram of morphisms of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids commutes:

(B.31)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 13.60167pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.88885pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{Gr}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.47806pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.60167pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-10.53441pt\raise-15.68056pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\beta}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.54984pt\raise-36.9464pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5853pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}\Im}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.60167pt\raise-31.36111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 37.60167pt\raise-31.36111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

Proposition B.32
.

Let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group, and let
A

be a finite
G
-CW-complex.
Then the map

[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}\colon[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}

is multiplication by
-1
.

Proof.

The commutative diagram (
B.30
) witnesses that
the involution of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids

\Im\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
is the inverse morphism; the same is thus true for

\Omega^{\sigma}\Im\colon\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
.
In particular, the map

[A,\Omega^{\sigma}\Im]^{\alpha}\colon[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}

is multiplication by
-1
.
The commutative diagram (
B.31
)
shows that the composite

[A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{[A,\beta]^{\alpha}}\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}}\ [A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}

equals
[A,(\Omega^{\sigma}\Im)]^{\alpha}\circ[A,\beta]^{\alpha}=-[A,\beta]^{\alpha}
.
So
[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}
is multiplication by
-1

on the image of
[A,\beta]^{\alpha}
.
The homomorphism

[A,i]^{\alpha}\ :\ [A,\mathbf{Gr}]^{\alpha}\ \longrightarrow\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}

is a group completion of abelian monoids by Proposition
B.5
.
In particular, the group
[A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}
is generated by the image of
[A,\beta]^{\alpha}
.
The
C
-global equivalence
\gamma\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

from (
B.26
) satisfies
\gamma\circ i=\beta\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
,
see (
B.28
).
So the group

[A,\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}]^{\alpha}
is generated by the image of

[A,\beta]^{\alpha}
. Since
[A,\epsilon^{*}]^{\alpha}
is a group homomorphism
and multiplication by
-1
on a generating set,
it is multiplication by
-1
on all elements.
∎

Remark B.33
.

Something stronger than Proposition
B.32
is actually true:
the morphism
\epsilon^{*}\colon\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

equals
\Omega^{\sigma}\Im
in the homotopy category of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids,
and thus
\epsilon^{*}=-\Id_{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}}
.
Here is a sketch of the proof.
An ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
R
is
group-like
if for every augmented Lie
group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
, the abelian monoid
\pi_{0}^{\alpha}(R)
is a group.
One can adapt the proof of
[
24
, Theorem 2.5.33]

to the Real-global context and show that the morphism
i\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{BUP}}

is a Real-global group completion, i.e., initial in the homotopy category
of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids among morphisms from
\mathbf{Gr}
to a group-like object.
The Real-global equivalence
\gamma\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
satisfies

\gamma\circ i=\beta
, so the morphism
\beta\colon\mathbf{Gr}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

is also a Real-global group completion.
Since
\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}
is group-like and

\epsilon^{*}\circ\beta=(\Omega^{\sigma}\Im)\circ\beta

by (
B.31
),
the universal property of a
Real-global group completion shows that
\epsilon^{*}=\Omega^{\sigma}\Im
.

B.2.
Connective Real-global K-theory

The connective global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{ku}}
is defined in

[
24
, Construction 3.6.9]
,
adapting a configuration space model of Segal
[
30
]

to the global equivariant context.
In this subsection we recall the definition,
along with the involution
\psi
by complex conjugation,
which enhances it to the connective Real-global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}=({\mathbf{ku}},\psi)
.
In (
B.36
) we recall the eigenspace morphism

\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})
,
which we show to be a
C
-global H-map in Proposition
B.40
(i).

Construction B.34

(The connective Real-global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}
)
.

We let
{\mathcal{U}}
be a hermitian inner product space of countable dimension (finite or infinite).
We recall the
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}})
of ‘orthogonal subspaces in
{\mathcal{U}}
’.
For a finite based set
A
we let
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},A)
be the space of tuples

(E_{a})_{a\in A\setminus\{0\}}
, indexed by the non-basepoint elements of
A
,
of finite-dimensional, pairwise orthogonal
{\mathbb{C}}
-subspaces of
{\mathcal{U}}
.
The topology on
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},A)

is that of a disjoint union of subspaces of a product of Grassmannians.
The basepoint of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},A)
is the tuple where
E_{a}=\{0\}

for all
a\in A\setminus\{0\}
.
For a based map
\alpha\colon A\longrightarrow B
the induced map

{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},\alpha)\colon{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},A)\longrightarrow{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},B)

sends
(E_{a})
to
(F_{b})
where

F_{b}\ =\ {\bigoplus}_{\alpha(a)=b}\,E_{a}\ .

Every
\Gamma
-space can be evaluated on a based space
by a coend construction, see for example
[
24
, (4.5.14)]
.
Categorically speaking, this coend realizes the enriched Kan extension along
the inclusion of
\Gamma
into the category of based spaces.
We write
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},K)={\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}})(K)
for the value of the
\Gamma
-space

{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}})
on a based space
K
.
Elements of
{\mathscr{C}}({\mathcal{U}},K)
can be interpreted as ‘labeled configurations’:
a point is represented by an unordered tuple

[E_{1},\dots,E_{n};\,k_{1},\dots,k_{n}]

where
(E_{1},\dots,E_{n})
is an
n
-tuple of finite-dimensional,
pairwise orthogonal subspaces of
{\mathcal{U}}
,
and
k_{1},\dots,k_{n}
are points of
K
, for some
n
.
The topology is such that,
informally speaking, the labels sum up whenever two points collide,
and a label disappears whenever a point approaches the basepoint of
K
.

The value of the orthogonal spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}
on a euclidean inner product space
V
is

{\mathbf{kr}}(V)\ =\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V})\ ,

the value of the
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}))

on the sphere
S^{V}
;
the inner product on the symmetric algebra
is described in
[
24
, Proposition 6.3.8]
.
The action of the orthogonal group
O(V)
on
V
then extends
to a unitary action on
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
.
We let
O(V)
act diagonally,
via the action on the sphere
S^{V}
and the action
on the
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}))
. For the structure maps we refer to

[
24
, Construction 6.3.9]
.
The involution
\psi(V)\colon{\mathbf{kr}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{kr}}(V)

is induced by complex conjugation on
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
,
which preserves orthogonality of subspaces.

Construction B.35

(The eigenspace morphism)
.

We recall from
[
24
, (6.3.26)]
the
eigenspace morphism

(B.36)

\eig\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})\ .

Its value at an inner product space
V

\displaystyle\eig(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}(V)=U(V_{\mathbb{C}})\ \longrightarrow\ \map_{*}(S^{V},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}(\Sym((V\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V\oplus\sigma}))

\displaystyle=\map_{*}(S^{V},{\mathbf{kr}}(V\oplus\sigma))=\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})(V)

is defined as follows.
For a unitary endomorphism
A\in U(V_{\mathbb{C}})
,
we let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in U(1)\setminus\{1\}
be the eigenvalues
different from
1
, and we let
E(\lambda_{j})
be the eigenspace of
A

for the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
.
We let

(B.37)

\mathfrak{c}^{-1}\ :\ U(1)\ \xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\ S^{\sigma}\ ,\quad\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda)=i\cdot(\lambda+1)(\lambda-1)^{-1}

be the inverse of the Cayley transform (
2.7
).
Then
\eig(V)
is defined by

\eig(V)(A)(v)\ =\ [E(\lambda_{1}),\dots,E(\lambda_{n});\,(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{1})),\dots,(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{n}))]\ .

In other words,
\eig(V)(A)(v)
is the configuration
of the points
(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{i}))\in S^{V\oplus\sigma}

labeled by the eigenspace
E(\lambda_{i})
of
A
, whence the name.
Strictly speaking,
E(\lambda_{i})
is a subspace of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
,
which we embed into the linear summand of
\Sym((V\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}})
.

If
A\in{\mathbf{U}}(V)
has eigenspaces
E_{1},\dots,E_{n}
for eigenvalues

\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
, then the eigenspaces of
{{}^{\psi}A}
are

\psi(E_{1}),\dots,\psi(E_{n})
for eigenvalues

\bar{\lambda}_{1},\dots,\bar{\lambda}_{n}
.
The involution on
(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})(V)={\mathbf{kr}}(V\oplus\sigma)

is by complex conjugation on the labeling vector spaces,
and by sign on the
\sigma
-coordinate that stores the eigenvalues.
The inverse Cayley transform
\mathfrak{c}^{-1}\colon U(1)\longrightarrow S^{\sigma}

is
C
-equivariant in the sense that
\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\bar{\lambda})=-\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda)
.
So the map

\eig(V)\colon{\mathbf{U}}(V)\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})(V)

commutes with the involutions.
The upshot is that the eigenspace morphism (
B.36
)
is a morphism of orthogonal
C
-spaces.

We consider an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid
R
and an orthogonal
C
-spectrum
Y
.
On several occasions we will need to know that
the map
[A,f]^{\alpha}\colon[A,R]^{\alpha}\longrightarrow[A,\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)]^{\alpha}

induced by a certain morphism
of orthogonal
C
-spaces
f\colon R\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)
is additive.
To this end, we now introduce the notion of a ‘
C
-global H-map’.

Construction B.38
.

For every orthogonal
C
-spectrum
Y
, the stable structure
provides an abelian group structure on the object
\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)

in the
C
-global homotopy category, as follows.
The canonical morphism
\kappa\colon Y\vee Y\longrightarrow Y\times Y

from the wedge to the product is a
C
-global equivalence of orthogonal
C
-spectra,
so it induces a
C
-global equivalence of orthogonal
C
-spaces

\Omega^{\bullet}(\kappa)\ :\ \Omega^{\bullet}(Y\vee Y)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \Omega^{\bullet}(Y\times Y)\cong\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)\times\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)\ .

This morphism becomes an isomorphism in the based
C
-global homotopy category,
where we can thus form the composite

\oplus\ :\ \Omega^{\bullet}(Y)\times\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)\ \xrightarrow[\cong]{\Omega^{\bullet}(\kappa)^{-1}}\ \Omega^{\bullet}(Y\vee Y)\xrightarrow{\Omega^{\bullet}(\nabla)}\ \Omega^{\bullet}(Y)\ .

Here
\nabla\colon Y\vee Y\longrightarrow Y
is the fold morphism.
The morphism
\oplus
makes
\Omega^{\bullet}(Y)
into an abelian group
object in the
C
-global homotopy category;
and for every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
,
its effect on
\pi_{0}^{\alpha}(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)=\pi_{0}^{G}(\alpha^{*}Y)

is the addition on stable homotopy groups.

Definition B.39
.

Let
R
be an ultra-commutative
C
-monoid, and let
Y
be an orthogonal
C
-spectrum.
A morphism
f\colon R\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}Y
of based orthogonal
C
-spaces
is a
C
-global H-map
if the following diagram commutes in the
based unstable
C
-global homotopy category:

\textstyle{R\boxtimes R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(f\rho_{1},f\rho_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{R}}
\textstyle{(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)\times(\Omega^{\bullet}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\oplus}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}Y}

Here
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\colon R\boxtimes R\longrightarrow R
denote the projections to the two factors.

The ultra-commutative multiplication on
{\mathbf{U}}
induces a

C
-ring spectrum structure on the suspension spectrum
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
.
This, in turn, induces a product structure on
the equivariant homotopy groups of
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}
.
The morphism
\varrho\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow{\mathbb{S}}

arising from the unique morphism of orthogonal spaces
{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\ast

is a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-ring spectra, and thus induces a
morphism of equivariant homotopy rings

\varrho_{*}\ :\ \pi_{\star}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{\star}^{G}({\mathbb{S}})

for every augmented Lie group.
The morphism
{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\ast
has a section given by the multiplicative unit of
{\mathbf{U}}
,
so
\varrho_{*}
is surjective.
The
augmentation ideal
of
\pi_{\star}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})

is the kernel of this homomorphism
\varrho_{*}
.
The morphism
q\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}

is induced by the based morphism
{\mathbf{U}}_{+}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{U}}
that maps the external basepoint
of the source with the multiplicative unit in the target.

Proposition B.40
.

(i)

The morphism
\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})
is a
C
-global H-map.

(ii)

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
, the composite

\pi_{\star}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ \xrightarrow{\ q_{*}\ }\ \pi_{\star}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\ \xrightarrow{\eig^{\flat}_{*}}\ \pi_{\star}^{G}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})

annihilates the square of the augmentation ideal.

Proof.

For use in both parts we introduce an orthogonal
C
-spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}

that is Real-globally equivalent to a product of two copies of
{\mathbf{kr}}
.
The value of the orthogonal spectrum
{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}
on a euclidean inner product space
V
is
the configuration space

{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}(V)\ =\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V}\vee S^{V})\ ,

the value of the
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}))

on the wedge of two copies of
S^{V}
.
The action of
O(V)
, the structure maps of
{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}

and the involution are defined in much the same way as for
{\mathbf{kr}}
.
The projection and fold maps

p_{1},\,p_{2},\,\nabla\ :\ S^{V}\vee S^{V}\ \longrightarrow\ S^{V}

induce continuous, based and
(C\times O(V))
-equivariant maps of configuration spaces

p_{1}(V),\,p_{2}(V),\,\nabla(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}(V)\ =\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V}\vee S^{V})\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V})\ =\ {\mathbf{kr}}(V)\ .

For varying inner product spaces
V
,
these maps assemble into morphisms of orthogonal
C
-spectra

p_{1},\,p_{2},\,\nabla\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{kr}}\ .

We claim that the morphism

(p_{1},p_{2})\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{kr}}\times{\mathbf{kr}}

is a
C
-global equivalence.
To see that, we let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be an augmented Lie group.
We call an orthogonal
G
-representation
V

ample
if the complex symmetric algebra

\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
is a complete Real
(G,\alpha)
-universe. If
V
is ample, then
the
G
-
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha^{\flat}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})),-)
is special
by
[
24
, Theorem 6.3.19 (i)]
,
and it is
G
-cofibrant by
[
24
, Example 6.3.16]
.
So the
G
-
\Gamma
-space
{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha^{\flat}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})),S^{1}\wedge-)
is very special
and cofibrant, and thus takes wedges of finite based
G
-CW-complexes to products,
up to
G
-weak equivalence, by
[
24
, Theorem B.61 (i)]
.
In particular, if
V
is ample and
V^{G}\neq 0
, then the map

\displaystyle(p_{1}(V),p_{2}(V))\ :\ \alpha^{\flat}({\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}(V))={\mathscr{C}}

\displaystyle(\alpha^{\flat}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})),S^{V}\vee S^{V})\ \longrightarrow

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha^{\flat}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})),S^{V})\times{\mathscr{C}}(\alpha^{\flat}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})),S^{V})=\alpha^{\flat}({\mathbf{kr}}(V)\times{\mathbf{kr}}(V))

is a
G
-weak equivalence.
The ample
G
-representations with nonzero
G
-fixed points
are cofinal in all orthogonal
G
-representations, so this proves
the claim that the morphism
(p_{1},p_{2})
is a
C
-global equivalence.

(i) We define a morphism of orthogonal
C
-spaces

\eig_{[2]}\colon{\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]})
,
a variation of the eigenspace morphism (
B.36
),
but with two (instead of one) unitary parameters.
Its value at an inner product space
V
is the map

\displaystyle\eig_{[2]}(V)\ :\ ({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})(V)\ \longrightarrow\ \map_{*}(S^{V},

\displaystyle{\mathscr{C}}(\Sym((V\oplus\sigma)_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V\oplus\sigma}\vee S^{V\oplus\sigma}))

\displaystyle=\map_{*}(S^{V},{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}(V\oplus\sigma))=\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]})(V)

defined as follows.
Elements of
({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})(V)
are pairs
(A,B)

of unitary endomorphisms
A,B\in U(V)
that are
transverse

in the following sense:
there exists an orthogonal direct sum decomposition

V=V^{\prime}\oplus V^{\prime\prime}
such that
A
is the identity on
V^{\prime}
, and
B
is the identity on
V^{\prime\prime}
.
The transversality hypothesis in particular means that
A
and
B
commute
(but it is stronger than that),
so
A
and
B
are simultaneously diagonalizable.
We let
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\in U(1)\setminus\{1\}
be the set eigenvalues
of
A
and
B
different from
1
,
we let
E(\lambda_{j})
be the eigenspace of
A
for the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
,
and we let
F(\lambda_{j})
be the eigenspace of
B
for the eigenvalue
\lambda_{j}
.
By the transversality hypothesis, all these eigenspaces
E(\lambda_{i})
and
F(\lambda_{j})

are pairwise orthogonal.
We can then define the map
\eig_{[2]}(V)
by

\eig_{[2]}(V)(A,B)(v)\ =\ \{E(\lambda_{j}),i_{1}(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{j})\}_{1\leq j\leq n}\ \cup\ \{F(\lambda_{j}),i_{2}(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{j})\}_{1\leq j\leq n}\ .

As before,
\mathfrak{c}^{-1}
is the inverse Cayley transform (
B.37
).
And
i_{1},i_{2}\colon S^{V\oplus\sigma}\longrightarrow S^{V\oplus\sigma}\vee S^{V\oplus\sigma}

denote the embeddings of the two wedge summands.
In other words, the eigenspace
E(\lambda_{j})
of
A
is attached to
the point
(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{j}))
in the first wedge summand,
and the eigenspace
F(\lambda_{j})
of
B
is attached to
the point
(v,\mathfrak{c}^{-1}(\lambda_{j}))
in the second wedge summand.

The following diagram of orthogonal
C
-spaces then commutes:

(B.41)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 53.66017pt\raise-72.64874pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.32541pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eig}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern
