0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces

The morphism
(p_{1},p_{2})\colon{\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{kr}}\times{\mathbf{kr}}
is a Real-global equivalence.
The functors
\sh^{\sigma}
and
\Omega^{\bullet}
preserve Real-global equivalences and products,
so the upwards middle vertical morphism is a Real-global equivalence of orthogonal
C
-spaces.
Hence the morphisms decorated with a tilde are Real-global equivalences.
After passing to the Real-global homotopy category, we can invert the
Real-global equivalences, and the resulting diagram witnesses
that
\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})
is a
C
-global H-map.

(ii) In the commutative diagram (
B.41
),
we pass to adjoints for the adjunction
(\Sigma^{\infty},\Omega^{\bullet})
.
This yields the commutativity in the
C
-global stable homotopy category
of the right part of the following diagram:

\textstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\times(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q\times q}
\textstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\times(\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eig^{\flat}\times\eig^{\flat}}
\textstyle{(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})\times(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}})}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\rho_{1},\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\rho_{2})}
\scriptstyle{q_{[2]}}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\mu}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}\mu}
\scriptstyle{\eig^{\flat}_{[2]}}
\scriptstyle{(\Sigma^{\infty}\rho_{1},\Sigma^{\infty}\rho_{2})}
\textstyle{\sh^{\sigma}({\mathbf{kr}}^{[2]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sh^{\sigma}\nabla}
\scriptstyle{(\sh^{\sigma}p_{1},\sh^{\sigma}p_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{\infty}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eig^{\flat}}
\textstyle{\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}}

Given representation-graded homotopy classes
x\in\pi_{V}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
and

y\in\pi_{W}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})
, we write
x\boxtimes y
for the image of
(x,y)
under
the composite

\displaystyle\pi_{V}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\times\pi_{W}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ \longrightarrow\ \pi_{V\oplus W}^{G}((\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\wedge(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}))\ \cong\ \pi_{V\oplus W}^{G}(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}}))\ ,

where the first map is the exterior pairing
[
24
, (3.5.13)]
,
and the isomorphism is induced by the strong symmetric monoidal structure on the
unreduced suspension spectrum functor, see
[
24
, (4.1.17)]
.
Then

(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\mu)_{*}(x\boxtimes y)\ =\ x\cdot y\ .

The morphism
\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\rho_{1}\colon\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})\longrightarrow\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}

factors as the composite

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}({\mathbf{U}}\boxtimes{\mathbf{U}})\ \cong(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\wedge(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\ \xrightarrow{\Id\wedge\varrho}\ (\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}})\wedge{\mathbb{S}}\ \cong\ \Sigma^{\infty}_{+}{\mathbf{U}}\ ,

where the final isomorphism is the unit isomorphism of the smash product.
So if
y
belongs to the augmentation ideal, then

(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\rho_{1})_{*}(x\boxtimes y)\ =\ x\cdot\varrho_{*}(y)\ =\ 0\ ,

and hence

\displaystyle(\sh^{\sigma}p_{1})_{*}((\eig^{\flat}_{[2]}\circ q_{[2]})_{*}(x\boxtimes y))\

\displaystyle=\ (\eig^{\flat}\circ q\circ(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\rho_{1}))_{*}(x\boxtimes y)\ =\ 0\ .

Similarly,
(\sh^{\sigma}p_{2})_{*}((\eig^{\flat}_{[2]}\circ q^{[2]})_{*}(x\boxtimes y))=0

whenever
x
belongs to the augmentation ideal.

The morphism
(\sh^{\sigma}p_{1},\sh^{\sigma}p_{2})
is a
C
-global equivalence,
so the induced map on equivariant homotopy groups is an isomorphism.
So if both
x
and
y
lie in the augmentation ideal, we conclude that

(\eig^{\flat}_{[2]}\circ q_{[2]})_{*}\allowbreak(x\boxtimes y)=0
.
The commutativity of the above diagram then yields the desired relation:

(\eig^{\flat}\circ q)_{*}(x\cdot y)\ =\ (\eig^{\flat}\circ q\circ(\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}\mu))_{*}(x\boxtimes y)\ =\ \ ((\sh^{\sigma}\nabla)\circ\eig^{\flat}_{[2]}\circ q_{[2]})_{*}(x\boxtimes y)\ =\ 0\ .\qed

B.3.
Periodic Real-global K-theory

The global K-theory spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}

was introduced by Joachim
[
15
, Definition 4.3]

as a commutative orthogonal ring spectrum,
see also
[
24
, Construction 6.4.9]
.
Joachim showed in
[
15
, Theorem 4.4]

that the genuine
G
-spectrum underlying the global spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}
represents

G
-equivariant complex K-theory. A different proof can be found in

[
24
, Corollary 6.4.23]
.
Joachim’s orthogonal ring spectrum can be enhanced to an orthogonal
C
-spectrum
{\mathbf{KR}}

by suitably incorporating complex conjugation; to my knowledge,
the additional involution was first discussed in the literature
by Halladay and Kamel
[
12
, Section 6]
, who use the notation
KU_{\mathbb{R}}

for the resulting orthogonal
C
-ring spectrum.
In
[
12
, Proposition 6.2]
,
Halladay and Kamel identify the genuine
C
-fixed point spectrum of
{\mathbf{KR}}

with
KO
, thereby verifying that
KU_{\mathbb{R}}
is a model for Atiyah’s Real K-theory spectrum.

We recall the definition of the orthogonal
C
-spectrum
{\mathbf{KR}}

in Construction
B.43
, and we refer to it as
the
Real-global K-theory spectrum
.
Theorem
B.52
justifies the name:
for every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
,
the orthogonal
G
-spectrum
\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}})
represents

\alpha
-equivariant Real K-theory
KR_{\alpha}
.
Our treatment proceeds from a self-contained proof
in Theorem
B.46
that the pre-Euler class
of the
C
-sign representation is nilpotent in
\pi_{\star}^{C}({\mathbf{KR}})
,
which immediately implies that
the geometric fixed point homotopy groups
\Phi^{G}_{*}(\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}}))

vanish for all
surjective
augmentations
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
.
This implies that the Real-global homotopy type of

{\mathbf{KR}}
is coinduced (or ‘relative
C
-Borel’)
from the underlying global spectrum.
Theorem
B.49
shows that (and how) the Real-global space

{\mathbf{U}}
is the Real-global infinite loop space underlying
{\mathbf{KR}}\wedge S^{\sigma}
.
Consequently, the Real-global space
{\mathbf{BUP}}\sim\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}

‘is’ the Real-global infinite loop space underlying
{\mathbf{KR}}
,
see Theorem
B.52
(i).

Construction B.42

(Clifford algebras)
.

We let
V
be a euclidean inner product space. We define the complex
Clifford algebra
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
by

{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\ =\ (TV)_{\mathbb{C}}/(v\otimes v-|v|^{2}\cdot 1)\ ,

the quotient of the complexified tensor algebra of
V

by the ideal generated by the elements
v\otimes v-|v|^{2}\cdot 1

for all
v\in V
. We write
[-]\colon V\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
for the

{\mathbb{R}}
-linear and injective composite

V\ \xrightarrow{\text{linear summand}}\ TV\ \xrightarrow{1\otimes-}\ (TV)_{\mathbb{C}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\ .

With this notation the relation
[v]^{2}=|v|^{2}\cdot 1
holds in
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)

for all
v\in V
.
The Clifford algebra construction is functorial for
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear isometric embeddings,
so in particular
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
inherits an action of the orthogonal group
O(V)
.
The Clifford algebra is
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded, coming from the grading of
the tensor algebra by even and odd tensor powers.

The complex Clifford algebra is in fact a
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
C^{\ast}
-algebra.
The
\ast
-involution on
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
is defined by declaring
[v]^{\ast}=[v]

for all
v\in V
and extending this to a
{\mathbb{C}}
-semilinear anti-automorphism.
This makes the elements
[v]
for
v\in S(V)
into unitary elements of
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
.
The norm on
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
arises from the operator norm on the endomorphism
algebra of the exterior algebra of
V_{\mathbb{C}}
, as explained, for example,
in
[
24
, Construction 6.4.5]
.

The complex Clifford algebra
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)

supports three different involutions relevant for our purposes:

•

The grading involution
\alpha\colon{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
;
it is
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear and multiplicative, and satisfies
\alpha[v]=-[v]
.
The
+1
and
-1
eigenspaces of
\alpha
are, respectively,
the even and odd summands of
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
.

•

The conjugation involution
(-)^{*}\colon{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
;
it is conjugate-linear and anti-multiplicative, and satisfies
[v]^{*}=[v]
.

•

We define
\psi\colon{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
as the unique
conjugate-linear and multiplicative map satisfying
\psi[v]=[v]
.
The fixed points of this involution are thus the real Clifford algebra
associated with the positive definite form, i.e.,

{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)^{\psi}\ =\ (TV)/(v\otimes v-|v|^{2}\cdot 1)\ .

The three involutions
\alpha
,
(-)^{*}
and
\psi
of
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)

commute with each other.
The pair
((-)^{*},\alpha)
makes
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
into a complex,
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded
C^{*}
-algebra,
and only these data enter into the definition of
{\mathbf{KU}}
as an orthogonal spectrum.
The conjugate-linear involution
\psi
of
{\mathbb{C}}(V)
will enter in the definition
of the involution of
{\mathbf{KR}}
which makes it an orthogonal
C
-ring spectrum.

Construction B.43

(The Real-global K-theory spectrum)
.

For a euclidean inner product space
V
,
we write
{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
for the Hilbert space completion of
the complexified symmetric algebra
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
,
with respect to the inner product specified in
[
24
, Proposition 6.3.8]
.
Then
{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
denotes the
C^{*}
-algebra of compact operators on
{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
,
concentrated in even grading.
The value of the commutative orthogonal ring spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}

at
V
is defined by

{\mathbf{KU}}(V)\ =\ C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ .

The unit map of the ring spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}

uses the ‘functional calculus’

(B.44)

\fc\ :\ S^{V}\ \longrightarrow\ C^{\ast}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V))\ ,\quad v\ \longmapsto\ (-)[v]\ ,

For
v\in V
the
\ast
-homomorphism
\fc(v)
is given on homogeneous elements
of
s
by

f[v]\ =\ \fc(v)(f)\ =\ \begin{cases}\ f(|v|)\cdot 1&\text{ when $f$ is even, and}\\
\ \frac{f(|v|)}{|v|}\cdot[v]&\text{ when $f$ is odd.}\end{cases}

For
v=0
the formula for odd functions is to be interpreted as
f[0]=0
;
this is continuous because for
v\neq 0
,
the norm of
f(|v|)/|v|\cdot[v]
is
f(|v|)
, which tends to
f(0)=0

if
v
tends to 0. If the norm of
v
tends to infinity, then
f(|v|)
tends to 0, so

\fc(v)
tends to the constant
\ast
-homomorphism
with value 0, the basepoint of
C^{\ast}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V))
.
The unit map

(B.45)

\eta_{V}\ :\ S^{V}\ \longrightarrow\ C^{\ast}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ =\ {\mathbf{KU}}(V)

is then defined as the composite

\ S^{V}\ \xrightarrow{\ \fc\ }\ C^{\ast}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V))\ \xrightarrow{(-\otimes p_{0})_{*}}\ C^{\ast}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ =\ {\mathbf{KU}}(V)\ ,

where
p_{0}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
is the orthogonal projection onto the constant summand
in the symmetric algebra. The multiplicativity of the unit maps follows from
the multiplicativity property
[
24
, (6.4.8)]

of the functional calculus maps.

Now we explain the involution on
{\mathbf{KU}}(V)
,
see also
[
12
, Definition 6.2]
.
Complex conjugation is an conjugate-linear isometry

\psi\colon\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})\longrightarrow\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
;
so it extends uniquely to a continuous conjugate-linear isometry

\psi\colon{\mathcal{H}}_{V}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
of the Hilbert space completion.
Conjugation by
\psi
is then a conjugate-linear involutive automorphism

(-)^{\psi}\ :\ {\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathcal{K}}_{V}

of the
C^{*}
-algebra of compact operators. For example,
if
E
is a finite-dimensional
{\mathbb{C}}
-subspace of
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})
,
and
p_{E}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
the associated orthogonal projection, then

(p_{E})^{\psi}\ =\ \psi\circ p_{E}\circ\psi\ =\ p_{\psi(E)}\ .

We combine the conjugate-linear and multiplicative
involutions
\psi\colon{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)
and

(-)^{\psi}\colon{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\longrightarrow{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
into one
conjugate-linear and multiplicative involution

\psi\otimes(-)^{\psi}\ :\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ .

Finally, we define an involution

\psi\ :\ {\mathbf{KU}}(V)\ =\ C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ \longrightarrow\ C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ =\ {\mathbf{KU}}(V)

by sending a homomorphism
h\colon s\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
to the composite

s\ \xrightarrow{\ (-)^{*}\ }\ s\ \xrightarrow{\ h\ }\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ \xrightarrow{\psi\otimes(-)^{\psi}}\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ .

The first map is conjugation on
s
, i.e., pointwise complex conjugation
of functions.
If
h
is a
\ast
-homomorphism, then in particular
h\circ(-)^{*}=(-)^{*}\circ h
.
So the involution on
{\mathbf{KU}}(V)
is also given by postcomposition of

h\colon s\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}
with the map

{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ \xrightarrow{(-)^{*}}\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ \xrightarrow{\psi\otimes(-)^{\psi}}\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V}\ .

This composite is
{\mathbb{C}}
-linear and anti-multiplicative.
We omit the verification that these involutions are compatible
with the multiplication and the structure maps of the orthogonal spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}
.
We write
{\mathbf{KR}}=({\mathbf{KU}},\psi)
for the
periodic Real-global
K
-theory spectrum
,
i.e., the commutative orthogonal
C
-ring spectra
{\mathbf{KU}}
with
C
-action by this involution.

We write
a=a_{\sigma}\in\pi_{-\sigma}^{C}({\mathbb{S}})
for the pre-Euler class (
0.1
)
of the sign representation of the group
C
,
and also for its image in
\pi_{-\sigma}^{C}({\mathbf{KR}})
under the unit morphism
{\mathbb{S}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}
.
In the
C
-spectrum that represents Atiyah’s Real K-theory,
the third power of
a
vanishes.
Our proof that the orthogonal
C
-spectrum
{\mathbf{KR}}
models the ‘correct’ Real-global homotopy type
proceeds by proving this property for
{\mathbf{KR}}
from scratch.

Theorem B.46
.

The relation
a^{3}=0
holds in
\pi_{-3\sigma}^{C}({\mathbf{KR}})
.

Proof.

For every
n\geq 0
, the class
a^{n}
is represented by the composite

S^{0}\ \xrightarrow{\ a^{n}\ }\ S^{n\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\eta_{n\sigma}}\ {\mathbf{KR}}(n\sigma)\ .

By definition (
B.45
) of the unit maps of
{\mathbf{KR}}
, that composite
factors through
\fc\circ a^{n}\colon S^{0}\longrightarrow C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma))
.
So it suffices to show that for
n=3
,
the
C
-fixed point
\fc(0)
lies in the same path component of

\left(C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(3\sigma))\right)^{C}

as the basepoint, the zero homomorphism.
We will show that this fixed point space is homeomorphic to a circle,
and so in particular path-connected.

For any
n
, the
C
-action on the representation
n\sigma
is by multiplication by
-1
.
Hence the induced involution on the Clifford algebra

{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(-\Id)\ :\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)

is a
{\mathbb{C}}
-algebra automorphism that negates the element
[v]

for all
v\in S(n\sigma)
.
So for the
C
-representation
n\sigma
, the involution
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(-\Id)

induced by functoriality of
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(-)
coincides with the grading involution
\alpha
.
The involution on
C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma))
relevant for our
present purpose is thus given by

•

conjugating a graded
\ast
-homomorphism
f\colon s\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)

by the complex conjugations
(-)^{*}\colon s\longrightarrow s
(pointwise complex conjugation)
and
\psi\colon{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)\longrightarrow{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)
, and

•

applying the involution
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(-\Id_{n\sigma})=\alpha
coming from
functoriality of
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(-)
.

In other words, we need to identify the fixed points of the involution

C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma))\ \longrightarrow\ C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma))\ ,\quad f\ \longmapsto\ \alpha\circ\psi\circ f\circ(-)^{*}\ .

For every
{\mathbb{Z}}/2
-graded complex
C^{*}
-algebra
A
,
evaluation at the function

r\ :\ {\mathbb{R}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbb{C}}\ ,\quad r(x)\ =\ \frac{2i}{x-i}

in the
C^{*}
-algebra
s
is a homeomorphism

C^{*}_{\gr}(s,A)\ \cong\ \{x\in A\colon xx^{*}=x^{*}x=-x-x^{*}\ ,\ \alpha(x)=x^{*}\}\ ,

compare
[
24
, (6.4.3)]
.
For
A={\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)
, the relevant involution
on the left hand side corresponds to the involution on the right hand
side sending
x\in{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n\sigma)
to
\psi(\alpha(x^{*}))=(\psi(\alpha(x)))^{*}
.
The previous map thus restricts to a homeomorphism

\displaystyle\left(C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n))\right)^{C}\

\displaystyle\cong\ \{x\in{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n)A\colon xx^{*}=x^{*}x=-x-x^{*}\ ,\ \alpha(x^{*})=x=\psi(x)\}

\displaystyle\cong\ \{x\in C(n)\colon xx^{*}=x^{*}x=-x-x^{*}\ ,\ \alpha(x^{*})=x\}\ .

The second homeomorphism uses that
{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(n)

is the complexification of the real Clifford algebra

C(n)\ =\ T({\mathbb{R}}^{n})/([v]\otimes[v]-|v|^{2}\cdot 1)\ ,

with
\psi
corresponding to the complex conjugation automorphism
of
{\mathbb{C}}\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}C(n)
.

Now we specialize to
n=3
.
We write
e=[1,0,0]
,
f=[0,1,0]
and
g=[0,0,1]
for the
multiplicative generators of
C(3)
coming from the standard
orthonormal basis of
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
. Then
e
,
f
and
g
are odd,
pairwise anticommuting, fixed by the conjugation
(-)^{*}
, and satisfy

e^{2}\ =\ f^{2}\ =\ g^{2}\ =\ 1\ .

We want to find all
x\in C(3)
that satisfy

x\cdot x^{*}\ =\ x^{*}\cdot x\ =\ -x-x^{*}\text{\quad and\quad}\alpha(x^{*})\ =\ x^{*}\ .

The involution
x\mapsto\alpha(x^{*})=\alpha(x)^{*}
fixes the basis elements

1
and
efg
, and it negates
e
,
f
,
g
,
ef
,
eg
, and
fg
.
The condition
\alpha(x^{*})=x
thus forces
x
to be an
{\mathbb{R}}
-linear
combination of
1
and
efg
. So
x=y+z\cdot efg
for some
y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The relations
x\cdot x^{*}=x^{*}\cdot x=-x-x^{*}
then amount to

(y+1)^{2}+z^{2}\ =\ 1\ .

The solutions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to this equation form a circle, as claimed.
∎

We write
\tilde{E}C=(EC)^{\diamond}
for the unreduced suspension
of the universal free
C
-space
EC
.

Theorem B.47
.

Let
M
be an orthogonal
C
-spectrum that admits
a
{\mathbf{KR}}
-module structure in the Real-global stable homotopy category.

(i)

The Real-global spectrum
M\wedge\tilde{E}C
is trivial.

(ii)

The orthogonal
C
-space
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)
is coinduced.

Proof.

(i) We let
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
be any augmented Lie group.
If the augmentation
\alpha
is trivial, then
G
acts trivially on

\alpha^{*}(\tilde{E}C)
, which is thus
G
-equivariantly contractible.
In particular, the geometric fixed point homotopy groups
\Phi^{G}_{*}(M\wedge\tilde{E}C)

vanish.

If the augmentation
\alpha
is surjective, then we need to argue differently.
Because
a^{3}=0
, also
\alpha^{*}(a)^{3}=0
in
\pi_{-3\alpha^{*}(\sigma)}^{G}(\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}}))
.
Because
(\alpha^{*}(\sigma))^{G}=\{0\}
, we have
So
\Phi^{G}(\alpha^{*}(a))=1
in the geometric fixed point ring
\Phi^{G}_{0}(\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}}))
.
So
1=\Phi^{G}(\alpha^{*}(a))^{3}=\Phi^{G}(\alpha^{*}(a^{3}))=0
, and so
the ring
\Phi^{G}_{0}(\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}}))
is trivial.
Since
M
is a
{\mathbf{KR}}
-module spectrum, the group

\Phi^{G}_{k}(\alpha^{*}(M))
admits a module structure over
\Phi^{G}_{0}(\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}}))=0
,
and thus
\Phi^{G}_{k}(\alpha^{*}(M))=0
for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
.
Since
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is surjective, we have
(\alpha^{*}(\tilde{E}C))^{G}=S^{0}
,
and thus

\Phi^{G}_{*}(\alpha^{*}(M\wedge\tilde{E}C))\ \cong\ \Phi^{G}_{*}(\alpha^{*}(M))\ =\ 0\ .

So all geometric fixed point homotopy groups of
M\wedge\tilde{E}C
vanish,
for all augmented Lie groups, and thus
M\wedge\tilde{E}C
is trivial in
the Real-global stable homotopy category.

(ii) Since
M
is a
{\mathbf{KR}}
-module, also the Real-global spectrum

\map(EC,M)
admits a
{\mathbf{KR}}
-module structure.
So the Real-global spectra
M\wedge\tilde{E}C
and

\map(EC,M)\wedge\tilde{E}C
are trivial by part (i).
Hence the two horizontal morphisms in the following commutative square
are Real-global equivalences:

\textstyle{M\wedge EC_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{M\wedge p_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}\wedge EC_{+}}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{\map(EC,M)\wedge EC_{+}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\map(EC,M)\wedge p_{+}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\map(EC,M)}

Since the morphism
p^{*}\colon M\longrightarrow\map(EC,M)
is global equivalence
of underlying non-Real global spectra, the left horizontal morphism
is also a Real-global equivalence, by the stable analog of Proposition
A.13
.
Hence the right vertical morphism

p^{*}\colon M\longrightarrow\map(EC,M)
is a Real-global equivalence.
The functor
\Omega^{\bullet}
takes Real-global equivalences
of orthogonal
C
-spectra to Real-global equivalences
of orthogonal
C
-spaces; and it commutes with
\map(EC,-)
. So the morphism

\Omega^{\bullet}(M)\ \xrightarrow{\Omega^{\bullet}(p^{*})}\Omega^{\bullet}(\map(EC,M))\cong\map(EC,\Omega^{\bullet}M)

is a Real-global equivalence.
Proposition
A.16
then shows that
\Omega^{\bullet}(M)

is coinduced.
∎

Construction B.48
.

We recall from
[
24
, Construction 6.4.13]

the construction of the morphism of commutative orthogonal
C
-ring spectra

j\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}\ \longrightarrow\ {\mathbf{KR}}

from connective to periodic Real-global K-theory.
Its value at a euclidean inner product space
V
is the map

j(V)\ :\ {\mathbf{kr}}(V)\ =\ {\mathscr{C}}(\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}}),S^{V})\ \longrightarrow\ C^{*}_{\gr}(s,{\mathbb{C}\text{l}}(V)\otimes_{\mathbb{C}}{\mathcal{K}}_{V})\ =\ {\mathbf{KR}}(V)

defined by

j(V)[E_{1},\dots,E_{n};\,v_{1},\dots,v_{n}](f)\ =\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}f[v_{i}]\otimes p_{E_{i}}\ .

Here
f[v_{i}]=\fc(v_{i})(f)
is the functional calculus map (
B.44
),
and
p_{E_{i}}
is the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
E_{i}
of
\Sym(V_{\mathbb{C}})\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{V}
.
Already in
[
24
, Construction 6.4.13]
we omitted the detailed
verification that these maps indeed form a morphism of orthogonal ring spectra.
We shall honor this tradition here, and also refrain from checking that
the map
j(V)
commutes with the complex-conjugation involutions.

The following theorem generalizes
[
24
, Theorem 6.4.21]

from the global to the Real-global context; it says that

{\mathbf{U}}
is a Real-global genuine infinite loop space,
with delooping the
\sigma
-suspension of
{\mathbf{KR}}
.

Theorem B.49
.

The morphism of based orthogonal
C
-spaces

\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig\ :\ {\mathbf{U}}\ \longrightarrow\ \Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

is a Real-global equivalence and a
C
-global H-map.

Proof.

The orthogonal
C
-space
{\mathbf{U}}
is coinduced by Theorem
B.22
.
The orthogonal
C
-space
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

is coinduced by Theorem
B.47
.
The morphism
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

is a global equivalence of underlying non-Real orthogonal spaces
by
[
24
, Theorem 6.4.21]
.
So
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig
is a Real-global equivalence
by Proposition
A.15
.

The eigenspace morphisms is a
C
-global H-map by Proposition
B.40
(i).
Since
\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)
arises from a morphism of orthogonal
C
-spectra,
also the composite

\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig
is a
C
-global H-map.
∎

Now we proceed to justify that
{\mathbf{KR}}
deserves its name:
for every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
,
the orthogonal
G
-spectrum
\alpha^{*}({\mathbf{KR}})
represents
\alpha
-equivariant
Real
K
-theory
KR_{\alpha}
.
If the augmentation
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C
is trivial, this amounts to
the fact that the underlying
G
-spectrum of
the global spectrum
{\mathbf{KU}}
represents
G
-equivariant
complex
K
-theory, see
[
15
, Theorem 4.4]
or

[
24
, Corollary 6.4.23]
.
For
G=C
augmented by the identity, this amounts to the fact
that the genuine
C
-spectrum underlying
{\mathbf{KR}}

is a model for Atiyah’s Real K-theory spectrum, see
[
12
, Proposition 6.2]
.

Construction B.50
.

\Theta\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})
be the unique morphism
in the unstable based
C
-global homotopy category that makes the following diagram
commute:

(B.51)

The Real-global equivalences
\gamma
and
\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{{\mathbf{KR}}}

are defined in (
B.26
)
and
[
24
, Proposition 3.1.25]
, respectively,

The lower left horizontal morphism is a Real-global equivalence by Theorem
B.49
.

Theorem B.52
.

(i)

The morphism
\Theta\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{KR}})
is a Real-global
equivalence and a
C
-global H-map.

(ii)

For every augmented Lie group
\alpha\colon G\longrightarrow C

and every finite
G
-CW-complex
A
, the composite

KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \cong_{\eqref{eq:BUP_to_KR_alpha}}\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{[A,\Theta]^{\alpha}}\ {\mathbf{KR}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)

is an isomorphism of rings.

Proof.

(i) Since all other morphisms in the defining commutative diagram (
B.51
)
for
\Theta
are Real-global equivalences, so is
\Theta
.
For the second claim we exploit that the morphism

\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}j)\circ\eig\colon{\mathbf{U}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{KR}})

is a
C
-global H-map, by Theorem
B.49
.
Hence also the lower horizontal composite in (
B.51
) is a
C
-global H-map.
Since
\gamma
is a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-monoids, the
counter clockwise composite in (
B.51
) is also a
C
-global H-map.
Since
\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{{\mathbf{KR}}})
arises
from a stable
C
-global morphism, it commutes with the ‘stable H-space structures’
from Construction
B.38
. So
\Theta
is a
C
-global H-map, as claimed.

(ii) Since
\Theta
is both a Real-global equivalence and a
C
-global H-map,
the induced map
[A,\Theta]^{\alpha}
is a group isomorphism.
The map (
B.10
) is a group isomorphism by
Theorem
B.12
(iii).
So it remains to show that the composite in the statement of the theorem is also
multiplicative, and hence an isomorphism of rings.
By design, the composite factors as

KR_{\alpha}(A)\ \cong_{\eqref{eq:BUP_to_KR_alpha}}\ [A,{\mathbf{BUP}}]^{\alpha}\ \xrightarrow{[A,\vartheta]^{\alpha}}\ {\mathbf{kr}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)\ \xrightarrow{\ j_{*}\ }\ {\mathbf{KR}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)\ ,

where
\vartheta\colon{\mathbf{BUP}}\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{kr}})
is the unique morphism
in the unstable based
C
-global homotopy category that makes the following diagram
commute:

\textstyle{{\mathbf{BUP}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{\vartheta}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}({\mathbf{kr}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\tilde{\lambda}^{\sigma}_{{\mathbf{kr}}})}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}{\mathbf{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}\eig}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\sigma}(\Omega^{\bullet}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\Omega^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\sigma}(\sh^{\sigma}{\mathbf{kr}}))}

Since
j\colon{\mathbf{kr}}\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}
is a morphism of ultra-commutative
C
-ring spectra,
the induced map
j_{*}\colon{\mathbf{kr}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{KR}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)
of equivariant cohomology
theories is multiplicative.
We claim that
[A,\vartheta]^{\alpha}\circ\eqref{eq:BUP_to_KR_alpha}\colon KR_{\alpha}(A)\longrightarrow{\mathbf{kr}}^{0}_{\alpha}(A)
is also multiplicative.
If the augmentation
\alpha
is trivial, this is shown in

[
24
, Theorem 6.3.31 (ii)]
.
The proof for surjective augmentations is analogous, and we omit it.
∎
